Stability follow-up (second Camera2 A/B, build 47300560, ASUS to S24 GATT, 2026-10-13). The viewer's decoder rebuild worked (`decoder_rebuild reason=size`, 66-112 ms, no errors), but the cold arm changed size four times in 40 s and each change cost about 1.7 s of "Recovering video".

- Why the target fell to 97 kbps. It was not an under-filled encoder. At the 495-544 kbps target the encoder produced 519-555 kbps, the sender queue grew 616, 1320, 2015, 2754, 3278, 3816 ms with the child reporting late, and the link carried only 152-212 kbit per window ("BLE GATT TX 262-323 kbps", about the 263 kbps two-PDU bound of a 15 ms interval); the cut to 0.85 x 152 - 32 = 97 kbps was the rate controller's usual response to a 3.8 s queue. The probe (1488 kbps) was a burst rate; the same pair in the warm arm carried 700-800 kbps on the same 15 ms interval, so the link capacity moved by a factor of three between adjacent runs for a reason the logs do not show. What the ladder did wrong was to turn that into size changes: it follows the target alone, with 10 s up holds and 15 s between changes.
- Stream resolution changes are now rare (streams only; calls keep their timers). A step down needs the target under the rung's limit for 3 s and congestion evidence that has lasted 3 s (`ClimbGuard.sustainedCongestion`: queue of 400 ms or more, or a late child with a queue, without a clean sample in between), so a target that fell on its own never changes the size; at least 15 s between changes. A step up needs 30 s without a queue over 150 ms or a late child, the target at 1.25x the larger rung for 30 s, and 60 s since the last change, one rung at a time. Replaying arm B with these timers gives one change (480p to 240p after 3 s of congestion) and no step back before 60 s.
- Arm C's frame-rate steps (15 to 12 to 10 at a 705 kbps TX, no freezes) came from the earlier congestion rule of the latency guard: a 534-580 ms queue from the controller's own climb overshoot (offered 1003 kbit against 800 carried, child on time) stepped the frame rate one level at once. Queue age alone is the rate controller's job and it had already cut the target; the frame rate now steps on congestion only when it is severe and continuous (1000 ms or more queued, or a late child with 400 ms, for 4 s).
- The viewer keeps the last frame through a planned size change: a keyframe the sender marked FLAG_DISCONTINUITY (the restart marker, set only when the restart changes the coded size) no longer starts the recovery cover (`isPlannedSizeChange`, log `planned_size_change: keepLastFrame=true`); a recovery already showing keeps its cover, and an unmarked rebuild is still treated as a recovery. The picture stays as the old size's last frame until the first frame of the new size renders.

Risks to check on phones. A resolution change restarts the camera encoder (call and now stream) and its first keyframe is large on a weak link; receivers must follow the in-band size change inside the header geometry, including relay children that joined before the change; AV1 repeats a codec-config packet per rung, and the broadcaster keeps up to its cap of distinct configs for late joiners. The measured ladder's 300 kbps unmeasured assumption can start 360p15 on a pair that carries less; the controller then walks it down within seconds.

Phone A/B plan (control = switch absent; arm = `test_auto_ladder=true`, same frozen APK, same pair, same transport, rate control Automatic; `test_auto_ladder_class=strong` only on the S24). Weak pair (65-150 kbps goodput, e.g. ASUS to A15s GATT, or Wi-Fi 2.4 GHz on): call and stream, expect audio-only recommendation or 240p10-15, no freeze longer than the control, media age not above control. Mid pair (300-450): call and stream at 360p15 against 240p/200; expect VMAF +8-9 at equal rate. Strong pair (700-1000, e.g. ASUS to S24 GATT): call and stream, 480p15/24 against 240p/200; strong class 720p15. Measure per case: VMAF (display reference where the source is native raw), delivered fps and visible-fps intervals, freezes and longest stall, receiver playout/media age lines, every rung change (`auto_ladder`, `call_resolution`), requested versus effective, camera restarts, and that Manual launches are byte-identical to the control.

### Viewer decoder across a size change (2026-10-13)

Changed: `VideoStreamDecoder.kt`, new `DecoderFormatTracker.kt` and `CodedVideoSize.kt`, `BitstreamDroppability.kt` (the AV1 sequence header now keeps `max_frame_width/height`), `AndroidVideoReceiver.kt`, `PrivateCallMedia.kt` (follow the new size; a replacement call decoder starts from the current csd), `MainActivity.kt` (marker on `restartStreamCamera` and `startPrivateCallCamera`, control extra), `CameraVideoEncoder.kt` (`outputResolution`), `BluetoothVideoBroadcaster.kt` and `ForwardedStreamSecurity.kt` (late-joiner csd cache), `DebugTunables.kt`. Wire: none (PROTOCOL "Mid-stream size changes"). Switch: default-on `test_decoder_size_rebuild` (control arm false).

What was broken (offline review of the code against libwebrtc, media3 and the MediaCodec reference; the corruption itself is not yet reproduced on a phone): the viewer configured one MediaCodec at the header's size, with no `KEY_MAX_WIDTH/HEIGHT`, and a size change was only logged (`decoder_output_size`). Under the Auto ladder the header names the requested (maximum) size while the encoder starts at the ladder's rung, so a viewer could be misconfigured from its first frame; a rung change restarts the encoder and its new csd was queued as a separate `BUFFER_FLAG_CODEC_CONFIG` buffer although MediaCodec accepts a changed configuration only together with a keyframe, and only for adaptive VP8/VP9/H.264/H.265 (AV1 is not documented). Recovery after a flush re-queued the stream-start csd, so after a rung change it put the old AV1 sequence header in front of a keyframe of the new size. The restart sent no discontinuity, so frames of the old size could still be queued ahead of the new keyframe. A late joiner could be handed the csd of two encoders (up to 4 cached packets at a source, 8 at a relay).

Fix: libwebrtc's `AndroidVideoDecoder.reinitDecode` pattern (release and create a decoder for the new picture; media3 re-creates a non-adaptive decoder the same way). The size is read from the keyframe bitstream so old and new builds stay wire-compatible; a keyframe that does not say changes nothing. The first decoder is configured at its first keyframe's size with the header as the adaptive-playback maximum. A size-changing restart marks its first keyframe `FLAG_DISCONTINUITY` (old receivers treat it as a recovery point); restarts that keep the size (flip, camera on/off) are unmarked and behave as before unless their csd differs. If the new decoder cannot be opened with the new csd it is retried without csd. Alternatives not built: in-place adaptive reconfiguration (rejected: undocumented for AV1, and the vendor results differ), a dedicated size record (rejected: it would break older receivers).

Rejected/limits: a relay sees only outer mesh flags, so its late-joiner csd cache is pruned by "config after media" only; a source also prunes at a discontinuity keyframe that follows no new config.

Re-check (affected scope): every Auto-ladder stream case and call-ladder case, any pair, any transport, VP9 and AV1, hardware and software decoders, plus a preservation chain for relayed streams and late joiners. Cases with the ladder off and no restart are affected only by the first-keyframe configure size and `KEY_MAX_*` (identical sizes when the header equals the keyframe size).

Phone test plan (frozen APK; control arm `test_decoder_size_rebuild=false` on the same pair): (1) stream rung change down and up (Auto ladder on the weak pair, VP9 hardware viewer and AV1 software viewer, plus an AV1 hardware viewer): `decoder_rebuild reason=size` or `size+csd` once per change, no green or pink frame, first frame at the new size within about 300 ms of the keyframe, display aspect follows; (2) camera flip and camera off/on at a constant size: no `decoder_rebuild` unless csd changed, rendering unchanged; (3) a viewer joining after a rung change (direct and through a relay): configured at the current size (`decoder_format configured=`), csd count 1; (4) decoder recovery (a forced key-frame loss) after a rung change: the restored csd is the new one (`Decoder recovery restored 1 codec-config buffer(s)`) and the picture recovers; (5) a call resolution-ladder step in both directions; (6) `test_decoder_size_rebuild=false` reproduces the earlier behaviour (control). Pass per phone: no corrupt picture across the change, rebuild under 150 ms on hardware decoders (`decoder_rebuild ms=`), no new freeze beyond the one keyframe wait.

### GPU scale ladder (2026-10-13)

Changed: new `GpuScaleMath.kt` (rung sizes, crop, texture matrix, capture-mode choice, the switch `GpuScaleFeature`), `GpuFrameRenderer.kt` (EGL context, external-texture shader, one draw per frame), `GpuScaleCapture.kt` (the shared camera: open once, encoders attach and detach); `CameraVideoEncoder.kt` (new `gpuScale` request, `retainCaptureForRestart`, per-rung BENCHMARK lines), `DeviceVideoCapabilities.kt` (`gpuModesFor`), `MainActivity.kt` (stream and call fit, ladder rungs, camera-flip check, restart keep-alive, resets), `AndroidPrivateVideoCall.kt` (`supportedVideoModes` now receives the requested settings, so the call's negotiated size is checked against the GPU rungs and not replaced by the nearest camera mode). Time: 2026-10-13. Wire: none.

Why: a rung change restarted the camera at another camera mode (hundreds of milliseconds to over a second, with a "Camera could not restart" failure mode), and the rungs were limited to each phone's mode list (the ASUS 3:2 mode after 16:9 rungs changed the aspect). libwebrtc captures at one size and draws every frame into the encoder's input surface through a crop/scale matrix (`VideoAdapter` rungs are exact fractions of the capture, a few pixels cropped for alignment instead of distorting; `HardwareVideoEncoder` re-creates the codec on a size change and its first frame is a keyframe); Grafika's `ContinuousCaptureActivity` is the minimal form of the same pattern. MediaCodec has no runtime size parameter, so the encoder reset is unavoidable, but it is now tens of milliseconds and touches neither the camera nor the aspect.

Behaviour (default off, `test_gpu_scale_ladder=true` together with `test_auto_ladder=true`, DEBUG launches only):
- Applies to a stream or call that runs the Auto ladder on a hardware surface-input encoder (`GpuScaleMath.offReason` names the first blocking condition in the `scope=stream|call` BENCHMARK line). It does not apply to the native libaom (YUV input), raw fixtures, benchmark reference frames or Manual video settings; they keep the camera-mode path and their current behaviour.
- Capture: the smallest camera mode (SurfaceTexture output sizes, at most 1920x1088, at the session frame rate) whose same-aspect picture covers the session's largest picture, else the smallest mode whose centre crop to the session aspect does. The top rung is then a 1:1 copy and every other rung a downscale, never an upscale.
- Rungs: 720/540/480/360/240/180/144p at the session aspect (16:9 snaps from 854x480, so 240p is 426x240 as before), width and height rounded to the encoder's `getWidthAlignment`/`getHeightAlignment`, only sizes `isSizeSupported` accepts and the capture can supply without upscaling. `DeviceVideoCapabilities.gpuModesFor` returns them as modes so `AutoQualityLadder.fitSettings`, `CallResolutionLadder.buildRungs` and the flip check are unchanged.
- Draw: one external-texture draw per frame; the crop is centred and exactly the rung's aspect; a downscale of 1.05x or more averages four bilinear taps a quarter of an output pixel around the centre (a box-like filter, so 720p to 144p is not an aliased single tap); a 1:1 copy is one tap. `eglPresentationTimeANDROID` carries the camera's sensor timestamp, so presentation times stay continuous across encoder resets.
- Switching: `old.close()` (the capture stays open 2.5 s when the close is a rung change, and is released at once for camera off, flip, pause and stop), then `new CameraVideoEncoder.start()` on the same executor; the old encoder is closed first because a phone may allow only one hardware encoder of a codec. The restart marker (`RestartDiscontinuityMarker`) and the viewer's decoder rebuild are unchanged.
- Orientation: Camera2 buffers stay in sensor orientation, as when the camera fed the encoder directly (display rotation is a flag, and the encoded front-camera stream is not mirrored: the viewer's transform logs mirror=false), so the GL path adds no rotation or mirror; the sensor timestamp base is the same as before.

Not done / limits: the native libaom path still takes camera-mode sizes (a GPU rung there needs a read-back to I420 or a CPU downscale; keyframe-free libaom downscale is a separate change); rungs below 240p exist in the table but the Auto limits (`callLimits`, floor 240p, the frame rate falls instead) keep them unreachable until a limit is measured at 65-150 kbps; a lens flip to a lens whose camera offers no suitable capture stops the stream with the usual message; the capture adds one GPU draw per frame and a YUV to RGB to YUV round trip in the encoder input (compare colour and VMAF at equal rate before promoting).

Re-check (affected scope when the switch is on): every Auto-ladder stream and call case on a hardware surface-input encoder (VP9 and AV1, any pair, any transport), relay and late-joiner chains that see a rung change, and camera flip, camera off/on and pause/resume of such sessions. With the switch off the code path is unchanged.

Phone test plan (frozen APK; control arm = `test_gpu_scale_ladder` absent with `test_auto_ladder=true`; same pair, transport, rate control Automatic; run per phone S24, ASUS, M31 VP9 hardware, and the A15s only to confirm it stays on the camera-mode path with `reason=yuv_input_encoder`): (1) stream and call rung changes down and up during a run (weak link to force the steps): `rung=WxH | encoderResetMs` per change (target tens of milliseconds, record the actual), `firstFrameMs`, no `Camera could not restart`, the preview never blinks, `decoder_rebuild reason=size` once per change on the viewer and no green or pink frame; (2) `gpuMs/frame` submit and finish p50/p95 per rung on each GPU (record, do not assume 1-2 ms), plus battery and thermal status over a long run against the control; (3) picture correctness with a controlled target: no stretch (aspect of every rung), correct rotation in portrait and landscape, back and front camera, lens flip mid-stream, camera off/on, pause/resume, and the 3:2 ASUS mode keeping 16:9 rungs through a crop; (4) colour and quality at equal rate against the control (the YUV to RGB round trip); (5) a viewer that joins after a rung change, direct and through a relay; (6) context loss and app background/foreground (the preview surface refresh). Report requested versus effective rung per change, as the Auto ladder does.

## Default video settings (2026-10-10)

Calls and streams default to 426x240 at 15 FPS and 200 kbps (was 640x360 at 300 kbps). WebRTC's VP9 singlecast start limits put 360p at about 350 kbps, so 300 kbps under-fed 360p, and real Bluetooth links (especially relays) often deliver less. 360p and 480p remain choices, and the call resolution ladder can step up when the link allows. Benchmark launches pass explicit settings, so they are unaffected.

## Scope for the first release (2026-10-10)

- Scope: direct voice/video calls and multi-hop live streams (viewers relay automatically). Private-call relaying is DEBUG/test-only today; user-facing background call relaying (a "Helping a call nearby" notification, the person's own activity pre-empts it, pauses below 20% battery) plus multi-hop discovery (naming phase 2) is the first feature after release, behind an experimental switch.
- Publication: the public repositories are MeshLive/meshlive-android, MeshLive/meshlive-ios and MeshLive/.github, all GPL-3.0-or-later, copyright "The MeshLive Authors", each starting from a fresh single commit. The video stress generator is published inside meshlive-android. Device identifiers are scrubbed from published files, and the test phones' app identities are reset after launch. The [README](../README.md#how-this-was-built) names the AI used and [CONTRIBUTING.md](../CONTRIBUTING.md) sets the policy for AI-assisted work. The speech test audio (LibriSpeech CC BY 4.0, LibriVox public domain, an Open Goldberg Variations CC0 clip) is published with attribution in [THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md](../THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md).

## Open-defect investigation notes (moved from the plan, 2026-10-12)

These are the detailed findings that used to sit in the plan's open-defect and policy sections. The plan lists only what is still open.

### Open-defect investigation notes (the original table, moved from the plan)

## Open defects in shipped behaviour

These are open across every phone combination that uses the affected behaviour. The benchmark suite's case state (see
[TESTING.md](TESTING.md)) owns pass and fail history and retest requirements.

| Work | What must be established before closing it |
| --- | --- |
| Uneven playback and picture quality | Separate source pacing, encoder output, Bluetooth stalls, receive queues, decoder processing and presentation. Correlate traces with run, session and PID identity and each phone's clock; compare the same input and settings. Frame-timing proxies and short clean prefixes cannot approve screen smoothness. The retained scheduler analysis has initial-only clock snapshots, so obtain the missing stage and clock evidence before promoting another display policy |
| Connecting/Recovering flashes and reconnect-to-scan loops | Trace each setup, scan, cancellation, disconnect and completion against its owning attempt and session. Establish a stable UI state, bounded retries, truthful discovery and reusable availability after End, loss and cancellation; keep planned losses distinct from unexpected ones. Causes found offline from saved viewer logs (2026-10-11): the recovery cover (a 70% black layer) followed the decoder's recovery flag with no hysteresis and flashed on and off about a dozen times in 30 s on older builds; a failed join returns to the browser by design while its route cools down. Fix built (default on, control arm `test_receiver_ui_stability=false`, not yet tested on phones). Still open: a failed join still shows the playback screen for 0.25-0.4 s, some "Connecting" overlays wait the full 15 s with no progress text, and planned versus unexpected loss is not distinguished in the UI |
| Real three-phone chains | Diagnose the retained decrypt failures, missing completions and guard withdrawals without assuming that event order across different phone clocks proves the cause. Preserve the authenticated original source, requested immediate parent, ordered encryption, admission and overload protection; direct-source fallback fails the requested chain |
| Bidirectional calls and reverse setup | Resolve the reverse-caller GATT setup that queued MTU work without completing it; trace request, callback, teardown and stale-session ownership. Native inputs reject a negotiated geometry or FPS mismatch before media starts, so prepare exact matching native call requests or report ineligibility. Improve both directions, local controls, framing and recovery independently. Causes found offline (2026-10-11): the original wedge was MTU, PHY and priority work queued within 1 ms of the connection callback (fixed by the setup order MTU, services, subscription, then the client PHY); the stalls that remain (21 of 1,167 saved setup attempts with an MTU request and no callback) are either a dropped request (the stack logs `configureMTU() - No connection`, all before the deferred GATT close on SDK <= 30) or a request queued behind the stack's own service discovery (A15s median 1.34 s, a 5th relay child 3.2 s). Fix built (default on, control arm `test_gatt_mtu_probe=false`, not yet tested on phones): at 0.75 s without a callback one `readPhy` proves the request is only queued and the wait runs to 8 s; no callback keeps the 3 s verdict. Still open: the retry onto a half-closed ACL, and one A15s case that received the MTU response with no app callback |
| Relayed viewers hold seconds of media | A viewer behind a relay showed 2.7-7.5 s held age with its playout pinned at 2000 ms (direct 250-345 ms). Traced per stage (S24 -> M31 -> ASUS, LE CoC, 200 kbps offered): the source and the first hop add tens of milliseconds, the relay forwards the original frames, the relay's app queue holds 1.3-1.9 s, the Android socket buffer and stack below it 3.2-4.4 s, the air carries a flat 144 kbps (one PDU per 15 ms event), and the viewer's playout then holds 2 s because it cannot shrink while media is late. The relay skipped its child pacer whenever audio was queued anywhere, which is always during a backlog. Built 2026-10-11: `test_relay_pace_head` (default on, control arm false) and Experimental `test_relay_pace_lead_cap` (see the register in ARCHITECTURE.md). Open: a phone A/B on one frozen build (control / head fix / head fix + lead cap, CoC and GATT, 3 repeats, a direct control); a policy for a relay link slower than the stream (shed, withdraw and reroute, lower the sender rate, or batch audio records); a relay holds its own screen 1562 ms (the child-join reserve) |
| Relay dual-role scheduling (M31 relay child link) | A relay is LE peripheral to its parent and LE central to its child, both at 15 ms, so their anchors keep one fixed phase. On the M31 (Exynos 9611) a child anchor 11.3-13.7 ms after the parent anchor got no completed exchange (9 of 55 child attempts silent until disconnect reason 0x08, 22 never established with 0x3E; ASUS: 0 and 5 of 29); the 7.5 ms and 45 ms stages never died. Built: default-on `test_relay_child_stall_guard` (closes a silent LE child link after 2 s, counted by the establishment fallback), Experimental `test_relay_upstream_dck` (an Android 14+ parent requests DCK, 30 ms, for a relay-capable child so the relay's 15 ms child link has two events per parent event), an inert device-policy proposal, and `tools/relay_dual_role_snoop.py`. Open: the phone A/B with HCI snoops on both relay candidates (relay under test and control relay, GATT and CoC, planned viewer restarts labelled, 3 repeats, at least 15 child attempts per arm; DCK passes when the parent link shows 30 ms, no silent timeouts and no worse leaf freezes; the stall guard passes when a dead attempt ends within 3.5 s and no healthy join is closed). Evidence: DECISIONS "Relay dual-role scheduling" |
| Call speech behind video writes (head-of-line) | GATT call audio waited 620-1193 ms behind one video frame; the audio-headroom fix only stopped queue overflow. A four-arm A/B (control, fragments, fragments + speech-led playout, fragments + airtime-sized chunks) on M31 <-> ASUS and S24 <-> A15s is in DECISIONS "Call head-of-line A/B"; fragments with speech-led playout are on for direct GATT calls in user launches since 0.2.0-beta. Open: received-audio continuity and A/V offset with speech-led playout, CPU and battery cost on the M31 sender, a third repeat on two arms, A15s over CoC, and an HCI-level check that audio really goes on air between fragments |
| Visible age of the last received frame | A frozen frame must never be presented as live. Built behind the default-off switch `test_viewer_frame_age` (Settings > Features): stale-picture dim and badge, an honest "Last update" label (this phone's last picture, not camera-to-screen delay) and `viewer_frame_age` log lines. Remaining: a phone test in the relay loss/rejoin lane, then flip the default. Still open: a stall that keeps the link up replays its backlog late by the no-loss design (the bounded-age experiment is the separate answer); a clock-synced end-to-end age would need a protocol design |
| Stream link role reversal (design, needs a PROTOCOL review) | Today a relay or source is the LE central and connects to the advertising child. A relay that is a busy peripheral on its parent link cannot always establish that second, central connection (an M31 snoop shows ten LE Connection Complete events at 45 ms, each ending 0x3E after 6 events). Root fix: the child connects to the relay as central and the relay accepts the CoC, with the same authentication, grant and hop-local control. It would remove the central-initiation failures but not the equal-interval collision above, so do not build it before the dual-role A/B shows how many failures remain with `test_relay_upstream_dck`. Before coding, record in PROTOCOL.md the advertisement and accept flow and its admission check, discovery and privacy impact, and behaviour on Android 10-14 stacks |
| Requested versus effective settings | Use the register of concrete limits and the capability evidence to challenge call budgets, legacy protections and other limits. DEBUG higher-rate comparisons stay experiments. Expose every difference in encoder, allocation, geometry, rate, FPS, audio and buffer rather than silently substituting settings |

### Policy workstream status (build 454cf485, 2026-10-07 and later)

Status (build `454cf485`, 2026-10-07, plus later notes):

- **A.** No media is dropped for streams; native AV1 pairs showed 0 freezes and 0 audio drops up to a CoC offer 1200
  kbps above the link (measured 2026-10-07). Open: the policy for calls beyond the link's capacity (lower call frame
  rate, longer call delay, or the current video skip) and visible-FPS dips while an overloaded link's delay grows.
- **B.** Hop-local 6-byte reports (arrival rate, pinned delay, added delay); learned ceilings persist with a 6-hour
  re-check.
- **C.** Native inter frames are capped at 250 ms of the per-viewer link share. The M31's OMX VP9 encoder ignores
  video-QP bounds and offers only VBR, and its large frames are four-frame scene-change bursts (about 210 KB), so
  keyframe shaping (a DEBUG A/B) is not promoted. Next: forced CBR on the M31 (DEBUG `test_bitrate_mode`) and the
  15-20 s encoder trim, judged by same-build VMAF.
- **Relay child joins.** Cause found: the joining child's keyframe request crosses the parent link exactly when it
  loses airtime to the new child link (S24 VP9 keyframe of 55.7 KB, link 791 -> 269 kbps, a 1.7 s gap). Options: start
  the child from the relay's next natural keyframe, or grow the relay's playout delay before it connects a child.
- **D.** The picker uses the measured link (fixed maxima are fallbacks). Still to replace: the 20% per-link planning
  margin and the 0.9 ceiling headroom.

### Interim bounded-age evidence

Interim bounded-age evidence (not conclusive; repeats were still running): with the A15s sending over a weak
libaom/AV1 link, the bounded arm paused video one to three times per call (longest stall 3.8-9.2 s, 26-58% frozen,
against 0.4-2.7 s and 3-18% for no-loss) and did not improve speech; on M31 <-> ASUS calls at 1000 kbps it was worse
(CoC held-age p95 1479 against 1108 ms) or had about twice the freezes (GATT); S24-sending directions and M31 <-> ASUS
streams were about equal. Likely cause: the call arm assumes a 500 ms peer base delay (a 100 ms queue budget) and
requested keyframes re-create bursts. Current recommendation: keep no-loss as the default and bounded age as an
off-by-default experiment; a fairer variant would exchange the playback profile in call setup, make audio-only the last
resort and recover with intra-refresh or the next natural keyframe.

### Discovery privacy status (2026-10-12)

Status (2026-10-12): rotating discovery IDs, the BMH3 identity-hiding handshake and pseudonyms toward strangers are on
by default since 0.2.0-beta (one constant each; an explicit stored choice wins). The design was reviewed on 2026-10-10
(findings RV1-RV19). Coded: phase 1 (LE call-ready presence), the Visible-by-handle window, phase 1b (stream offers,
viewer requests, forwarders, call-relay chains), phase 2 (presence leases, `BDX1`, the recognition table), reach pages
version 6 for relayed calls and non-discoverable connectable sets on Android 14+; formats and vectors are in PROTOCOL.md
under "Experimental rotating discovery IDs". Measured once, on four phones: no field carried over any of three ID
changes (PRIVACY.md "Measurements"). Not measured: battery cost, the Bluetooth name read on the A15s, an
active attacker holding a contact's key, fake adverts, relays with the new handshake and abuse limits. Open design
question: the monotonic epoch never returns after a backward clock step, so a lead of 3 to 96 epochs leaves a phone
unable to receive a version 5 call until it recovers. The list below was the plan; items 0 and 1 were exercised in that
session, the rest follow:

### Design C status

- **Status.** The first phone probe (2026-10-11) found a helper crash from an API 33 call (fixed; see DECISIONS
  "Relayed calls: first phone probe"). In the 0.2.0-beta combined privacy session, one-helper calls completed on GATT
  and LE CoC; two helpers never formed a route. Deferred: automatic candidate iteration inside one attempt, the embedded
  single-entry carrier for phones without a free advertising set, Classic on-demand relays, mid-call re-routing,
  extended-advertising reach lists and an iPhone caller.

### Design F rationale, reviews and measurements

**Why.** Rotating discovery IDs hide the advertisement only; the BMH2 link handshake sends both identity keys in the
clear on every hop and answers any connector with its own. BMH3 is a SIGMA-style handshake in both reveal orders using
the existing primitives and the signing-only Keystore key, with a lease-keyed contact tag bound to the selected advert.
Phase 2 gives a stranger who connects a throwaway software identity per connection; the real identity follows only for
verified contacts or after the callee presses Accept, in a signed `REVEAL` bound to the session.

**Reviews.** Two independent security reviews (findings SR1-SR14 and BR1-BR11 for the handshake, PR1-PR15 for the
pseudonyms) were adopted or rejected with reasons in the design record; the executable specification is
`tools/bmh3_reference.py` with byte-exact vectors in the JVM tests.

**Measured** (the combined privacy session of 2026-10-11, build 3996bfb2): a snoop of the BMH2 control call showed both
identities in the clear and none in three stranger calls; the reveal followed Accept and never followed Decline. Not
measured: battery, the A15s name read, an active lease-key prober, fake adverts, relays with BMH3, message exchanges and
abuse limits.

### Stalled-setup rediscovery budget (2026-10-15, `test_stall_rediscover_ms`)

**Broken (measured):** lane11-stats-20261015/b2-calls-a15s-asus, A15s to ASUS private calls on 2M: 5 of 6 failed. The 2M setup stalled (`setup_stall`, action=close at 3 s); the A15s controller sometimes never delivered LL_TERMINATE, so the ASUS saw supervision timeout 0x08 about 7-9 s after the close (instead of 0x13 after 1-3 s) and re-advertised only 1.6 s after that, 8.6-12.6 s after the close. `rediscoverPeer` budgeted min(8 s, 15 s early-loss window minus elapsed), ended `rediscover=none` and dialed the stale address, which timed out after 12 s. In all 7 successful rediscoveries the address had changed (`sameAddress=false`), so the stale dial cannot succeed.

**Changed (Experimental, default off):** `test_stall_rediscover_ms` (8000 = unchanged; A/B arm 20000). After a `CallSetupStalledException` the scan is decoupled from the early-loss clamp and runs up to the configured total (60 s at most), a found fresh address is dialed at once, the stale address only when nothing was found. Bad-2M memory, the 1M retry, hop-local rules and the relayed-offer rule are untouched. Files: AdaptivePhy.kt (`StallRediscover`), AndroidPrivateVideoCall.kt, DebugTunables.kt, MainActivity.kt; tests `StallRediscoverTest`.

**Re-check:** phone A/B on A15s to ASUS (6 calls per arm, learned state reset, one frozen APK): calls reaching active, `BENCHMARK call_rediscover` waitedMs and dialed=fresh|stale; preservation: S24/M31 pairs must not change (the stall path is rare there). Affects every direct call dial that stalls on 2M, not only this pair.

### Duplicate role-reversal row

| Stream link role reversal (**design review required**, PROTOCOL change) | Today a relay or source is the LE central and connects to the advertising child. A relay that is a busy peripheral on its parent link cannot always establish that second, central connection: an M31 relay snoop (2026-10-10) shows ten LE Connection Complete events at a 45 ms interval, each ending 0x3E after 6 events. Root fix: the child connects to the relay as central, and the relay advertises its offer and accepts the CoC, with the same hop and end-to-end authentication, grant and hop-local control; the busy relay then never initiates, and each phone is central for at most its own parent link. Before coding, record in [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md): the advertisement and accept flow and its admission check before accept; discovery and privacy impact; behaviour on Android 10-14 stacks (CoC server on the relay); how the child chooses or keeps the 2M/1M PHY; and A/B evidence against `relay_establish_fallback` on the same topologies. |

## Design records

The full texts of the larger designs that used to sit in the plan, kept here as the record of what was proposed, reviewed and decided. [PLAN.md](PLAN.md) carries only each design's status and what is left; wire rules are in [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md). Related status and rationale sections are "Design C status" and "Design F rationale, reviews and measurements" above.

### Design A: store-and-forward messages (delay tolerant)

**Design review required.** Default off (`test_store_forward`, Experimental setting). The v1 wire format and its
differences from the original draft are in PROTOCOL.md under "Experimental store-and-forward messages". Nothing here
changes BMH2, BMS1 or BME1 or live sessions.

**Goal.** Text, voice (Opus 16-24 kbps), photo (about 1280 px) and short video (about 200 kbps, at most 60 s) reach a
contact or group without a live link, carried by phones that meet. A walk-past contact lasts about 28 s and leaves about
0.7-1.5 MB per pass, so transfers go small-first, are resumable and use a 2 MiB maximum bundle.

**Design in brief.** Each identity has a mailbox key and a contact card exchanged by QR or inside an authenticated
session; there is no directory. A bundle is sealed end to end (the recipient authenticates the sender, carriers learn
nothing about it, no transferable signature), padded, split into 8 KiB chunks with a hash list in an immutable header,
and addressed by a pairwise daily recipient tag. Carriers store bundles under quotas (64 MB foreign storage, at most
25% per neighbour), verify every chunk without decrypting, and hand copies over the existing authenticated, ordered hop
link (BMS1 hop type 11). Live media always has priority, and forwarding stays hop-local.

| Phase | Scope | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| v1 two-phone | Cards, `BSB1`/`BSM1`/`BSF1`, hop type 11, chunk, resume, dedup, expiry, receipts, storage, live preemption; direct link only. Coded, JVM-tested only | JVM vectors pass, and a phone pair completes all classes at 150-1200 kbps over GATT and Classic; a mid-chunk kill resumes; a live call preempts within one chunk |
| v2 one carrier | Opt-in carrier role, quotas, fairness, per-neighbour limits, a single-copy token, carrier battery budget | A -> B -> C with B unable to open the content (asserted in test); delivery latency, B's overnight battery and quota eviction measured |
| v3 gossip | Spray-and-wait (default 6 copies), a version-3 discovery digest, decoy WANTs, optional proof of work and tombstones | 4-6 phones in two groups plus a walking carrier: delivery ratio, latency, duplicate bytes and per-phone airtime measured, with no live-session regression |

Open design questions (recommendation first; each needs review before v2 ships):

| Question | Recommendation | Alternatives |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Who carries others' messages? | Opt-in, 64 MB quota, 50 MB/day cap, paused below 20% battery | On for everyone; contacts' groups only |
| Messages from non-contacts? | Contacts only | A capped request folder; public signed bulletins |
| Delivery receipts? | On for 1:1 with time coarsened to the hour, off for groups, no read receipts | All off; all on |
| Message lifetime? | 3 days default, 14 days maximum | 1 and 7 days; 7 and 30 days |
| Clearing delivered copies? | Expiry only, then tell the adjacent carrier in v3 | Gossip tombstones (bends the hop-local rule) |
| Mailbox link identity? | Persistent now, rotating later | Ephemeral now |
| Sender proof? | Deniable | Third-party provable |
| Spam control? | Quotas plus contacts only | Proof of work on everything |
| Message size? | Voice 2 min, video 60 s at about 200 kbps, 2 MiB bundles | Larger |
| **Forward secrecy** | Add it before carrying (v2) ships: one-time prekeys in the contact card or refreshed during live contact, a fresh key per message, prekeys deleted after use, and user authentication on the key-wrapping key where supported. Cost: more complex cards and prekey refresh | Keep v1 as is (direct only, nothing retained on carriers) and add it only for v2 carriers; or accept and disclose the limitation in the UI |

The limitation itself is described in PROTOCOL.md ("Forward-secrecy limitation"): both slot keys derive from the
recipient's long-term mailbox key, so a later compromise of that key decrypts retained bundles and reveals senders.

Risks: contact windows may be too short on slow stacks; mailbox ECDH splits at API 31; clock skew affects day tags and
expiry; Sybil storage abuse and colluding-carrier traffic analysis (inherent to delay-tolerant networks, so stated in
user-facing text); OEM background-scan limits; seized-phone forensics (see "Emergency wipe" in PROTOCOL.md). Prototype
first: a 1 MB bundle between two phones with a forced disconnect and a live-call preemption decides the 8 KiB chunk and
2 MiB limits.

### Design M: messaging v2 (chat threads, files of every format, receipts, forward secrecy, carriers)


**Goal.** A person you have verified can send you text, voice notes, photos and any file (a PDF, a zip, a video, an APK) with the same trust model as a call: end to end encrypted, authenticated to the contact, no server, no internet. Delivery is delay tolerant: phones that are adjacent exchange at once, and later (phase 3) other phones may carry sealed bundles between people who are never in range at the same time. Messaging must never steal the radio from a live call or stream.

**What users get** (the phase that delivers each is in the plan below).

- One *conversation* per contact: a thread with bubbles, date separators, a composer, and honest per-message state (waiting, sending with progress, delivered, read, not sent). Phase 1.
- An attachment sheet: Photo or video, File, Voice note. Photos are made smaller by default with a visible "Original" choice (and the location data is removed either way when we re-encode). Any file type can be sent up to 64 MB. Files above 1 MB are not pushed at the receiver: the receiver sees a small card (name, size, picture thumbnail), downloads on a tap or automatically up to a chosen size, and can cancel and resume. Phase 1.
- Receipts: two checks when the contact's phone has the message, filled checks when they read it (read receipts are off by default and only work when both people turn them on). Phase 1.
- "Keep the link open" while a thread is on screen and the contact is nearby, so text arrives within about a second instead of at the next exchange. Phase 1.
- Forward secrecy for stored messages: each message is protected by a key that the recipient deletes after opening it (a prekey, or from phase 2b a Double Ratchet message key), so a seized phone or a later key leak cannot decrypt old bundles that carriers or the sender still hold. Phase 2 and 2b (before any carrier ships).
- Other phones carrying sealed bundles for you (opt-in, 64 MB, pauses on low battery). Phase 3.
- Not in this design: group chats (sketched, phase 4), reactions, typing indicators, link previews, message editing, multi-device, cloud backup, video transcoding.

#### Why v1 is not enough (facts from the code, 2026-10-12)

| v1 behaviour | Consequence | Fix in this design |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MessagesActivity` lists "Inbox" and "Sent" separately and picks the contact in a spinner | No conversation; a reply is not next to what it answers | One thread per contact (the 16-byte conversation ID already exists in `BSM1`, derived from the two node IDs) |
| Cards (`BCC1`) are a second contact system: paste text, compare a 20-digit safety number, accept | Two ways to add a person, easy to skip verification | The mailbox card travels inside the verified-contact flow; a card whose identity key hashes to an already verified contact is accepted without a second comparison |
| Attachments: photo (1280 px, 300 KB target) and voice (2 min); whole message is one `ByteArray` and one bundle of at most 256 chunks (2 MiB) | No files; large memory use per message | `ATTACHMENT` manifest plus 1 MiB segments, streamed from and to disk |
| Opened messages are deleted at the bundle's expiry hour (default 72 h) | Conversations vanish after three days by default | Delivery lifetime and retention are separate; default is keep until deleted |
| An exchange needs both phones on the Call screen and a tap, or the auto-sync policy (65 s to 10 min backoff) | Chat feels like e-mail by pigeon even when the person is next to you | A per-thread live link (below) |
| Per exchange 8 MB, per neighbour 20 MB/day, 50 bundles per sender per day, own queue 32 MB | A single 10 MB file cannot be sent | Two admission tiers: accepted contacts get large allowances, unknown adjacent phones (future carriers) keep today's numbers |
| Receipt = class 0 bundle with an hour | Fine, but no read state, no batching | `RECEIPT` with a status and up to 64 IDs |

#### What the reference apps teach (mined 2026-10-12 from local clones of the projects; GPL/AGPL code is a pattern only, nothing is copied)

| Topic | What they do (citation) | What we take or reject |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Large attachment, who decides | bitchat pushes one file as 469-byte fragments with no resume; whole-file retry up to 2 times; 1 MiB cap on iOS, about 110 KiB on Android (`FileTransferLimits.swift:4-8`, `BLEFragmentAssemblyBuffer.swift`, `PRIVATE-MEDIA-MIGRATION.md:108-121`). qaul pushes 64,000-byte chunks to everyone with no cap, no consent and no resume (`rust/libqaul/src/services/chat/file.rs:39,934-975`). Kabootar floods 16,000-character chunks and clears unfinished transfers at restart (`lib/services/chat_service.dart:742-960`). Knit is pull only: the chat frame names a content hash and only the asker fetches, resuming by byte offset, 8 MiB in-mesh and anything bigger over a separate direct link, never custodied (`.agents/context/blobs.md`, `direct-transfer.md`) | Adopt Knit's pull model and size tiers (the receiver decides, resume exists, bulk bytes are never carried by third parties). We resume by chunk bitmap, which the store-and-forward link already has, so we do not need an offset protocol. Reject push-and-hope (bitchat, qaul, Kabootar) |
| Link speed vs size | Knit paces a Bluetooth file at 4 KiB/s base, 18 KiB/s on a boosted link (about 33 and 147 kbps), so an 8 MiB file takes about 8 minutes (`docs/PICTURE_FETCH_BLUETOOTH.md:252-289`) | Our measured links are 65 to 1000 kbps (8 to 125 KiB/s); the throughput table below sets which sizes are practical and what the UI says |
| Types and safety | Knit refuses installable packages and warns before saving risky types, and re-routes a file that is really an image through the image pipeline (`data/FileTypes.kt:55-71`, `AttachmentStore.kt:511-547`). bitchat allow-lists MIME types and checks magic bytes on receive (`BLEFileTransferPolicy.swift:44-70`, `MimeType.swift:102-165`). qaul checks only the extension (`file.rs:461-470`) | Any type may be sent, none is ever opened or installed automatically, magic bytes decide whether we draw a preview, risky types get a save-only path with a warning |
| Image re-encode | bitchat: 448 px, JPEG 0.82 stepping down to about 45 KB, metadata dropped by re-encoding (`ImageUtils.swift:16-115`). Kabootar: 1600 px, q80, plus a 240 px q55 thumbnail (`media_codec.dart:22-40`). Knit: 1280 px, q85, animated WebP for GIF, and "pass through untouched if already within bounds" | Default 1600 px longest edge, JPEG q80 (about 250-400 KB), 320 px thumbnail, "Original" opt-in; reject the 448 px preset (too small to read a document photo) |
| Receipts | bitchat: delivered and read as tiny Noise payloads, monotonic, sent three times with identical bytes; no gate for receipts between non-favorites (`DeliveryStatus.swift`, `RetryingControlPacketSender.kt:16-18`, `ChatLifecycleCoordinator.swift:215-218`). Meshtastic separates "relayed, not confirmed by the recipient" from "delivered to the recipient" (`MeshDataHandlerImpl.kt:392-436`). Knit batches group ticks (64 IDs, 45 s debounce) into one sealed frame and says a receipt must never purge a carried copy (`store-and-forward.md:28-64`) | Two-tier ticks (never call a carrier hand-over "delivered"), batching, read receipts as a mutual opt-in (bitchat has no privacy switch for them) |
| Outbox | bitchat: 100 messages per peer, 24 h lifetime, 8 unacknowledged attempts then failed, persisted sealed (`MessageRouter.swift:129-137`). Meshtastic: after 5 minutes without an acknowledgement the packet becomes retryable and is never retried when too large (`PacketHandlerImpl.kt:81-95`) | The v1 auto-sync policy already follows bitchat's 8 attempts; add a per-contact cap of 100 waiting messages |
| Ordering | bitchat Android orders private messages by local arrival sequence, not the peer's clock (`PrivateMessageArrivalOrder.kt`) | We order by `min(sender time, arrival time)`: a delay-tolerant reply cannot appear before the message it answers, and a wrong sender clock cannot place a message in the future |
| Forward secrecy for stored messages | Knit: X3DH-style bootstrap from a signed prekey that rotates every 7 days (current plus 4 kept) and an epoch ratchet (an epoch ends at 200 sends or 24 h, skipped keys 48 h, at most 200), chosen over a full Double Ratchet "because custody gives permanent holes and no ordering"; the claim is epoch-granular and says so (`docs/FORWARD_SECRECY_RATCHET.md:20-45,140-152`). qaul: Noise KK session rotation every 7 days or 2^20 messages with a 1 h grace, dormant by default (`docs/protocols/Noise-Session-Rotation.md`). bitchat: courier envelopes have no forward secrecy, stated plainly in the whitepaper, and it added optional one-time prekeys (`WHITEPAPER.md:81,136`). Briar: see the Briar row | One-time prekeys plus a 7-day rotating prekey (Knit's bootstrap, without the epoch ratchet, because we seal a fresh key for every bundle anyway) |
| Groups | Knit: sender-key chains seeded over the pairwise ratchet, founding roster at most 8, eventual re-key on leave. bitchat: at most 16 members, creator-signed roster with an epoch, a takeover check, offline members miss re-keys. qaul: pairwise fan-out, no group key, so one message gets N IDs and breaks ID-keyed dedup and receipts | Version 2 headers have room for up to 8 key slots in one bundle with one ID, which would avoid qaul's problem, but versions 3 to 5 require exactly one slot until groups are designed (second review, finding 8); phase 4 |
| Thread list and bubble UX | bitchat: unread boolean, relative time, status glyph with tap-to-reveal caption, received pictures blurred until tapped. qaul: unread count, last-message preview, image bubble if the MIME type is `image/*`, spinner while receiving. Meshtastic: reply, drafts shown in the list, mute with undo | Preview line, unread count, status glyph with a caption on tap, tap-to-download card |
| Notification privacy | bitchat: "hide previews" gives a generic title and no sender (`NotificationService.swift:98-112`) | v1 already posts no text and no sender; keep |
| Briar | Attachments are images only (JPEG, PNG, GIF), each its own sync message of at most 32 KiB after a compressor that halves to 1000 px and steps JPEG quality down; no chunking, with a source TODO for large messages (`MessagingManagerImpl.java:338-361`, `ImageCompressorImpl.java:50-80`). Messages are content addressed; an offer, request and ack triplet with `seen` and `requested` flags, 1000 stored offers per contact, resend at `2 x maxLatency x 2^txCount` and every unacknowledged message becomes eligible again on a new connection (`ExponentialBackoff.java`, `JdbcDatabase.java:2268-2376`). Three ticks only: stored, sent, delivered; no failed state (`OutItemViewHolder.java:21-31`). Disappearing timers: 1 minute to 365 days, the sender's countdown starts at the first acknowledgement and the receiver's when the message is read (`AutoDeleteManagerImpl`, `ConversationManagerImpl.java:136`). Transport keys ratchet one way per period (about 24 h for Bluetooth), three incoming sets kept, reordering window 32, so wire forward secrecy only: stored messages are not individually protected (`TransportCryptoImpl.java:110-246`, `TransportConstants.java:74`). Rejects messages more than 24 h in the future | Take the disappearing-timer start rules and the offer/request/ack shape (already ours). Our attachments are chunked with per-chunk resume, which Briar and Serval both lack. Briar's transport ratchet is option D in M9 and does not protect stored bundles |
| Serval Rhizome | Bundle manifest at most 8 KiB, payloads over 128 KiB in files; the sync queue ranks by `log2(remaining bytes)` with a bias of 0 when this peer is the next hop to the recipient, and fetches run in size-class lanes (under 1 KiB, 8 KiB, 64 KiB, 512 KiB, 4 MiB, larger) with one active fetch per lane so a big item never blocks a small one; eviction by age minus size; no general resume, only journals (`rhizome_sync_keys.c:276-296`, `rhizome_fetch.c:105-134`, `rhizome_store.c:213-300`, `rhizome_fetch.c:499-576`) | Adopt the size lanes inside one exchange: the writer always serves the smallest waiting class first (a text jumps ahead of the next chunk of a segment). Reject its absence of resume |
| Berty/weshnet | Docs only: ciphertext custody by replication devices that cannot read; per-device chain key per group, message key from the chain, used key discarded, no post-compromise security, chain renewal only proposed | Confirms that carriers hold ciphertext they cannot read; chain ratchets need ordering, so not used |

#### Decisions

**M1. One conversation per contact, one contact system.** A thread belongs to an accepted contact. Messages from non-contacts stay refused (Design A QA2). The mailbox card is exchanged inside the authenticated link as today (`CARD 9`); a card whose identity key hashes to the fingerprint of a verified contact (the Contacts tab, `BVMID1`/`BVMID2`) is accepted at once, because the user already compared that identity in person and the card is signed by it. Any other card stays pending behind the safety-number comparison. If a verified contact's card arrives with a different identity or mailbox key, the thread shows "<name>'s keys changed" and sends nothing until the user compares again (PATTERNS 8.3; today a changed key is silently ignored). A card is accepted only if its `created ms` is greater than the stored one for that identity (a replayed old signed card cannot roll a key back). A first message can be written as soon as a contact exists; it waits until the two phones meet. Migration from v1: accepted cards become threads, v1 messages stay readable as legacy rows, and messages still queued unsent in the v1 outbox are re-sealed once as version 3 bundles at the first v2 start (the v1 bundle is withdrawn). A peer that answered a v2 `HELLO` with `DONE(error)` is shown in the thread as "Their app is older: update to receive" until it answers a v2 hello.

**M2. What is a message.** Content `BSM2` (PROTOCOL) replaces `BSM1`. Kinds a v2 sender emits: text (at most 8000 bytes, which with the header fits one chunk), attachment manifest, segment, receipt, prekeys (phase 2). Voice notes and photos are attachments with a presentation hint, not separate kinds, so they share download, resume and storage. Location stays defined and unused. A thread is keyed by the *authenticated* sender (the contact whose slot opened the bundle): the conversation ID inside the message must equal `SHA-256(sorted node IDs)[0:16]` of this phone and that sender, and `reply-to` is honoured only for a bundle in the same thread; a mismatch is refused, so a contact can never file a message into another contact's thread. A reader that meets an unknown kind or a newer bundle version stores nothing, replies `REFUSE invalid` and counts the event; it never shows a half message. Every message carries a random 16-byte *message ID* inside the sealed content; the receiver deduplicates on (authenticated sender, message ID) as well as on the bundle ID (finding 3), and a bundle header must have exactly one key slot (finding 8).

**M3. Attachments of every format.**

- *Inline or segmented.* A file of at most 1,000,000 bytes travels inside its manifest bundle (inline): one bundle, no second step, and it is carriable by phase 3 couriers. A larger file (up to 64,000,000 bytes) is cut into segments of 1,044,480 bytes (255 blocks of 4 KiB, which keeps a segment inside 128 chunks and block aligned in the vault). Each segment is its own class 4 (bulk) bundle. The manifest lists, for every segment, its 32-byte bundle ID and its chunk count, so the receiver can verify before it fetches, knows what to ask for and what each segment must contain, and checks a whole-file SHA-256 at the end. The manifest also carries a random 32-byte *file key*; each segment's content key is `HKDF(file key, index)`, so a segment bundle needs no key slot of its own (its header carries only the pair tag). One authenticated, forward-secret seal (the manifest) therefore protects the whole file, instead of one prekey per segment (review M-R2).
- *The receiver pulls.* Bulk segments are never listed in `SUMMARY`. The manifest (small, class 0 to 2) arrives first and creates a card in the thread. When the user taps Download (or the automatic limit allows it) the receiver sends `WANT` for the segment IDs it learned from the authenticated manifest, with a synthesized admission entry (class 4, the tag of the pair, the manifest's expiry, the chunk count from the manifest). The sender serves them to the authenticated recipient only. This makes the receiver the owner of its airtime and storage (the Knit model), removes the "unsolicited 60 MB" abuse and makes resume natural: chunk bitmaps persist per segment, so an interrupted download continues from the next missing chunk. A WANT for an unlisted bundle is honoured only when the bundle is one of the sender's own bundles addressed to that authenticated peer. A receiver can stop a download with the new `CANCEL` message (the sender abandons that served bundle at the next chunk boundary) and ask again later with a new WANT; chunks that arrive within 5 seconds after a CANCEL are dropped without counting as poisoned or duplicate.
- *Not carried.* Class 4 bundles are refused by carriers (phase 3). A big file therefore needs the two phones to meet; the thread says "Waiting for <name> to be nearby" and shows the size. Files of up to about 1 MB (inline) can travel through couriers. This is deliberate: a 64 MB file cannot fit the measured walk-past window (28 s leaves 0.7 to 1.5 MB), and carrier storage (64 MB) must not be consumed by one transfer.
- *Formats.* No allow-list for sending. Receiving: the file name is a display label only (NFC, path separators, control, bidirectional-format and zero-width characters removed, 96 bytes at most, never a path: files are stored under a local random ID, so a sender-chosen file ID can never collide with or overwrite another file; a repeated file ID with different segment IDs from the same sender is refused). The MIME type is the sender's claim, accepted only as `type/subtype`. The app *sniffs* the bytes itself (magic numbers for pictures, audio, video, PDF, ZIP/APK/JAR, DEX, ELF, PE, scripts with a `#!` line) and the sniffed type overrides the claim everywhere: for the icon, for the preview, and for the type handed to another app. "Open" exists only for a small allow-list of safe sniffed types (pictures, audio, video, PDF, plain text); everything else, and every installable or executable type, has only "Save to Downloads" behind a warning. Open hands the bytes to the system through a `ContentProvider` that is not exported and gives each external app a one-time read grant for that file, revoked when the thread is left or after 5 minutes; it serves through `StorageManager.openProxyFileDescriptor`, which decrypts on demand, caps open descriptors at 4 and wipes its block cache on release, so no plaintext file is created in our storage. The other app can of course copy the bytes it is shown; the guarantee is about our storage. Save copies into the user's chosen location through the Storage Access Framework (outside our encryption, the user's decision). Nothing is parsed, executed or installed automatically.
- *Pictures and thumbnails.* A zero-click decode of attacker-chosen pixels is the classic messenger attack surface, and a size or heap check does not contain a native decoder bug (finding 10). So **nothing is decoded automatically**: a received picture or file shows a generic type icon, its name and size, and a picture is decoded only after the user taps it, from the sniffed header size (header limit 40 megapixels), down-sampled to the display, never at full size, in a service with `isolatedProcess` and no permissions where the platform guarantees one and otherwise in-process with the same bounds (the user chose to open it). The manifest still carries a JPEG thumbnail (at most 16 KiB, header of at most 320 x 320 px) so that automatic previews can be switched on later: `test_mv2_auto_thumbnails` (Experimental, off) may turn on only after an isolated, resource-limited, time-limited decoder is proven on every reference phone (T-M9); until then the thumbnail bytes are carried and never decoded. Hint fields (width, height, duration) never size an allocation.
- *Photos on send.* The photo path re-encodes: EXIF orientation applied first (`BitmapFactory` ignores it), transparency flattened onto white, longest edge 1600 px, JPEG q80, every metadata segment gone, plus a 320 px thumbnail (carried in the manifest, shown only when automatic previews are on). "Original" keeps the pixels: for a JPEG a small segment filter rewrites the file with every APP and comment segment removed (EXIF including its embedded thumbnail, MakerNote, XMP, ICC kept only if requested) and a minimal orientation tag added; for PNG, WebP, HEIC and other formats the bytes go unchanged and the sheet says they may carry hidden data. Video: a lossless remux with `MediaExtractor` and `MediaMuxer` that copies the audio and video tracks and drops the location and device metadata atoms (no transcoding; a compressor is a later option, Q-M10); other files are sent as they are, with the size and the expected time on the learned link rate shown before sending. A video that the platform cannot remux is sent as a file with the warning.
- *Voice notes.* Unchanged from v1 (AAC in MP4, 2 minutes), as an inline attachment with its duration in the manifest.
- *Auto-download.* Inline attachments always arrive with the message. For segmented files: Never, Up to 8 MB (default, Knit's in-mesh tier) or Up to 64 MB, only from accepted contacts, only when no call or stream is running, and only while the contact's share of the vault (25%, M7) has room; anything larger waits for a tap.
- *Size limit.* 64,000,000 bytes (62 segments). The limit follows storage, quota and the time table below, not a Bluetooth maximum: it is a policy number to be revisited with measured goodput (and a Wi-Fi bearer, which would lift it).

**M4. Delivery states and receipts.** Own message states: *Waiting* (queued, nobody to give it to; clock), *Sending n %* (a transfer is running), *Delivered* (the contact's phone holds and has opened the message: two checks; for a file, the card, not the bytes), *Downloaded* (a file whose segments the contact has fully received and verified; shown as "Delivered, downloaded" and the only state after which the sender's sealed segments are deleted), *Read* (filled checks; only with mutual read receipts), *Not sent* (lifetime over, damaged or too many failed attempts; with a Retry that makes a new bundle), and, from phase 3, *Handed on* (one hollow check: a courier holds it, which never counts as delivery, the Meshtastic lesson). Delivered, Downloaded and Read are independent facts, each kept as its own flag per message and set at most once (finding 18): receipts are deduplicated by (bundle, status), so an earlier receipt never suppresses a later one, and a receipt that arrives after the state became *Not sent* still sets its flag; the thread shows the highest applicable state. Delivered comes from the in-session `DELIVERED_LOCAL` when the contact is adjacent, or from a receipt bundle (status 1) when it travelled through carriers; Downloaded is receipt status 3; Read is status 2. Receipts are never answered by another receipt, they batch up to 64 IDs (sent at once in a chat session, batched with a random delay only on the carried path), and they never remove a carried copy by themselves (the recipient's adjacent carrier is told by `DELIVERED_LOCAL`, hop-local, as in Design A). Receipts reveal a coarse hour only. Read receipts are a switch that is off by default and symmetric: a sender asks for one only when its own switch is on, and a receiver answers only when its own is on, so turning it off is invisible and costs you seeing theirs (Signal's rule).

**M5. Ordering and duplicates.** The displayed time of a message is its sender time clamped to `[arrival - 14 days, arrival]`, ties broken by bundle ID, and the sender time must fall inside the created hour of the (carrier-visible) bundle header, so neither a future nor a past clock can place a message out of its real window. A bundle ID is content addressed and a tombstone stays until expiry plus 3 hours (Design A), so redelivery never makes a second bubble; a retry from the user's Retry button is a new message by design. The sender never re-seals a message that was queued, so the same bytes are offered to every carrier (Knit's "sealed once, re-sent verbatim"), except that an authenticated key-gone signal (M9) re-seals it once under the same message ID, so a replacement for an already opened message is dropped.

**M6. The thread link ("chat session").** An exchange today ends when both phones have nothing more to say. When a thread is on screen, both phones have the feature on, the contact is adjacent and neither side runs a call or stream, the exchange stays open as a *chat session*: either side may send a further `SUMMARY` with the new bundle (text arrives in about a second on a healthy link), a `PING` every 20 seconds keeps the connection alive (60 seconds without any record ends it; a PING never counts as transfer progress, M12a), it ends when the thread is left for 2 minutes or after 30 minutes (then re-opens if the thread is still open), and a call or stream ends it within one chunk exactly as today. If both phones have the thread open and each tries to connect, the phone with the lower node ID is the initiator and the other accepts. Starting a call from the open thread closes the session first (the call reuses the same link and endpoint), so the call-setup time with an idle session must equal the control arm (T-M3, T-M14). The responder's 60-second cooldown and 10-per-hour cap stay for unknown phones but are relaxed to 5 seconds and 60 per hour for an accepted contact whose `HELLO` set the chat flag; this means the responder must read `HELLO` before it applies the cooldown (today it checks first). What starts a session is unchanged and honest: a thread open on a phone that discovers the contact (the tab that lists people nearby already scans while it is visible), or the contact's phone scanning and finding this phone reachable. Receiving while the app is in the background is *not* promised in phase 1; it is measured first, with the v1 code and its DEBUG controls, in phase 0 (T-M8) and decides whether phase 3 adds an opt-in background check.

State-machine changes that this needs in the v1 link code (`StoreForwardLink.kt`), each with a JVM test (review M-R8): `onSummary` accepts repeated SUMMARY messages in a chat session instead of failing after the first (`peerSummaryDone`); DONE is held while the session is a chat session and a simultaneous `DONE(IDLE)` with a late SUMMARY is defined (the late SUMMARY is ignored and its bundles wait for the next exchange); the `offered` map is extended as new bundles are created; a bundle may be WANTed again after it was served and finished or after CANCEL (the "repeated WANT" violation then applies only to a WANT for a bundle whose job is still running); the 1,024 SUMMARY-entry limit is per 1,024 *bundles offered*, counted over a window of 30 minutes in a chat session, not per exchange; the tag table is refreshed when a card is accepted mid-session; the connection watchdog is 60 seconds in a chat session while the transfer-progress deadline stays 10 seconds per chunk (120 s total per non-chat exchange); the writer takes work from a priority structure (below) instead of FIFO.

**M7. Storage and retention.** The sealed archive (96 MB cap, unchanged mechanism) keeps only text, listing records and manifests *without* inline data. All file bytes, including inline attachments, go to a *file vault*: one random 256-bit key per file, kept in the file's sealed meta record, and the bytes in 4 KiB AES-GCM blocks (nonce = block counter, AAD = local file ID, block index and a final-block flag, so truncation is detected and a segment can be written at its offset). Default vault cap 1 GiB with a visible "Storage used by messages" row and "Free up space"; no single contact may hold more than 25% of it (the receiver stops asking, and refuses inline attachments with `REFUSE quota`, when that share is full), so one contact cannot starve the others (review M-R11). Retention is independent of delivery lifetime: *delivery lifetime* is how long the network keeps trying (text and inline 3 days, segmented files 14 days; 1, 3, 7 and 14 days remain selectable under Advanced); *retention* is how long a delivered message stays on the phone (default until deleted; an optional per-conversation disappearing timer from 1 hour to 4 weeks, sent inside the message and counted from the first delivery acknowledgement on the sender and from reading on the receiving phone, the Briar rule). A timer shorter than the receiver's floor (1 hour) is raised to the floor, and the thread shows a notice "Messages in this chat disappear after reading" with a Keep action per message, so a contact cannot silently impose "vanish in a second". Approved partial downloads are exempt from the 24-hour stalled-partial eviction (they expire with the message's lifetime). Own outgoing queue: 512 MiB per contact and 1 GiB overall including sealed segments (the segments of a downloaded file are deleted at once). Waiting messages per contact: 100. Sealed bytes are immutable (finding 11): a vault block is written once, a crash retry rewrites it only with identical bytes or uses a new vault file with a fresh key, sealed bundles are retransmitted verbatim, and anything that must change gets fresh keys. Physical accounting and global ingress caps are in M12a. All of it lives under `noBackupFilesDir/store-forward`, is excluded from backup and removed by the emergency wipe, as today.

**M7a. Small before large.** Inside one exchange the writer always serves the smallest waiting class first and interleaves at chunk granularity (Serval's size lanes): a text or receipt written while a 10 MB segment is streaming goes out before the next chunk of the segment, and a segment is never queued ahead of a message. This also bounds the time a live call must wait to preempt, as today. It replaces the FIFO job queue of v1 with a priority structure keyed by (class, remaining bytes).

**M8. Admission tiers.** The v1 numbers (8 MB per exchange, 20 MB per neighbour per day, 200 bundles per day, 50 per sender per day) were written for strangers. v2 keeps them for an adjacent phone that is *not* an accepted contact (the carrier tier of phase 3) and adds a contact tier: 128 MiB per exchange and 512 MiB per day in each direction, 200 messages per contact per day, and a separate allowance of 1,000 receipts per contact per day (receipts and read receipts are bundles but must not eat the message allowance; a bulk segment counts bytes, not messages). A contact is still limited by the receiver's own pull and by its 25% vault share: an attacker contact cannot make the phone store more than it asked for, except inline attachments of up to 1 MB, which are bounded by the 200-message allowance and the vault share.

**M9. Forward secrecy.** The v1 slot derives both keys from the recipient's long-term mailbox key `R`; a later compromise of `R` opens every retained bundle and its sender. Options considered (revised after the second review, finding 20: the first version of this table rejected the Double Ratchet because it "needs ordered, lossless delivery", which is wrong: the Double Ratchet specification supports lost and out-of-order messages by storing bounded skipped message keys):

| Option | What it gives | Cost and risk |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A. One-time and rotating prekeys, one prekey seal per message | Each message (or file manifest) is sealed to a prekey the recipient hands to that one contact in advance and deletes after opening; the long-term keys only authenticate the sender (deniable). With no one-time key left the sender uses a rotating prekey. Never the static key | A continuous dependency on material from the peer: batches, refills, a Keystore alias budget, spent-ID tracking, a `PREKEY_GONE` re-seal path; a contact not met for 21 days cannot be sent a forward-secret message. About 1.6 k lines |
| **B. Prekey bootstrap once, then Double Ratchet sessions with header encryption (recommended since the second review)** | Per-message forward secrecy (a message key is deleted on use), healing after compromise at every reply (post-compromise security), loss and reordering tolerated by skipped keys. After the bootstrap there is no prekey traffic at all: a phone can send for weeks to a contact it has not met since, because the sending chain needs nothing from the peer. Replays die with the deleted key | Session state (root key, chain keys, header keys, counters, skipped keys) that must commit atomically with the message store; skipped-key bookkeeping; header encryption so carriers cannot link ratchet public keys; simultaneous initiation; a large test surface and no published P-256 vectors (they are generated with a reference script and cross-checked against a second implementation). About 1.0 k (bootstrap, shared with A) plus 1.8 k lines |
| C. Knit-style epoch ratchet | Cheap, loss tolerant, FS per epoch (200 sends or 24 h) | Epoch-granular only, a second state machine next to the bundle seal |
| D. Briar-style per-period transport keys | Simple and robust; keys ratchet one way and old periods are deleted | Shared secret agreed in person for every contact; granularity is a whole period (at least the 14-day maximum lifetime); a lost sync cannot recover without meeting again; Briar itself protects the wire, not stored messages |
| E. qaul-style session rotation (7 days or 2^20 messages) | Small change | Coarse; it protects less than A or B for retained bundles |
| F. v1 as it is, disclosed | No work | Violates the Q10 recommendation before carriers ship |

*A and B side by side, on this app's constraints.*

| Criterion | A: prekey per message | B: bootstrap plus Double Ratchet |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sending to a contact not met for 3 weeks | Blocked ("Waiting to exchange keys") once the keys' 21-day use window ended | Works; the chain continues without the peer |
| Material needed from the peer | Continuous (20 one-time keys per contact, refills, a rotating key every 7 days) | One bootstrap per direction: the rotating signed prekey plus a few one-time keys |
| Forward secrecy | Per message with a one-time key; up to about 36 days of exposure with the rotating fallback | Per message; skipped keys are the only retained secrets and are purged at the expiry of the messages they could open (at most 14 days plus 2 hours) |
| After a past compromise | Heals as new prekeys are issued and old ones expire | Heals at the next reply (a new DH step); a one-way sender stays on one chain |
| Loss, reordering, duplicates through carriers | Each bundle is independent; duplicates by bundle ID | Skipped-key window (256 per contact); duplicates by bundle ID, and a used key is gone |
| Failure modes found by review | Replay of batches, re-seal duplicates, consumption crashes, exhaustion, Keystore load | State commit on crash, skipped-key flooding (bounded), simultaneous start, a corrupted state needing a visible session reset |
| Header visibility to carriers | Masked prekey ID | Ratchet public key hidden by header encryption (the receiver trial-decrypts at most 3 header keys per session) |
| Size | 1.6 k + 0.9 k tests, 7-8 agent-days | 2.8 k + 1.8 k tests, 15-18 agent-days in total (bootstrap 7-8, ratchet 8-10) |

*Verdict.* The recommendation changes from A to B, built in two steps because A's machinery is B's bootstrap: phase 2 delivers the prekey bootstrap (batches, slot version 4, storage rules below) and, with `test_mv2_ratchet` off, uses it as option A (a prekey seal per message); phase 2b adds the sessions (slot version 5) and `test_mv2_ratchet` on becomes the default after its tests. Both arms stay switchable. The reasons B wins are the 21-day blocking window (a real failure for the off-grid user whose contact is away for a month), the removal of the continuous prekey traffic and the Keystore alias load that produced most of the second review's findings, and post-compromise healing. B costs about 1.8 times as much and brings session-state risk; if the user prefers the smaller step, A alone satisfies the Q10 rule and remains correct. Neither option gives *metadata* forward secrecy: the tag is derived from the static pair secret `Kp`, so someone who later obtains `R` and `S` and holds captured bundles can match their daily tags to the pair and learn who talked on which day, even though the payloads and the sender field stay sealed (finding 5). That is disclosed in the user text; erasable ratcheted tag chains (see M10) are the Experimental route and decision Q-M11.

*What forward secrecy achieves, precisely* (review M-R5, finding 5). `k2` needs `ECDH(S, R)`, so a leaked prekey alone opens only the sender-identity part. Forward secrecy protects *captured or retained ciphertext* (what carriers and the sender's outbox hold) against a *later* compromise of the static keys and of the prekey or message key; it does not protect a seized, unlocked phone (the archive stays readable), a message while its key is still held, or the addressing metadata above. Compromise of `R` lets an attacker impersonate any sender toward that recipient (key-compromise impersonation, as in Noise X). The signature on a prekey batch only stops prekey substitution; it is a non-repudiable statement that the issuer gave these keys to this contact, a small deniability cost that is accepted.

*Rules that apply to the bootstrap (and to A), from the reviews.*
- *No downgrade (finding 1).* Each contact has a pinned minimum security version. Once a contact's phone has sent a version 4 or 5 bundle or a `BPK1`, this phone refuses any lower version from or to it (`REFUSE invalid`, visible in the thread as "Sent without forward secrecy was refused"). Version 2 and 3 bundles are read only on a direct link from a contact whose pin is still below 4; they are never carried and never re-offered to a third phone. Phase 3 carrier admission accepts only version 4 and 5 headers, and phase 3 does not ship until this pin exists.
- *Spent keys stay spent (finding 2).* Key IDs are serial numbers that the issuer never reuses (a persisted counter). The phone that receives a batch persists, per issuer, the set of IDs it has used (as ranges) and refuses a batch entry whose ID is in it or whose ID is already bound to a different key; an equal-generation batch is idempotent. A replayed signed batch therefore cannot resurrect a consumed key, also across crashes (the used set is written before the seal is used).
- *Consumption is one recoverable transaction (finding 4).* Opening a message does: (1) append a journal record "open-intent: bundle ID, logical message ID, key ID"; (2) store the message, the logical ID and the bundle tombstone; (3) delete the key; (4) mark the journal record done; only then is the delivery acknowledged. A restart replays unfinished records forward. A one-time key is *claimed* by the first bundle that opens with it; a second bundle naming a claimed key is refused as a replay even if the key still exists.
- *Budgets (finding 9).* At most 40 unused one-time keys outstanding per contact and 400 overall, counted from the issuer's own records (a peer's `prekeys held` claim only schedules a top-up, never raises a cap); at most 2 batches per hour per contact and 12 per hour overall, including the immediate batch after `PREKEY_GONE`; a slot that fails to open costs the sender (and the supplying neighbour) double the allowance of a good bundle, and more than 20 failed opens per hour from one neighbour ends the exchange.
- *Storage (finding 13).* Every prekey gets its own Keystore wrapping key on every API level (API 31 and later: the prekey itself is a Keystore EC key), deleted when the key is used or expires; the number of Keystore aliases is capped at 200 overall, and contacts beyond the cap are issued rotating keys only until aliases free up. Sub-batches of 5 are not used, because a rewritten file would stay decryptable by older copies while its wrapper survives. The alias creation time and limits are measured on the required phones in a spike before phase 2 starts (blocking). Erasure on flash is best effort and the user text says so; the emergency wipe enumerates and deletes every alias and file.
- *Lifetimes.* Every key carries a `use-until` time of issue + 21 days; the sender never seals to a key past it; the recipient deletes the private half 15 days after `use-until`, so no bundle that was legally sealed can outlive its key. A new rotating prekey is issued every 7 days; up to three are usable at once.
- *Re-seal after `PREKEY_GONE` (finding 3).* Every message has a random 16-byte *logical message ID* inside the sealed content (`BSM2`), and the receiver deduplicates on (authenticated sender, logical ID) as well as on the bundle ID, with the same tombstone lifetime, so a replacement bundle for an already opened message is dropped. A sender re-seals only on a refusal that comes from the authenticated adjacent recipient over a direct link, or on an end-to-end `RECEIPT` status 4 (`key gone`) sealed by the recipient; a hop-local refusal from a carrier never causes a re-seal.
