Pass reading: a dead anchor, when it happens (`relay_child_stall ... stage=hop`), is followed by `action=holdoff` lines instead of 133 dials, `relay_parent_fallback ... withdraw_offer` does not appear, and the A15s joins within about 30 s of the stall (`relay_child_stall ... result=ok`), with hop 2 units flowing. Control: `test_relay_stall_holdoff=false` on the same build should reproduce the withdrawal after a dead anchor. Preservation: S24/ASUS/M31 relay chains in video mode still form on the first dial, and `relay_child_stall result=ok handshakeMs` stays near 300 ms.

#### Audio chain: dead-anchor stalls counted apart from refusals (b14 music r1, 2026-10-13)

**What music r1 shows.** The hold-off worked (no stale 133 dials), but the M31 relay's stall guard caught dead-anchor links four times in a row: `relay_child_stall stage=hop silentMs=2004` at 20:45:04.2 and `2005` at 20:45:20.1 (15.9 s apart, outside the 10 s hold-off), then `relay_parent_fallback ... withdraw_offer | failures=2 | markMs=300000`; the mark expired at about 20:50:20 and two more stalls at 20:50:27.4 and 20:50:42.4 withdrew the offer again. The A15s joined nothing for 10 minutes. Four of four links drawing a dead anchor in one session is not the 16 percent per attempt seen in relayfix-0725a213; in this session something about the pair made every attempt dead, and r2 and r3 (same build) formed on the first dial. The guard did its job; the withdrawal policy is what made it a permanent failure.

**Why the 5-minute mark is wrong for stalls.** The mark exists for refusals (GATT status 133/62, the stack reporting HCI 0x3E): the child cannot be connected to, and handing it to another parent is right. A stall means the connection was made and the link layer then delivered nothing after the connection update; the next link redraws the anchor. Treating two of those like two refusals withdraws a reachable child for 5 minutes after 16 s.

**Fix (`test_relay_stall_separate`, default on).** `EstablishmentFailureTracker.onStall`: stalls (`RelayChildFailureKind.STALL`, classified first so a stalled attempt whose message names a 133 is still a stall) have their own list, need 3 within 2 minutes (a dead anchor at 16 percent, three in two minutes is rare in a healthy session) and mark the child for 30 s, escalating 60, 120, 300 s on consecutive marks without a success; a completed end-to-end handshake clears the level. Refusals keep 2 failures within 2 minutes and the 5-minute mark; stalls neither add to nor reset them. Hop-local (a per-child key on the relay, nothing is sent). Log: `relay_parent_fallback ... kind=stall` with `markMs` on a mark and `action=count_stall` for a counted stall that does not mark. `RelayStallSeparateTest` replays the four r1 stall times: the control rule marks at the second and fourth, the new rule marks never, and it covers the 30/60 s escalation, the window, the success reset, the refusal rule and the classifier.

**What this does not fix.** A session in which every link is dead stays dead; the child is simply retried (the child's route cooldown spaces the dials about 16 s apart) instead of locked out for 5 minutes, and after three stalls in two minutes the mark is 30 s, then 60 s, so the relay still stops spending the child's slot on a hopeless pair. The cause of a persistently dead pair is open: the A15s's last reception coincides with the 7.5 to 15 ms connection update in every dead link (b5, b13, b14), which points at that update on the MediaTek peripheral as much as at the Exynos child anchor; the next phone arm to try is the child link without the 15 ms update (priority left at its connection value) on this pair, together with an HCI snoop of the A15s.

**Rerun plan.** Chain ASUS source, M31 relay, A15s leaf, GATT, Music then Voice, frozen APK from this branch, A15s running at least 60 s before the chain starts, 10 min each, five runs per preset, plus `test_relay_stall_separate=false` once per preset. Pass reading: when a stall happens the log shows `count_stall` (and `holdoff` for sightings in the next 10 s) and the A15s joins on a later request within about 40 s (`relay_child_stall result=ok`); `withdraw_offer` appears only after three stalls inside two minutes and then with `kind=stall markMs<=30000`; the control reproduces `failures=2 markMs=300000`. Preservation: refusal cases (status 133 to a vanished child) still withdraw for 5 minutes after two failures; video relay chains unchanged.

#### The viewer's input overflow after a stalled-2M move (b1-a5a8f056 stalled2m d2, 2026-10-13)

**What happened.** The A15s wrote 7 kbps for 20 s on 2M, `stalled_one_way` moved the link to 1M at 21:42:01.98 with about 17 s of audio (`audioQueueAgeMaxMs=16652`) and 20 s of video (`videoQueueAgeNowMs=20556` at 21:42:03.8, 25.6 s by 08.8) queued, and the sender then wrote at link speed (48, 62, 78 ... 131 kbps). The viewer logged `GATT video receiver input queue overflowed` at 21:42:14.31, 12 s after the move, and nothing after it: the per-2-s status lines had already stopped at 21:41:50 (12 frames), no `GATT sender disconnected`, no route cooldown, no reconnect. The A15s kept writing and kept receiving the viewer's authenticated heartbeats every second until the end of the capture, so the sender never saw a problem.

**What overflowed.** The viewer's GATT input pipe (`GattBytePipe`), bounded at 512 chunks, a chunk being one GATT value. The audio backlog is the cause: the audio writer takes audio ahead of video, so the first thing the move released was 17 s of 20 ms Opus records (about 850 records of about 80 bytes, one or a few per GATT value), and the viewer's receive loop paces audio at real time by design (`OpusAudioDecoder.offer` applies back-pressure and plays late audio, never discards it; the Android buffer-pool counters in the log show the audio decoder busy for 15 s after the move). 850 small records against 512 chunks overflows after 10 to 12 s, which is the observed time. The decoder input queue (41 frames) is not the culprit: its offer does not block (it drops and asks for a keyframe), and `decoder 1/41` stayed idle.

**Why there was no recovery.** `GattBytePipe` closed itself on overflow, but nothing ended the link: the GATT connection stayed up, the viewer's heartbeat thread kept answering, and the receive loop stayed in its audio back-pressure (the exception that would end it, `Audio decoder stopped consuming for 3000 ms`, only fires when the decoder stops, not when it merely plays slowly). The sender's only liveness test is those heartbeats. So one phone wrote into a dead input and the other reported it healthy until the capture ended: a zombie session. That is a bug whatever the flood was.

**Fixes** (both default on, control arms `=false`).
1. `test_gatt_input_failure_ends_link`: a failing input pipe (overflow, bad chunk header, reordering) cancels the connection, so the sender sees the loss and the existing redial and re-request path recovers. The pipe is also bounded by bytes first (1 MiB, about 16 s of a 500 kbps stream) with a 4,096-chunk bound, so a backlog of small records no longer overflows after 40 KB.
2. `test_phy_move_flood_guard`: at a PHY move on a live stream, media queued longer than 8 s (four times the largest playout) is dropped once: video codec-safely (resume at a queued keyframe still inside the playout, else wait for a requested one), audio older than the viewer's playout (a sender gap to the viewer, which realigns on the timestamp jump; the Opus decoder already handles that case). This is a correctness bound, not the preference the Experimental `test_phy_move_skip_backlog` (4 s video, audio kept) tests: the user direction "late audio is played, never discarded" is about audio that is late within the viewer's window; 17 s of audio at a 2 s maximum playout cannot be played in sync, and writing it at link speed into a receive loop that plays it at real time takes the session down. The guard changes nothing below 8 s, so the F case (6.7 s) and the one-PDU moves seen so far are untouched. Calls and audio-only live never use it.
`PhyMoveSkipTest` replays the queue (850 audio records over 17 s: 750 removed, the newest 2 s kept; the guard thresholds), `GattBytePipeTest` replays the 850 small records against the default pipe (no overflow; the old 512 would overflow) and the byte bound.

**Not fixed / open.** (a) The exact blocking point of the viewer's receive loop after the overflow is inferred (the log shows no exception and the audio decoder counters running); the link-end fix does not depend on it. (b) A viewer in the same state against an older sender still sees the 17 s burst; the byte bound keeps it from overflowing but it then plays the late audio. (c) The 20 s detection (3 windows after warm-up) is what lets the backlog grow; a faster detector for a 7 kbps writer is a separate change.

**Rerun plan.** Same pair and conditions as `stalled2m` (A15s source, ASUS viewer, memory reset, 90 s), frozen APK from this branch, five runs plus one run each with `test_phy_move_flood_guard=false` and with `test_gatt_input_failure_ends_link=false`. Pass reading: after `stalled_one_way` the log shows `phy_move_skip ... kind=flood_guard | skippedAudio=` (hundreds) with `awaitKeyFrame` and a keyframe request; the viewer shows no `input queue overflowed`, resumes video within about 10 s of the move, and its status lines continue to the end. Control (guard off): the byte bound alone keeps the pipe alive, so expect late audio and late video but no zombie; with the link-end fix also off the original zombie should reproduce. Preservation: S24/M31 and ASUS/M31 streams show no `phy_move_skip`; the 6.7 s F case shows none (below 8 s).

#### Audio chain: the M31's dials to the A15s fail with 0x3E and the 5-minute refusal mark (b5-audio-chain-cb548fc5 music r1 and r3, 2026-10-13)

**What each failed dial is.** From the M31's stack at 22:21:38.8 to 39.15 (r1): the A15s scan result for the address arrives at 38.756 (rssi -51), `connect()` at 38.818, `GATT_Connect` to the accept list at 38.842, the link is created (handle 0x00a0), and 270 ms later `Failed to read remote features status:Connection Failed Establishment` and `bta_gattc_open_fail ... GATT_ERROR(133)`. That is HCI 0x3E: the M31's controller sent the connection request and the peripheral never answered the first anchor points, six connection events in a row. On the A15s no connection is ever reported (`onServerConnectionState` absent). So it is not a stale or outdated address (the address was scanned 12 ms before the dial, from an advertising set 0.5 to 4 s old), not an A15s refusal in the application sense, and not a collision with an A15s dial (the A15s makes none; its scanner stopped at 22:21:37.36, before it started advertising at 38.28). One PHY detail: the retries were requested on 1M after the 2M ones, which changes nothing, because connection establishment always runs on the advertising PHY (the same observation as relayfix mesh4ab-52681dbf loop14b-1 0006, where an M31 relay as central logged ten Connection Complete events each followed 270 ms later by 0x3E).

**Per-session sticky, or a high per-attempt failure rate?** r1: eight of eight dials failed with 0x3E: four at 22:21:38.8 to 42.4 and, after the 300 s mark, four more at 22:26:46.5 to 50.1 (the same handle 0x00a0 each time). r3: 16 0x3E events and two withdrawals (22:45:11 and 22:50:21). r2: the first round failed twice with 0x3E (22:33:23.3 and 24.3) and the third attempt, one second later, connected (handshake 428 ms, `relay_child_stall result=ok`), so the failure is not an absolute refusal; voice and the control had none. The child changed its address every 15 to 30 s in between (40:C5.. then 6F:88.., 58:0B.., 58:0D..), so the address is not the variable. What stays constant is the M31's parent link (ASUS to M31, 15 ms interval, anchor fixed for the session) and the A15s's controller state; with the data at hand (no HCI snoop of the M31 or the A15s) a bad relative phase between the new connection's first anchor and the parent's events fits "most attempts fail in some sessions, and r1 and r3 never got a good one inside the 2-failure, 3-second budget", but it is not proven. The b14 dead-anchor links (A15s last reception at the 15 ms update, four of four in one session) are the same kind of session-level effect one step later in the life of the link. The next measurement is an HCI snoop of the M31 and the A15s in a failing session.

**Is the 5-minute mark the right reaction?** No, when the child keeps asking and has no other parent. The mark's purpose (RelayEstablishFallback) is to make the child choose another parent; here the M31 is the only relay and the requested chain forbids the direct source. The child kept requesting every 15 to 20 s (92 sightings skipped by `skip_connect` in r1), so the withdrawal only removed the relay's chance to retry, and the relay's two failures were spent within 3 s because the child, advertising in burst mode, was rediscovered every second (dial, 133, 1M setup, dial, 133, mark).

**Fix (`test_relay_refusal_escalation`, default on, control arm `=false`).** (1) Refusal marks escalate 30, 60, 120, 300 s on consecutive marks (the `EstablishmentFailureTracker` schedule the Experimental establishment budget already uses), cleared by a successful link, instead of a flat 5 minutes. (2) A refusal round holds the next dial to that child off for 10 s (`RelayStallHoldoff`), so the two failures that mark a child are at least 10 s apart and a transient or phase-dependent failure gets a second chance with a different timing. Hop-local, nothing sent. `RelayRefusalEscalationTest` replays the r1 pattern (a sighting every second): the control arm spends both failures at 0 and 1 s and withdraws for 300 s (4 dials in 10 minutes); the new arm dials at 0 and 10 s, marks 30 s, dials again at 40 and 50 s, marks 60 s, then 120 s and 300 s (about 10 dials), and a success resets the level.

**Not fixed.** If the cause is a session-level phase between the M31's parent link and a new central link, retries only help when the phase moves; they cost two 1-second dials per 30 to 300 s. The way to move it from the app is a connection update on the parent link, which only the ASUS (central) can start. An HCI snoop of the M31 in a failing session should show the connection events around the failed 0x3E and settle it.

**Rerun plan.** Chain ASUS source, M31 relay, A15s leaf, GATT, Music (the preset that failed) and Voice, frozen APK from this branch, A15s running at least 90 s before the chain starts (as in b5), 10 minutes each, five runs per preset, one run each with `test_relay_refusal_escalation=false`, and the HCI snoop of the M31 and the A15s pulled for any run with a 0x3E. Pass reading: a run that fails its first two dials shows `holdoff` lines between them (rounds at least 10 s apart), `withdraw_offer ... kind=refusal markMs<=30000`, and the A15s joins on a later dial within about 2 minutes (`relay_child_stall result=ok`) in sessions where the failure is transient; in a session that stays dead the log shows dial pairs at about 0, 40, 110, 240 s instead of one pair per 5 minutes. Control: `=false` reproduces `markMs=300000` after two dials 1 to 3 s apart. Preservation: the loop14b M31 case (a child with another parent) still falls back within the first mark; video relay chains unchanged.

#### Change impact (for the inventory at implementation)

Phase 1 touches the store-and-forward node, store, link and crypto, the Messages UI, the Contacts tab and notifications, and adds a `ContentProvider`. It changes no call or stream stage. Per AGENTS, cases that use the changed link stages with the feature on are new; with it off, call and stream cases are not affected by phase 1 (the preservation check T-M14 proves it). The v1 messages cases (if any) are NEEDS RETEST on the first build that contains phase 1, because the content format, the bundle version and the retention behaviour change. This design changes these requirements: v1's "archive expires with the bundle" (retired: retention is separate), the 2 MiB message limit (kept per bundle, lifted per attachment), the per-exchange 8 MB (kept for non-contacts).

#### Decided questions (answered 2026-10-12: the recommendation, first column of options, was chosen for every row)

| Question | Recommendation | Alternatives |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Q-M1 Where do conversations live? | B: contact rows become conversation rows in the Contacts tab, no new tab, move to a Chats tab (A) if messaging becomes a main use | A fifth tab "Chats"; merge into Calls; a header inbox icon |
| Q-M2 Largest file and automatic download | 64 MB maximum, automatic download up to 8 MB from accepted contacts, tap above that | 8 MB maximum like Knit's in-mesh tier, with larger files waiting for a Wi-Fi bearer; 25 MB; no maximum except storage |
| Q-M3 Read receipts | Off by default, symmetric (both must turn them on) | On for verified contacts; always on |
| Q-M4 How long do messages stay on the phone? | Until deleted, with an optional disappearing timer per conversation (Briar's start rules) | Expire with the delivery lifetime (today); fixed 7 or 30 days |
| Q-M5 Forward secrecy | **Changed from A to B after the second review:** a prekey bootstrap, then Double Ratchet sessions with header encryption and bounded skipped keys (phase 2, then 2b), before carriers; the prekey-per-message option A stays as the smaller, switchable step and as B's bootstrap | A alone (cheaper, but a contact not met for 21 days cannot be sent a forward-secret message); Knit-style epoch ratchet; Briar-style periods; disclose only |
| Q-M6 Photos | Re-encode to 1600 px and q80 by default, "Original" as a choice on the sheet | Always original; bitchat's 448 px; ask every time |
| Q-M7 Dangerous file types | Any type can be sent; Open exists only for sniffed pictures, audio, video, PDF and text, everything else is Save-only behind a warning | Refuse to receive them (Knit); no special handling |
| Q-M8 Locked phone | Accept that messages are stored while locked and readable when the app is unlocked, document it; research an asymmetric at-rest scheme later | Require unlock to receive (blocks receiving in a pocket); user-authentication key (same problem) |
| Q-M9 Groups | Design after phase 2, with Design B's roster; one key slot per bundle until then, and a group bundle needs a signed logical ID and a recipient-set commitment | Never; pairwise fan-out per member |
| Q-M10 Video compression | Later option (a MediaCodec compressor in 720p about 1 Mbps) once measured on the weak devices | Never; ship in phase 1 |
| Q-M11 Metadata forward secrecy | Disclose that the day tag derives from the static pair secret, so a later key compromise reveals which pairs talked on which day from captured bundles; offer erasable per-message tag chains as an Experimental switch | Make erasable tag chains mandatory before carriers (resynchronisation cost, finding 17); accept silently (rejected) |
| Q-M12 Automatic picture previews | A generic icon, decoding only after a tap; offer "Show previews automatically" only once decoding in a separate process is proven on every reference phone | Decode thumbnails automatically in the app process (usual in messengers, with a zero-click attack surface); never decode |
| Q-M13 A peer on an older app (user, 2026-10-12) | Keep the composer enabled: messages wait in the thread and the banner says "Their app is older"; nothing is blocked | Disable the composer until the peer updates (no unsendable messages, but no way to leave a note for a slow updater) |

### Design B: group calls (3-6 people) and group push-to-talk

**Design review required.** Design only; default off (`test_group_call`). Existing 1:1 calls, streams, BMH2, BMS1, BME1
and the static BGK1 group-key harness stay unchanged.

**Airtime.** A 20 ms Opus frame at 32 kbps costs about 64 kbps on the link after packet, BME1 and BMS1 overhead (about
50 kbps with the existing 40 ms audio-pair format). Planning uses a = 50 kbps per audio stream and v = 120 kbps per
low-rate video stream. Opus DTX cuts a silent talker to near zero, so real load scales with how many people speak; the
worst case assumes everyone sends. A hub's total radio airtime is the limit, not one link (an M31 GATT two-link relay
tops out at 625 kbps, and in a fan-out test leaves dropped to 0.9-7.6 FPS).

| Topology | Busiest phone, audio (N = 3 / 4 / 6) | Links per phone | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Mixing star** (hub decodes, mixes "everyone but you", re-encodes) | 2(N-1)a = **200 / 300 / 500 kbps** | hub N-1, leaf 1 | Small CPU cost; adds about 40-60 ms and one transcode |
| Forwarding star | N(N-1)a = 300 / 600 / **1500 kbps** | hub N-1, leaf 1 | Above the M31-class ceiling at N of 4 or more |
| Full mesh | 2(N-1)a per phone | N-1 (5 at N = 6) | 15 links to keep alive; weak stacks fail on the second or third link |
| Mixing relay tree | 2(c+1)a per node | parent plus c children | Hop-local, extends range; each hop adds about 60-100 ms and a transcode |
| PTT forwarding star (one talker) | at most (N-1)a = 100 / 150 / 250 kbps | as star | No mixing while one person holds the floor |
| Active-speaker video via star | +Nv = 360 / 480 / 720 kbps | as star | One video at a time; gallery video (3.6 Mbps at N = 6) is impossible |

**Recommendation: a mixing star, built so a star is a depth-1 mixing tree.** Hub load grows linearly, leaves need one
link (legacy stacks fail on extra links), and existing hop-local liveness and admission apply unchanged. A leaf hosting
its own children can reuse the mixer later, after the star is measured. Video is active-speaker only, N of 4 or fewer,
admitted only when the hub's measured capacity covers audio plus Nv, forwarded and never mixed.

- **Audio.** Leaves send Opus with DTX over per-edge endpoint sessions; the hub keeps one jitter buffer per leaf, mixes
  the three loudest active streams and encodes one mix-minus per leaf; with exactly one active stream it forwards that
  packet unchanged (re-sealed per edge). Same-room echo is the main acoustic risk: use the VOICE_COMMUNICATION source
  with platform echo cancellation, earpiece or headset by default and a visible speaker toggle.
- **Floor control (the hub arbitrates).** First request wins; a talker captures into a local buffer of at most 400 ms;
  maximum talk time 60 s; a lost holder frees the floor.
- **Keys.** Per-edge endpoint sessions mean removing a member immediately cuts access to new media. A group epoch key
  `BGK2` (increasing epoch, roster hash, controller and succession list, expiry, controller signature) is wrapped per
  member; a new epoch is issued on every join and leave, the old one accepted for 2 s, and a child that does not
  acknowledge within 2 s is closed. The hub hears all audio and can misattribute who is speaking in a mix.
- **When the host leaves.** The signed roster names a succession list (deputy first, by measured capacity, battery and
  signal). A planned handover or an unplanned loss moves leaves to the deputy with fresh authentication and a new epoch
  (target at most 10 s of silence, to be measured); a partition is not merged automatically in v1.
- **Wire changes (all new, all fail closed):** a discovery version-3 group kind with a keyed 5-minute token; BME1 type
  9 `GROUP_CONTROL` (`BGC1`); BME1 type 7 payload `BGK2`; a `BTV3` header flag for mixed audio plus an origin index for
  passthrough PTT; a group-invite reason on the call invite.

Open design questions (recommendation first): topology (mixing star; or forwarding star, full mesh, relay tree); group
video (active speaker only, N of 4 or fewer; or audio only); PTT floor (first come first served; or a leader who can
interrupt, a next-speaker queue); host leaves (a deputy takes over with a new key; or the call ends); rekey on join as
well as leave; speaker misattribution (accept and disclose in the UI; or signed talk-spurts); relay trees for range
(after the star is measured); who adds members (the hub admits from a QR roster; or any member, a fixed roster).

Risks: hub aggregate airtime over N-1 links is unmeasured; legacy stacks (M31 GATT 625 kbps relay ceiling, A15s CoC
slot exhaustion, S22 second-link timeouts) limit who can host; same-room echo; tandem-transcode quality in trees; hub
battery drain (rotate via succession); a mouth-to-ear target of at most 400 ms, to be measured physically; split-brain
and rekey storms under churn. Prototype order: (1) an audio-only mixing star, N = 3 over GATT, measuring hub
throughput, per-leaf media age, mouth-to-ear latency and hub CPU and battery; (2) N of 4-6 with DTX on and off; (3) PTT
passthrough with the floor state machine; (4) `BGK2` rekey on join and leave with JVM tests. Video and trees come after.

### Design C: relayed private calls and multi-hop discovery

**Design review required.** Default off (`test_relayed_calls`, Settings > Advanced "Experimental: relayed calls"). The
design was reviewed independently on 2026-10-11 and builds on the earlier draft (R14-R22) and on these design
decisions: hop-local reading accepted, relay helping on by default while the app runs once the feature is enabled,
consent asked once and preselected, a radius of 2 relays. Wire rules: PROTOCOL.md "Experimental relayed calls and
multi-hop discovery". With the switch off, every byte on air and on the link is unchanged.

**Goal.** A caller A reaches a call-ready phone D that is one or two relay phones away, and an ordinary phone relays
calls in the background without being told which call to take. Before this, only DEBUG relays existed (a launch extra
names the callee).

- **Roles and reach table.** Target D (call-ready, consents), relay helper R (opted in, call endpoint idle), caller A.
  Each helper keeps a table of targets fed only by adverts it scanned itself: D's own call offer gives a cost-1 entry, a
  neighbour's reach-page entry gives cost + 1, up to the cap (default 2, DEBUG `test_relayed_calls_max_relays`, at most
  4). Reach pages are a second legacy non-connectable advertising set (one page per second).
- **On-demand setup.** A connects to R1's call listener, authenticates the hop, and sends `SETUP(target, budget,
  remaining time, setup ID)` as the first hop record (hop type 12). R1 checks its table, connects to the next hop
  itself and regenerates the same message downstream; `READY` and `FAIL` return one hop at a time. After READY the
  existing opaque call relay copies the ordered end-to-end records, and A runs the ordinary end-to-end handshake
  through the chain.
- **Hop-local, with stated stretch.** A phone re-advertises only what its direct neighbours told it, and setup is
  regenerated at every hop with a new ID, smaller budget and shorter deadline. Reachability spreads by repeated
  re-advertisement (a distance vector with an age), and the target's identifier travels the chain inside the SETUP, so
  each relay on the path learns its adjacent links, the target and (from the signed end-to-end hello it copies) both
  endpoint identities. A passive observer near a relay learns that D is within N hops of it.
- **Identity and abuse limits.** A relay cannot read or forge content (end-to-end keys). A requires the end-to-end
  peer to match the target it selected (the full fingerprint for a verified contact); each helper link identity must
  equal the helper offer that was selected; the last relay accepts only the target or another relay as its child.
  Absolute deadlines from READY (10 s end-to-end handshake, 20 s Invite) and a 60 s ringing cap stop a caller from
  pinning helpers.
- **Rotating IDs.** With both experiments on, relaying uses role-0 rotating IDs only (reach pages version 6, `SETUP`
  message 4, every hop checked with `BDO1`/`BDX1`); DEBUG `test_relayed_rotating=false` restores the earlier exclusion.
- **Battery and pre-emption.** Helping runs only while the phone is call-ready and none of these hold: battery below
  20% and not charging, Battery Saver, severe thermal status, Bluetooth off, the phone's own exchange. A relayed call
  already running is not cut by a pause. Starting or answering the phone's own call or stream ends any relay in
  progress. The foreground notification reads "Helping a call nearby" with a Stop action. Idle scanning cost per hour is
  a gate to measure on each reference phone before any default is considered.
- **Failure and fallback.** A shows the route ("Nearby", or "Via 2 phones, heard 8 s ago, unconfirmed"), picks the
  fewest relays then the freshest, and skips a failed pair for 30 s. A chain that breaks mid-call ends the call;
  automatic re-routing is future work. Direct fallback is a failed requested chain: `test_relayed_calls_require=<n>`
  accepts only a chain of exactly n relays. One bearer end to end; only LE transports are supported on demand. iPhones
  can neither help nor be relayed targets until the compact amendment.
- **Deferred:** automatic candidate iteration inside one attempt, the embedded single-entry carrier for phones without a free advertising set, Classic on-demand relays, mid-call re-routing, extended-advertising reach lists and an iPhone caller. Phone-probe history: [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md) "Relayed calls: first phone probe".
- **Phone verification queue** (one helper at a time, `test_relayed_calls=true` on every participant): run
  `python -B tools/dex_api_scan.py` on the APK first; then one helper on GATT with `test_relayed_calls_require=1`,
  rotating IDs off and on (S24 -> ASUS -> M31 and the reverse, then each phone as helper; the M31's dual-role limit
  decides which may help), then the same on LE CoC. Record setup time (invite to ringing) and compare quality with the
  DEBUG chain at the same settings (relayed video cap 15 FPS / 110 kbps).

### Design D: dual listening, CoC and GATT at once

**Design review required.** Default off (`test_dual_listening`, Settings > Features "Reachable over both Bluetooth LE
links"). Wire rules: PROTOCOL.md "Experimental dual listening".

**Problem.** A call endpoint advertises one transport. The A15s stack stops accepting LE CoC listens and connects after
about 15 registrations per Bluetooth process. The safety net (automatic GATT when LE CoC fails) moves the failing phone
to GATT, but only phones that also scan GATT see it, and once it is a GATT endpoint it lists only GATT offers, so it can
no longer call a CoC phone.

**Design.** A CoC-primary call offer carries one more bit (`capabilitySummary` bit 3, "also answers over GATT"); the
endpoint hosts the existing GATT server beside the CoC server (one advertising set, one database); a caller tries CoC
first for a bounded 9 s, then GATT to the same device from the same sighting; the endpoint keeps its primary transport
and gets a per-link override. Out of scope: streams, store-and-forward as the dialing side, relay hops, Classic,
GATT-primary dual, and the iPhone compact form. Cost: one more GATT server registration and six ATT handles per dual
endpoint. An independent review (14 findings) was adopted.

**Phone test.** With the switch off, the scan response and behaviour are unchanged; with it on, calls between mixed
pairs set up within 1 s of the off matrix, the A15s forced to GATT lists CoC phones and completes 20 consecutive call
cycles, and the first 30 s of a call over the second transport match a native call of that transport. The S22, Xiaomi
and A55 run it as soon as they are available.

**Open design question:** the monotonic epoch of rotating IDs never returns after a backward clock step (see "Discovery
privacy").

### Design E: uniform adverts, capability fields behind the handshake

**Design review required. Designed 2026-10-11; not coded.** A version 5 advert hides who the phone is, but the rest of
its bytes are the same in every epoch of one phone: the transport bit, the two codec bits, the relay-helper flag, the
relay-consent and dual-listening bits, and for streams the resolution, frame rate and bitrate. Together they are a few
bits of per-phone state that split a crowd into groups (GAEN and DULT require everything that differs between devices to
rotate with the address or be identical for all). PROTOCOL.md "Fields that stay constant across epochs" lists the
inventory and the reductions already made.

What has to stay in the advert: kind, transport, accepting/paused/full, route cost, sequence. What can move behind the
handshake (its hello already carries the codec masks, bitrate and `maxChildren`): the codec masks, the helper flag, the
consent bit, the dual-listening hint and the stream's resolution, FPS and bitrate. The cost: a caller can no longer hide a
phone with no common codec before connecting (an incompatible call fails after the handshake with a clear message, after
one connection of 2-4 s), and a relay helper learns the consent only on connecting.

| Option | What changes | Tradeoff |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A | Measure first: count the distinct version 5 layouts per kind on the reference phones before flipping defaults | No code now; the decision comes while changing version 5 is still free |
| B (recommended if more than one layout is populated) | Redefine version 5: the capability summary of calls and streams becomes a constant, and the codec check, helper flag, consent bit and dual hint move into the signed hello | One more failure path for calls with no common codec; a protocol version change |
| C | Leave the bits and disclose them as a few bits of quasi-identifier | Nothing to build; a crowd is split into a few groups |

### Design F: BMH3 identity-hiding handshake and stranger pseudonyms

**Coded 2026-10-11; on by default since 0.2.0-beta** (Settings > Features "Hidden identities" and its child
"Pseudonymous toward strangers"; DEBUG `test_bmh3`, `test_bmh3_pseudonyms`). Wire rules: PROTOCOL.md "Experimental
BMH3 identity-hiding link handshake" and "Phase 2: pseudonymous stranger identities". The protocol choice and the
rejected alternatives are in DECISIONS "BMH3 identity-hiding handshake: protocol choice" and "Pseudonymous strangers:
design choice".

Rationale, reviews and measurements are in [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md) ("BMH3 identity-hiding handshake: protocol choice", "Pseudonymous strangers: design choice"); the executable specification is `tools/bmh3_reference.py`.

**Still open.** Streams answer BMH2, so a source's identity stays visible to viewers. A phone with pseudonyms on refuses
the old handshake, so it cannot call or be called by older versions or by a phone with the switches off. Message
exchanges and relayed calls work only with verified contacts while pseudonyms are on. A stranger who calls or answers
still learns who you are once you accept. Binding the verification QR to a live handshake is a follow-up.

### Design G: naming and identity display v2 (names off the air, generated stranger names, one precedence function, mutual in-person verification, Visible by name)

**Designed 2026-10-12; not coded; independent review done before coding (findings below).** Wire rules: PROTOCOL.md "Naming v2". Research: how Bitchat, Briar, Meshtastic, Meshenger, Knit, Signal, Threema, SimpleX, AirDrop and Quick Share name people (the comparison table is in the private block at the end). It builds on, and does not replace, "Peer labels" (signed `BNL1` names inside the session), rotating discovery IDs, BMH3 with stranger pseudonyms (Design F), the UTS #39 look-alike check and the verified-contact store with the Contacts screen.

**What people will see (plain words).**

- Nobody broadcasts a name. Phones nearby show up as a friendly generated name such as "Blue Otter". The name is made from the phone's current 15-minute random discovery ID, so it changes when the ID changes and cannot be used to follow anyone. Your own phone shows what others see: "Others nearby see this phone as Blue Otter until 14:15".
- When you call or answer someone you have not verified, the name they chose for themselves arrives inside the encrypted call and is shown with a tilde: "~Rahul". The tilde means "this is what they say; nobody checked". You see "~Rahul" only if Rahul set a name; otherwise you keep the generated name.
- People you verified in person show under the name you gave them, with a shield. What they call themselves later never changes your list. If someone who is not your contact claims a contact's name, or a contact's key changes, you get a clear warning instead of a silent change.
- Verifying is done together, face to face: each phone shows a code and scans the other's. A photo of a code stops working after five minutes or after one use. A scan proves you reached the phone that holds that key through a secret from its screen; it does not prove you stood in front of it, so the app asks "Is this Rahul's screen you scanned?" before it marks anyone. The shield means "I scanned them"; "scanned each other" appears when they scanned you too.
- An optional switch, "Visible by name to everyone for 10 minutes" (for a meeting, a camp, a protest line), lets people nearby see a name you pick next to a separate ID that exists only for those ten minutes (your ordinary ID never carries the name). It turns itself off after exactly ten minutes.

**Decisions in one table.**

| # | Question | Decision | Default and off-switch |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| G1 | Where does a self-chosen name go? | Never into any advertisement (outside G5). Only into signed `BNL1` labels inside the endpoint session, as today. The existing design already does this; what changes is when it is sent and the default. | "Names and subjects" ON once its phone test passes (today OFF); the name itself is empty until the user types one; audience setting below; switch in Settings > Features |
| G2 | What do strangers look like before a connection? | A generated two-word name from the current rotating ID (call-ready phones, stream offers), from the throwaway key for an incoming stranger call, never from a stable key; handle for fixed-prefix adverts as today | ON with rotating IDs; `generated_names` switch (OFF shows "Nearby phone" and the handle as in 0.2.0-beta) |
| G3 | Who wins, yours or theirs? | Your label always wins for a verified contact. One precedence function names every row on every screen. Their claimed name is a detail, never the title. Key and look-alike warnings. | Always on; the warnings have no off-switch |
| G4 | What is "verified"? | In-person scan, both ways, with a live check (`BVMID2`). Code-only comparison no longer gives the shield. | `bvmid2` (ships OFF until its phone test N5 passes); "Accept older verification codes" (`bvmid1_compat`, Advanced, default OFF) for 0.2.0-beta phones |
| G6 | Blocking | Referenced only; the two features it must plug into are listed | Separate feature |

#### G1. Names stay inside the session

**What exists.** `BNL1` labels (PROTOCOL "Peer labels") are identity-signed, bound to the call or stream ID, sent in BME1 type 10 only when both endpoint hellos carry bit 5, and never on air. The link and the endpoint session are encrypted, so a radio sniffer sees neither bit 5 nor the name (a relay in a relayed call still reads the endpoint hello's bits, an existing residual). The callee's name goes out only at Accept (or at Ringing to a verified contact). The caller's name and subject, since Design F review PR6, are held until the callee's `REVEAL` is verified, so a copied advert collects nothing. The only public naming surface is the stream title page (a title, not a person; the Go-live dialog says it is public).

**What is missing or wrong.**

1. The switch ships OFF (`CallFeatureDefaults.NAMES_AND_TITLES = false`), so nobody sends a name. It bundles two things with different risk: names inside the session, and public title pages. Split them: "Names and subjects" (in-session, default ON once its phone test passes) and "Show my stream title to everyone nearby" (title pages, keeps its present rule: public by default for a stream the user titles, with the note).
2. PR6 means a stranger who rings sees no name at all, only a generated pseudonym name. That is the safest rule and the worst call experience: the callee must accept, and so reveal who they are, before learning who is calling. Disaster and camp use is mostly stranger to stranger.
3. There is no audience control: a set name goes to everyone you call.
4. A stable name is a stable identifier: every stranger you call can compare notes. Nothing says so in the UI.

**Decision.**

- **Audience** ("Who sees my name", Settings > Your ID; only shown when a name is set): "Everyone I call or answer" (default), "Only people I verified", "Nobody". With no name set nothing is sent and the callee sees the generated name (fresh per attempt, so strangers cannot link two calls). Copy next to the field: "Everyone you call sees this name and can compare it with what other people saw. Leave it empty to stay anonymous."
- **Callee's name:** with "Everyone I call or answer" unchanged (at Accept, or at Ringing for a verified contact). With "Only people I verified" it is sent only after the caller's reveal is verified and found to be a verified contact (before that the callee cannot tell a stranger from a contact whose lease expired; review F11). Declining or letting it ring reveals nothing.
- **Streams:** unchanged. The stream title is the only label; a person name on broadcasts would link every broadcast to a person and is out of scope.
- **Text rules (added; review F13):** the existing rules do not block the tilde or its look-alikes (`LabelText` skips the Common script), so a name may not start or end with `~` or a tilde look-alike, may not contain a shield or lock symbol, a tilde anywhere, or the app's own separators, and may not reduce (UTS #39 skeleton, spaces and punctuation removed, case folded) to a generated name (so "BlueOtter" and "Blue 0tter" fail too). The receiver drops such a label (fails safe, like any bad label). Screens draw a claimed name in its own styled element, never in one string with the marks. The existing rules (32 bytes, NFC, no bidi or format characters, Moderately Restrictive scripts, no handle-shaped text) stay.
- **Compatibility:** nothing new on the wire. A 0.2.0-beta peer has bit 5 off (its switch ships off) or on (explicitly enabled): labels flow only when both carry it, so against such a peer the new build simply shows the generated name. A 0.2.0-beta peer with labels switched on shows the early name by its old rules (quoted, no tilde).

#### G2. Generated names for strangers

**Rule.** `name = adjective[i] + " " + noun[j]` where `d = SHA-256("BluetoothVideoMesh/Naming/StrangerName/1" || 0x00 || input)`, `i = d[0]`, `j = d[1]`, and `input` is exactly one of:

1. the 6-byte RDI of a version 5 / compact v2 advert (call offers, stream offers, forwarder offers), as seen by a scanner or as computed by the owner for its own role-0 and role-1 IDs;
2. the 16-byte node ID of the throwaway key of an incoming stranger call (per connection or per attempt, from Design F), on both phones of the call.

Nothing else is ever an input: not the node ID, prefix, fingerprint, handle or lease key of a real identity, not a per-install secret, not the advertising address. The function has no overload that accepts those types, and a test greps the callers. A fixed-prefix (version 2) advert, a Classic role or a Visible-by-handle window has no rotating ID: it keeps the full handle as today (the handle is stable by construction, so a stable-key name would be a tracking handle). A name derived from the RDI discloses nothing the advert does not already disclose, and changes whenever the RDI changes (every 15-minute epoch, and between roles: the same phone is "Blue Otter" as call-ready and "Gold Fox" as a stream source, which is the unlinkability of role 0 and role 1 working as designed).

**Why derive it from public bytes.** The same input gives the same name on every phone, so people can say it aloud ("I am Blue Otter") and the owner's own phone can show it. A per-phone secret would remove that and add nothing against an attacker, who can advertise any RDI anyway.

**Display.**

- Nearby and watch lists: the generated name as the title, "Nearby phone" and the role (can take calls, is broadcasting) below, a colour chip from `d[2] & 7` (never the only cue) and no signal strength or distance next to a name (Telegram's People Nearby was trilaterated and removed). A stream offer without a title is "Blue Otter, broadcasting" with a camera mark. A stream title read from a title page stays "unconfirmed".
- Own phone (Settings > Your ID and on the Call screen): "Others nearby see this phone as Blue Otter until 14:15" (the next boundary), with a second line while broadcasting; shown only while the phone is advertising, with the suffix when it knows of a collision and otherwise a note that a number may be added if another phone has the same name.
- Incoming stranger call: "Gold Fox is calling" (name from the throwaway); the caller's screen says "You appear as Gold Fox", which lets two people confirm out loud that the call on the screen is the one they mean. It authenticates nothing (16 bits, see below), so the ring screen shows "Not verified". A redial is a new attempt and arrives under a new name; the ring screen says "A new call. Calls from the same phone show a new name each time." so a caller who redials is not mistaken for several people.
- While a call or join is running the title is pinned to the name it started with (the ID changing at 14:15 does not rename an open call).
- After a self-claimed name arrives: the title is the name in its own style with the tilde mark and the words "calls itself" ("~ Rahul, calls itself"), "Gold Fox - not verified" as the second line. Words always accompany the glyph (review F17); a screen reader says "calls itself Rahul, not verified".
- A recognised contact (presence lease) keeps "Mum, seen nearby, unconfirmed". Contacts are never given a generated name.
- Recents: a stranger's entry stores the generated name and the time only (strangers' names are not cached, as today); it is not a handle for later. This amends Design F step 5, which replaced the recent-calls entry by the revealed real identity: the real node ID and fingerprint of an unverified stranger stay in memory for the call and are persisted only if the person is then verified (review F20: a seized phone should not hold a list of stranger keys).

**Collisions.** Two phones in one list can draw the same name (16 bits: about 0.7 % at 30 phones, 7 % at 100). The list grows the label until the rows differ, never merges rows by name (the OSHI mistake): same name gets two digits from `d[3] % 100` ("Blue Otter 42"); if those also match, the last four hex digits of the RDI. The digits come from the RDI, not from arrival order, so every phone that sees the same rows shows the same text. A copied ID is flagged only when two addresses carry the same RDI at the same route cost at the same time, repeatedly (review F4: one ID on a second address inside an epoch is normal, and a relay mirrors a child's ID at route cost + 1), with "More than one phone is using this ID" and a second tap. Because anyone can advertise any RDI (a name that collides with a friend's costs about 2^16 hashes, and with the digits 2^24), the name is a convenience for picking a phone in a room, not a credential: a call to a copied advert fails the existing discovery binding (the reveal must open the selected RDI), the subject is held until then, and trust comes only from verification. A phone that sees only one of two colliding rows shows no suffix; the design says so rather than promising uniqueness.

**Confusables and imitation.** The list is ASCII lowercase, so generated names have no cross-script confusables. The checks that matter run at display time with `LookAlike`: a self-claimed name that has the same skeleton as, or is identical to, (a) a contact label, (b) your own name, (c) a generated name, or (d) another row visible now, is handled by kind (review F14: an alarm that fires constantly teaches people to ignore it). For (a) a contact label: the alarm wording "Not your contact Mum" (the present code excludes identical text; identical must now be flagged too, because a stranger claiming exactly "Mum" is the most likely imitation). For (b), (c) and (d): a neutral note ("2 people nearby call themselves Rahul"), never a warning. Contact labels are forced unique (Meshenger), case- and skeleton-insensitive, in "Save contact as" and rename.

**Word lists (`names-v1`, replaced by `names-v2` on 2026-10-12, see "Word lists v2" below).** Two lists of 256 words (`assets/names/adjectives-v1.txt`, `nouns-v1.txt`; 16 bits; with `d[3]` that is enough because collisions are resolved at display time). Original work written for this project and released under GPL-3.0-or-later with the app; no third-party list is copied, so no attribution is owed (EFF wordlists, Diceware, BIP39 and Knit's lists were studied only for method; EFF lists are CC BY 3.0, Knit is GPL, BIP39 is MIT: all compatible, none used). Rules for entries: common English words of 3-7 letters, a-z only, one meaning, easy to say aloud and unlikely to be misheard; nouns are animals and plain natural things; adjectives are colours, materials and calm qualities. Also screened out for the places MeshLive is meant for (review F23): colours and animals with a political, religious, caste or insult reading in common target cultures (for example red, orange, saffron, green, black and white as party or community colours; cow, pig, dog, donkey, monkey, rat), read by speakers of at least Hindi, Arabic, Spanish and French before freezing; the lists are frozen only after 256 entries each pass every rule. Not allowed: any word on the offensive-word lists used offline for screening (LDNOOBW English plus Spanish, French, German, Hindi and Arabic transliterations; the screening lists are not shipped), words with a sexual, violent, medical, religious, national, ethnic or political reading, and words that carry authority, emergency or trust cues (police, army, official, admin, help, rescue, safe, free, verified, trusted, urgent, fire, medic) because in a disaster a name must not look like a message. Within a list: edit distance of at least 2 between any two words, no homophones (CMU dictionary check), no entry that is a prefix of another. All 65,536 pairs are screened against the offensive lists as phrases and as concatenations. The files, their SHA-256 and the screening report are committed; the hash is a test constant, so any edit fails the build. The list is frozen once shipped: changing it changes every phone's display of every ID, so a new list is `names-v2` with a release note, never an in-place edit. Localisation: the lists are English in v1; translated lists keyed by the same indices would make two people with different phone languages read different words aloud, so they are an open question rather than a default.

**Honest limits.** 16 bits are not an authenticator and not a short authentication string: a man in the middle can generate keys until the throwaway names match in seconds. The generated name is for finding and saying, the safety code and QR for knowing. It makes it easy to say "call Blue Otter", which also makes it easy for a bystander to hear the name; the rotating ID bounds the lifetime of that to the epoch.

**Off-switch.** `generated_names` (Settings > Advanced, DEBUG `test_generated_names`): off shows the 0.2.0-beta text ("Nearby phone", the handle). Everything else keeps working.

#### G3. Your label wins; one precedence function

- **One function names a person on every screen** (nearby list, watch list, ring screen, in-call header, call log, notifications, Contacts, Verify), in this order, first match wins (Meshtastic's single security-state function, #7117, and Threema's tilde and dots):

| Rank | State | Title | Mark | When |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Key changed | "Not Mum" or the handle | red warning, call refused or explained | prefix of a verified contact with a different key (existing `MISMATCH`), or a name claim, below |
| 2 | Blocked | (Blocked-people feature) | grey | referenced, not designed here |
| 3 | Verified contact | your label | shield | the session key equals a verified fingerprint |
| 4 | Recognised contact | your label, "seen nearby, unconfirmed" | no mark | a presence lease matched an advert; no connection |
| 5 | Self-claimed | "~Name", generated name below | tilde | a verified `BNL1` kind 1 from an unverified peer |
| 6 | Generated | "Blue Otter" | none | rotating advert or stranger throwaway |
| 7 | Handle | full handle | none | fixed-prefix advert, Classic, Visible-by-handle window |

  A help screen "What the marks mean" lists the seven rows. Lock-screen notifications use no name (existing Q16 decision).
- **Your label wins.** The contact's own current name is never the title. It is shown in the contact's detail and once, as a note, when it changes ("Sunita now calls herself 'Sunita K'. [Use it as your label] [Ignore]"; nothing changes unless you tap). Not shown beside the label in lists (Briar shows "alias (their name)" always; the open questions ask whether to copy that).
- **Where their claimed name is kept.** A new sealed file `identity-verification/contact-claims.bin` (same Keystore key and `AtRestCipher`, atomic writes) maps contact fingerprint to the last verified claimed name (at most 32 bytes) and the time. Only contacts get an entry; strangers' names stay in memory for the session. It is separate from `verified-contacts.bin` so a downgrade to a build without it loses only the note, never a contact. Forget and wipe delete it.
- **The verified mark belongs to the key.** The store is keyed by the full fingerprint (existing). A name change does not touch it, so the Bitchat #1708 attack (a verified key renames itself "medic") has nothing to attack: the title is your label. The converse is covered by the name-claim warning.
- **Name-claim warning (soft key change).** A reinstalled or replaced phone has a new key and a new prefix: it is a stranger. If its self-claimed name equals or looks like a contact's label or that contact's last claimed name, the ring screen and list say "Not your contact Mum. If Mum has a new phone, scan her code again in person." **Before the reveal** (review F12) the same condition may be a real contact whose presence lease expired, who arrives as a stranger; the text is then neutral: "Says they are Mum. The app will check when you answer." The alarm wording appears only after a reveal with a different key, or for a stranger who never reveals. After an in-person mutual scan of that phone the Verify screen asks "Is this Mum's new phone? [Yes, replace Mum's key] [No, add a new contact]"; replace keeps the label, forgets the old key (and its presence leases, starting a new generation) and logs nothing else. Without the in-person scan nothing is offered.
- **Hard key change.** A phone that has a verified contact's prefix but another key is the existing `MISMATCH`: refused by default, with "This phone claims to be Mum but its key is different from the one you checked. Someone may be pretending to be Mum." No change to the mechanism; the text and the precedence row are new.
- **No second source of names.** The OS Bluetooth name is never used (existing rule).

#### G4. Verified means scanned in person, by both

**Today.** `BVMID1.<fingerprint>.<name>` is a static code; a verification succeeds only when a live endpoint handshake has that fingerprint, which is why a photo proves nothing alone. The remaining gap is social (a photo passed off as a meeting). Verification is one-sided per phone; presence leases need both sides, so a one-sided scan gives no recognition. The safety code can be compared over any channel and also marks "verified".

**Decision.**

1. **Meet in person screen** (Settings > Verify a contact, Contacts > Add contact, the lock in a call). Top half: your code (`BVMID2`); bottom half: the camera. Either phone may scan first. Text: "Hold the phones together. Each of you scans the other's code."
3. **Mutual by construction (Briar).** The displayer sees the inbound tagged connection with the scanner's real key and shows "Rahul scanned your code. Now scan Rahul's code." It marks nothing until it has scanned the other phone's code. Each scan is its own tagged session, so each phone learns the other's scan from a real authenticated event (review F7), and "You and Rahul verified each other" needs both events. A shoulder-surfer who photographs a code and scans first can win the race: the displayer then sees his claimed name and handle, the real person sees "That code was already used", and a second fresh attempt cancels the pending scan at once with "Two phones used your code. The first one may not be the person in front of you. Start again." (review F9; a radio observer replaying a sniffed first message cannot raise this alert). He learns only the fingerprint the code already showed, and he cannot become verified on the displayer's phone because the displayer must scan his code in person. While the screen alone has made the phone call-ready, it accepts only the code's tag and closes every other connection (review F10).
4. **Outcomes, in words.** "You verified Rahul" (you scanned), "Rahul verified you" (they scanned), "You and Rahul verified each other" (both: the shield and "Save contact as" prefilled with their claimed name, editable; leaving it empty keeps the handle). The shield means "I scanned them" and is set on the scanner's phone on its own scan; "verified each other" is a separate fact that needs both scans. Presence leases move only inside an answered call between two phones that have each other verified (Phase 2 rule, unchanged), so recognition starts after the first answered call that follows a mutual scan; the screen says "Your phones will recognise each other after your next call." (review F8). A one-sided result says so ("Rahul has not scanned your code yet").
5. **Code-only comparison** (the 60-digit safety code) stays as a read-aloud aid on the same screen, but no longer gives the shield. A contact marked that way today keeps working, shows an outline mark "Checked by code" and a button "Scan to confirm"; no existing contact loses protection or the leases it already holds at upgrade. New code-only checks create the same outline mark, keep the `MISMATCH` refusal and the expected-contact check, and get no new leases until scanned both ways (review F18). This follows the direction "verified is in person only" and keeps old data.
6. **Fallbacks.** Rotating IDs off or a phone that cannot start a non-connectable advertising set: the screen offers the BVMID1 flow (needs an existing connection). "Accept older verification codes" (`bvmid1_compat`, Advanced, default off since the G-Q4 compat review) shows BVMID1 beside BVMID2 for a 0.2.0-beta phone and lets a BVMID1 scan give the shield; it is a labelled downgrade because BVMID1 has no capability. With it off, a BVMID1 scan on a new build gives only the "Checked by code" outline.
7. **Remote adds** (a pasted link, an introduction) are out of scope; if added later they can never produce the shield (Meshtastic: verified never arrives via a relay).

#### G5. Visible by name for 10 minutes

**Behaviour.** Settings > "Visible to everyone nearby" has two buttons that share one window state and one countdown: "By ID" (today's "Visible by handle for 10-25 minutes", unchanged: fixed prefix, so old builds and people who know your handle can find you) and "By name" (new). Starting one ends the other (never both: a name next to a fixed prefix would give observers a permanent name-to-key table). "By name" asks for the name to show (empty, with a hint to use a nickname, and a confirmation if it equals the name used in calls, because a real name in a public window is the risky default; review F16), explains the cost in two lines, shows a persistent notification with a countdown and "Stop now", and reverts by itself after exactly ten minutes. Phones nearby show "~Rahul - Blue Otter" until then. If "Block unknown callers" is on it asks whether to allow calls from anyone for the same ten minutes; the answer reverts with the window.


1. a **window offer**: a connectable set advertising announcement version 8 (the version 5 layout, 22 bytes) whose six prefix bytes are the phone's **role 3 ID** for the current epoch (role 3 is reserved for this window; the ID is derived exactly like roles 0 and 1, so it is unlinkable to them) and whose route-token field carries `SHA-256(name)[0:4]`. A caller selects it like a call offer, opens it with `BDO1` (role 3) and meets a throwaway key as for any stranger call;
2. **name pages** (version 7): a non-connectable scannable set carrying `version 7 | window RDI (6) | SHA-256(name)[0:4] | page (index<<4|count) | 1..10 name bytes`, up to four pages, one per second.

A scanner shows a page's name only if the window offer it is currently seeing carries that same hash (review F2: without the hash anyone could attach "~Police" or "~Informer" to someone else's live window ID). Nothing is signed, so the name is shown "~Rahul, calls itself", never with a mark. Nothing is broadcast before the button is pressed or after the window ends; a phone that cannot get the free advertising sets says "This phone cannot show a name to others" and does nothing.

**Privacy cost, in plain words.** For ten minutes anyone nearby with the app, or a scanner that knows versions 7 and 8, can link the name to the window ID. The ID your phone used before the tap and the ordinary ID it keeps using during the window never carry the name, so an earlier log of them gains nothing; an observer who sees both in the same place at the same time can still guess they are one phone (the residual of any two adverts from one place). If the ten minutes span the 15-minute change, the window ID changes with the epoch and the name carries over, so those two window IDs are linked to each other and to nothing else. The window ends at exactly ten minutes, so no named ID outlives the name. Visibility does not give anyone your real key (callers still meet a throwaway) and does not make you reachable more than "Reachable for calls" already does, but it does invite stranger calls, so the ring limits and the tarpit stay unchanged. No distance, signal bars or ordering by closeness appear next to a name.

**Ending.** Exactly ten minutes after the tap (`elapsedRealtime`, so a wall clock moved back cannot extend it); Stop, an app restart or Bluetooth off end it earlier. No jump to another epoch is needed and the monotonic epoch of the ordinary IDs is never advanced by a window, so the first draft's problems (review F1, F5: continuity link to the next ID, drift past the two-epoch tolerance) do not arise. The "By ID" window keeps its 10 to 25 minutes.


**Interaction with rotating IDs and the 0.2.0-beta window.** The window needs rotating IDs on (the role 3 ID is a rotating ID). The existing Visible-by-handle code (window state, notification, 1-8 s restart, 25-minute cap) is reused for the shared state; "By ID" keeps its present meaning and wording. That a 0.2.0-beta phone shows no phantom row and still calls the ordinary row is a required phone check (N7).


#### G6. Blocking (referenced)

"Block unknown callers" and "Blocked people" are being built separately and stay local. Design G needs only: (1) a `BLOCKED` row in the precedence function above (keyed by full fingerprint, so a rename does not escape it, as in Bitchat and Knit); (2) the rule that a stranger can be blocked only after the reveal gave a real key, while "Block unknown callers" acts on the untagged connection before any ring; (3) the Visible-by-name prompt above; (4) generated names are never block handles.

#### Wire and storage changes (all of Design G)

| Change | Bytes on air or link | Old peers |
| --- | --- | --- |
| G1 | none (sending policy and defaults only) | labels still need bit 5 on both ends |
| G2 | none (display only) | n/a |
| G3 | none on the wire; a new sealed local file `contact-claims.bin` | n/a |
| G4 | `BVMID2` QR text; the existing contact tag accepts `K_qr` as one more tag key; no new handshake message | BVMID1 parsers reject the new magic: a 0.2.0-beta phone cannot read the new code and cannot verify with a new build unless the new build has `bvmid1_compat` on (they lose the shield, not the call) |
| G5 | new advertising payloads, only inside a window: version 8 "window offer" (a connectable role 3 ID, version 5 layout, name hash in the token field) and version 7 "name page" | both rejected as unsupported versions by 0.2.0-beta scanners: no row, no crash (a phone check proves it with a 0.2.0-beta build); they lose the feature |

#### Threat model

| Threat | G1 names in session | G2 generated names | G3 label and warnings | G4 verification | G5 Visible by name |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Tracking | A stable name links every stranger session of one user; audience control, empty default and a plain warning; generated name when empty | Name is a function of the rotating ID: no new linkage | none | `K_qr` is random, per code | Links the name to the window ID only (never to the ordinary IDs), and the two window IDs across a boundary inside it; opt-in, exactly 10 minutes |
| Impersonation | "~" marker, look-alike and identical-name flags, reserved forms refused | Chosen collisions possible; display-only, flagged, binding at reveal | Your label wins; soft and hard key-change warnings | Photo useless after 300 s and first use; key possession proven live; scoped session | Anyone can advertise their own name; nobody can name another window ID (offer hash) |
| Replay | Labels bound to the call ID (existing) | none | none | Tag bound to RDI, epoch and msg1; replay cache | Pages attach only to a currently seen window ID |
| Contact-graph leakage | Name goes to verified contacts only at Ringing after the real-key path; stranger callers get no contact signal | none | Claims file is local, sealed | Only the displayer learns the scanner; mutual completion needed for leases | The window is independent of contacts; no contact data is broadcast |
| Harvesting by a copied advert | Name before answer is exposed to a copy (setting to hold it) | none | none | Real key never goes to an unbound phone (existing) | Name is public by choice |

#### Tests and phone checks

JVM (no phone): `StrangerNameTest` (vectors for RDI to indices, frozen list hashes, no overload accepting a stable-key type, list rules: lengths, edit distance, prefix, ASCII, banned-word screen output); `DisplayIdentityTest` (every row of the precedence table, ties, identical-name flag, forced-unique labels); `LabelTextTest` additions (`~`, generated-name equality); `CallLabelPolicyTest` (audience x contact/stranger x pseudonym x timing, including the held case and a 0.2.0-beta peer without bit 5); `ContactClaimsStoreTest` (seal, atomic write, forget, downgrade ignores it); `Bvmid2Test` (parse and reject every malformed field, expiry, one use, epoch change, mutual state machine, old magic); `NamePageTest` (vector, reassembly, conflicting digests, a page for an RDI whose offer carries no or another hash, stale RDI, 60 s rule, a four-page name of 3- and 4-byte code points); `VisibleWindowTest` (about 10 minutes ending at a boundary, boundary crossing, no epoch drift across many windows, mutual exclusion with Visible-by-handle, restart, clock step back); `QrSessionScopeTest` (the code's session refuses INVITE, exchange, relay request and lease records and times out; a `PSEUDONYM` answer is refused; fresh versus replayed msg1 after use); `CopiedIdTest` (address rotation inside an epoch and a relay mirror do not warn; simultaneous addresses do), and `CallLabelPolicyTest` also covers the callee audience in pseudonym sessions and the lease-expired contact text; the existing `Bmh3PseudonymTest` and `Bmh3VectorTest` extended with a `K_qr` tag vector in `tools/bmh3_reference.py`.

Phone checks, each with the same gates on every phone and a frozen build: N1 an HCI snoop and an advert capture over a full day of use show no name bytes outside a window (`tools/rotid_tracker.py` extended to fail on any printable name); N2 a stranger call S24 to M31 with names set shows "~Name" on the ring screen with the setting on and nothing with it off, and a call to a copied advert (scripted) collects the name only in the "before answer" arm; N3 the generated name of a phone matches on its own screen and on two other phones, changes at a boundary and not at a restart inside an epoch; N4 a collision is forced with two scripted adverts and both rows read differently; N5 the mutual scan on two phones (both orders, each scan its own session), a photographed code after 300 s, a second use of a code (fresh attempt versus replay), a three-phone shoulder-surfer case, and the screen turning a non-reachable phone call-ready only for itself and restoring afterwards (also after a crash), with phone pairs taken from the inventory under the same gates; N6 a reinstalled contact (new key, same claimed name) raises the soft warning, an in-person replace works, and a same-prefix different-key phone still hits `MISMATCH`; N7 Visible by name: the window offer and name pages seen by a second phone for exactly ten minutes and gone afterwards, a third phone advertising a page for the first phone's window ID shows nothing, no name in a snoop afterwards, the ordinary role 0 advert unchanged and nameless during the window (byte compare with a window-off capture), a 0.2.0-beta phone neither shows a phantom row for the window offer nor fails to call the ordinary row (calls, relayed calls and contact recognition), a call through the window ID rings on the window phone, a phone without free advertising sets says so, a window spanning an epoch boundary changes the window ID and keeps the ordinary ID rotation unchanged, and the same phone over several windows keeps its epoch lead at zero; N8 TalkBack reads the precedence rows and the tilde ("claims to be Rahul"); N9 battery for one hour with the window open against closed. Anything not run stays UNMEASURED.

#### Implementation order and switches

1. `DisplayIdentity` precedence function plus the existing screens moved onto it (no behaviour change except text); 2. `generated_names` and the lists; 3. split of the labels switch, audience and timing settings, text rules; 4. `contact-claims.bin` and the warnings; 5. `BVMID2` (needs the `K_qr` tag key in the BMH3 responder checks and the Meet in person screen); 6. window offer (role 3 ID, version 8), name pages and the shared window. Each step ships behind its switch and gets its row in docs/SWITCHES.md when it is coded (`generated_names`, `names_audience`, `names_before_answer`, `bvmid2`, `visible_by_name`). Experimental or alternative arms (held name, BVMID1, no generated names, handle window) stay switchable and off or on as above; none is deleted.

### Design AL: audio-only live stream (radio)

**Design review required.** Design only; default off (`test_audio_live`, Experimental Settings > Features switch "Audio-only live"). Nothing here changes BMH2/BMH3, BMS1, BME1, BTV3, BFG1, existing video streams or calls. The wire rules are in [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) "Experimental audio-only live streams". Decision 2026-10-12 (user): compatibility with 0.2.0-beta matters little (few downloads, and the app has an updater), so where the only reason for a weaker design is that release, the cleaner design is used. The fail-safe rule stays: an older phone must never crash or show a black or broken picture. It simply cannot join an audio-only stream.

**Goal.** A standalone audio broadcast ("Go live: audio only", a nearby radio): announcements, events, emergencies, a neighbourhood or festival channel. It is a first-class stream kind, not "video with the camera off". Audio costs about one seventh of the lowest video setting on the wire, so it reaches more hops and more listeners, works on links where video collapses, and lets the screen stay off.

**What users get.**

- **Go live** (feature on) opens a short choice: *Video and audio* (today's flow) or *Audio only (radio)*. The choice is remembered. Audio only shows no camera preview: a panel with the title field (if Names and titles is on), a quality row (Voice, Low data, Music), a live input level meter, the listener count, and Start, Mute, Pause and Stop. Mute keeps the stream and its cadence running: it gates capture atomically, discards every captured sample and unsent partial batch (nothing recorded before the tap goes out afterwards, review 16), then sends signed silence; audio already sent may still play at listeners, and the panel says so; Pause releases the microphone (the lifecycle state PAUSED that streams already have), so the Android microphone indicator goes off.
- **Watch** rows for audio-only streams carry a radio icon and the subtitle "Audio only", then the usual reach ("nearby", "via 2 phones") and the paused and full labels. The row title is the signed or unconfirmed title, else "Radio from <name or handle>". Rows for blocked people stay hidden, as for video.
- **Listening screen** (no video area): the title or handle, a route chip ("direct" or "via N phones"), the live state (Live, Reconnecting, Paused by broadcaster, Ended), a moving level meter so a listener can tell audio is flowing, elapsed time, Mute and Stop. Volume is the phone's media volume; the app never changes it.
- **Background and lock screen.** Listening keeps going with the screen off and the app in the background. It runs under the existing foreground service (types `connectedDevice | mediaPlayback`) with a media-style notification and a framework `MediaSession` (no new library): actions Mute/Unmute (local only) and Stop; headset play/pause toggles local mute. The lock screen shows only neutral text ("Listening to a live broadcast"), a new `NotificationKind` beside the existing session kind (whose public text is just "Active"); it never shows a name (AL-9). The `MediaSession` carries no title or metadata, because lock-screen media controls, the quick-settings carousel, car and watch displays ignore the notification's public version; it is deactivated and released when listening ends. The notification uses the framework `Notification.MediaStyle` (the service uses `NotificationCompat` and androidx.media is not a dependency). The receiver stays owned by the activity in v1, so the service stops and clears its notification whenever the receiver closes (including activity destruction), and never says "Listening" without a running receiver (AL-10); an application-scoped listener is the follow-up if tests show activity kills.
- **Broadcaster safeguards (AL-3, AL-8).** A broadcaster uses `microphone | connectedDevice`, needs only the microphone and Nearby permissions (never the camera, unlike today's `hasRequiredPermissions()`), and must start from the visible app (Android 14 refuses the microphone type from the background). If the service ends up holding `connectedDevice` only (the existing refusal fallback), the broadcast stops with a visible error: a background `AudioRecord` without the microphone type delivers silence, and a silent "live" broadcast is not allowed. The broadcaster's notification says "Broadcasting audio" with Mute and Stop actions. Mute keeps the microphone open, so the Android indicator stays on, and the panel says so. A "Still broadcasting" reminder fires after 30 minutes with no listener or after 2 hours in total, and the broadcast stops 5 minutes after an unanswered reminder. An incoming private call during a broadcast: the call screen ends the broadcast after the user accepts (today's behaviour stops streaming when the call browser opens), and declining leaves it running.
- **Calls win.** Accepting a private call ends the listening session cleanly (a relaying phone withdraws its child with the existing route-withdrawal record, so the child re-routes). A notice offers "Listen again" after the call. A transient audio-focus loss (another app, a cellular call) mutes playout and keeps the link; a permanent loss pauses playout and keeps the link for 60 s, then leaves.
- **Disclosure** (before the first Go live and in the listening screen's info): "Anyone nearby with MeshLive can listen, including phones that pass it on. Titles from people you have not verified are unconfirmed." The broadcaster's first-use text adds: "Listeners can tell it is your phone: until streams use a throwaway identity, everyone who joins learns your phone's long-term identity key, and can link broadcasts from different days to it." Listening never starts by itself; every join is a tap.

**Audio design (Opus, 48 kHz mono).** The header already fixes 48 kHz mono Opus, every Android 10+ phone has a MediaCodec Opus decoder, libopus is already in the APK for calls, and iOS has libopus. Codec2, LPCNet or Lyra reach lower rates but have no platform decoder, no iOS path and no measured behaviour here; they are rejected for v1 because the saving (a few kbps) is smaller than the link overhead (below).

| Setting | Choice | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Encoder | Native libopus (the call codec); MediaCodec `c2.android.opus.encoder` as fallback | MediaCodec exposes no application type, no signal hint and no VBR constraint (AOSP `C2SoftOpusEnc`). Packets are standard Opus either way, so any decoder works. Dependency (review AL-6): the native codec is today tied to the call path (`ConnectivityFeatureDefaults.NATIVE_OPUS` is false until phone test T10; the decoder uses it only in communication mode). Audio-only live gets its own entry points and its own switch `test_audio_live_native_opus`, independent of the Settings toggle "Call audio with loss recovery"; T-AL1 includes the native-against-MediaCodec comparison. Without native decode there is no concealment, so a shed gap is realigned with silence by the existing sender-gap rule |
| Application and signal | `OPUS_APPLICATION_VOIP` with a speech signal hint for Voice and Low data; `OPUS_APPLICATION_AUDIO` for Music | Needs one new `application` argument in the existing JNI create call |
| Presets | **Voice 24 kbps (default)**, **Low data 16 kbps** (wideband cap), **Music 48 kbps** | Opus recommended bitrates: wideband speech 16-20 kbps, fullband speech 28-40, fullband mono music 48-64 (Opus recommended settings, wiki.xiph.org). A code comment in `MicrophoneOpusEncoder` records an offline sweep (2026-10-09) in which complexity below 7 cost 0.4-0.6 PESQ-WB at 24-32 kbps; the artifact was not located during review, so complexity 10 is kept as the existing choice and is to be re-measured (also on the A15s CPU) |
| Frame size | 20 ms | Matches today's capture, PCM queues and decoders, and the record overhead is per record, so batching (below) fixes it without changing the codec. Longer frames (40-60 ms) also code slightly more efficiently at low rates, which is a possible later gain, not measured here |
| Records | 10 frames (200 ms) per record, using the existing `BTA2` batch (3 to 20 frames), flushed early on Mute, Pause and Stop | The existing pair record (`BTA1`, 40 ms) would add 28 kbps of headers on a mesh link; 200 ms cuts that to 7.8 kbps. A radio needs no conversational latency. The loss unit of an age shed is 200 ms |
| FEC (LBRR) | Off | Every hop is an ordered, retransmitting link: the only media loss is our own age shed, which drops whole records, and in-band FEC repairs one frame. Its bits (about 10-20%) buy nothing. `test_audio_live_fec` keeps the arm |
