| DTX | Off | Constrained VBR is expected to code silence in a few bytes per frame (a quiet channel then costs about the record overhead; to be measured), and DTX removes the steady cadence that liveness, the relay trickle test and the playout clock assume. `test_audio_live_dtx` keeps the arm |
| Rate control | Constrained VBR | Bounds peak record size for the age arithmetic |
| Capture | `AudioSource.MIC` with platform noise suppression and gain control when present; no echo canceller. Microphone, noise suppression and acoustic quality stay UNMEASURED (no acoustic adapter yet) | `CAMCORDER` is documented as tuned for video recording (Android `MediaRecorder.AudioSource`); `VOICE_COMMUNICATION` adds echo cancellation, a call audio mode and Bluetooth SCO routing that a radio does not need. The broadcaster does not play its own stream, so there is no echo path |
| Adaptation | v1: the broadcaster chooses a fixed preset; there is no automatic downshift (one encoder serves every listener, and a learned link rate is empty on first contact). The Go live panel suggests Low data when the broadcaster's own learned link is weak (a hint, never a silent change). Review finding AL-7 | Opus can change bitrate mid-stream with no header change, so the later closed loop (`test_audio_live_adaptive`, Experimental, driven by the worst direct child's queue age and shed rate) needs no wire change |

**Per-link load (planning figures; the measured values are the gate).** A mesh stream record costs hop header and tag 33 + mesh envelope 50 + endpoint header and tag 34 + inner packet header 13 = 130 bytes, plus the batch header (5 + 6 per frame). The existing 20% planning margin applies.

| Stream | Record | Wire rate | Planned (x1.2) | Receive + forward (relay) |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Audio Low data 16 kbps, 200 ms records | 595 B, 5/s | 23.8 kbps | 28.6 | 57 |
| **Audio Voice 24 kbps, 200 ms records** | 795 B, 5/s | **31.8 kbps** | **38.2** | **76** |
| Audio Voice 24 kbps, 100 ms records | 465 B, 10/s | 37.2 | 44.6 | 89 |
| Audio Voice 24 kbps, existing 40 ms pairs | 262 B, 25/s | 52.4 | 62.9 | 126 |
| Audio Music 48 kbps, 200 ms records | 1395 B, 5/s | 55.8 | 67.0 | 134 |
| Video default 240p 15 FPS 200 kbps (+32 kbps audio) | | 236 | 283 | 567 |
| Video 360p 15 FPS 300 kbps (+32 kbps audio) | | 336 | 403 | 807 |

**With the per-record signature (review 1) every audio record is 68 bytes larger:** Low data 663 B (26.5 kbps wire, 31.8 planned, 63.6 relay), **Voice 863 B (34.5 kbps wire, 41.4 planned, 82.8 relay)**, Music 1463 B (58.5 wire, 70.2 planned, 140.4 relay); `MeshBandwidthEstimator.audioLive*` computes these. The other rows are the unsigned figures; the 65 kbps weak pair then carries one Voice link at 53% (plan 64%) and a Voice relay (83) still does not fit. The video rows are the app's `MeshBandwidthEstimator` figures, which count only the 13-byte packet header per record; adding the same 117 bytes of hop, mesh and endpoint framing per record would put the video rows about 37 kbps higher (about 273 kbps on the wire for the 240p default). The audio rows include all framing, so the comparison understates the audio advantage, and the audio estimator is a new function, not a reuse of `perLinkKbps` (which hard-codes 32 kbps of audio).

Against the measured context: a healthy link carries 300-1000 kbps, so Voice uses 4-13% of one link; the learned relay ceilings (430-770 kbps) are 6-10 times above what a Voice relay needs, while a 240p relay needs 567 kbps and a 360p relay 807 kbps (the measured collapse with weak relays). The 65 kbps weak pair (ASUS to A15s GATT at 45 ms carried 44-88 kbps, CoC 56-65) carries one Voice link at 49% (plan 59%) and one Low data link at 37% (plan 44%). A Voice relay on that pair would not fit (76 > 65) and a Low data relay is marginal (57 of 65). What the code does is different (review AL-5): `relayFits` compares twice the per-link figure with a transport-level learned budget, not the 65 kbps per-peer rate, and the relay cold start always grants one child. So the app will admit a relay on a weak pair; the protection is the relay child health policy plus the new shed-rate rule below. Whether a relay on the weak pair works is a test outcome (T-AL2, T-AL3), not a prediction.

**Fan-out and hops.**

- **Source.** Children = min(floor(budget / planned per-link), `audio_live_max_children`). Bandwidth alone would admit 11-18 children (428-700 kbps budgets), but the measured limits on this radio are airtime shared between connections (the M31 served one of two 300 kbps viewers and starved the other) and controller scheduling, not payload rate. Default cap **4** direct listeners: a conservative start that the fan-out ladder test (T-AL4) replaces with a measured number per transport. The existing starvation rule (a child below 50% of its offer for 8 of 10 s while a sibling is healthy is released) applies unchanged.
- **Relay.** Viewer-forwarders keep exactly one child and one parent (unchanged `ReceiverNodeForwardingCore`). Admission is the existing `relayFits(budget, perLink)` with the audio per-link figure; relay duty pauses below 20% battery as today. A two-child audio relay is phase 2 behind its own switch, because its limit is the dual-role radio schedule, not bandwidth.
- **Reach.** Cap 4 direct x (1 direct + up to 3 relayed) = at most 16 listeners at 3 relays deep on one-child relays; realistically 8-12, because relays are volunteers. Two-child relays (phase 2) give 4 x (1 + 2 + 4 + 8) = 60 at the same depth (28 at two relays). The mesh TTL (`DEFAULT_MESH_TTL` = 8) allows 7 relays and the offer route cost caps at 15; the UI shows "via N phones". The v1 gate is 3 relays (4 hops); more is measured, not promised. This is a room, a venue or a block, not a stadium: Bluetooth connections are not a broadcast medium.
- **No keyframes.** An audio-only stream has none. A joining child starts at the next record after the cached codec configuration (the relay's existing bootstrap list), so a join costs about one record of delay against 1.5-1.7 s for a video join keyframe. This is a code change, not a property of the current code (AL-12): the forwarding core calls `waitForKeyFrame()` at every child join and the forwarder asks upstream for a keyframe, so an audio-only stream skips both; the source ignores a type-5 request instead of passing it to a video encoder; codec checks and `ReceiverNodeHealthPolicy(header.fps)` get audio-only equivalents.

**Latency and jitter.** Radio is one-way: an announcement heard 1-3 s late is fine; a stall, or an hour of accumulated lag, is not.

- **Listener playout** (new profile `RADIO`, on the existing `MediaPlayoutClock`): start 600 ms, adaptive up to 3 s, shrink after 60 s without late media, catch-up with the existing pitch-preserving speed change (up to 1.06x, so a 1 s backlog clears in about 17 s). Bursts of 0.5-2 s are routine on GATT at 15 ms intervals and during relay joins; there is no picture to keep in sync, so the start delay is higher than the streams' 250 ms.
- **Bounded age (on inside this feature; per-hop only, review 15).** The 3 s and 1.5 s limits are local queue residence at one sending hop. They do not bound how old a record is when a listener plays it: delay accumulates over hops and transport buffers while each queue stays under its limit. End-to-end age is measured (T-AL3), not guaranteed. Each sending hop (source child queue, relay child queue) drops its oldest whole records once the oldest queued record is older than 3 s, down to 1.5 s, and counts them; the listener hears a short concealed gap (native decoder concealment, then the existing sender-gap silence). Reason: a stale emergency announcement is worse than a gap, and this is the only media loss in a reliable chain. It is the audio counterpart of the Experimental bounded media age (video stays no-loss by default). `test_audio_live_age_bound=false` restores unbounded queues (control arm). Shed candidates are decided by two separate exact-match predicates, one for a source's plaintext inner flags (`AUDIO` or `AUDIO | AUDIO_PAIR`) and one for a relay's sealed mesh-envelope flags (8 or 72, on an audio-only stream), because inner bit 4 (debug integrity) and mesh bit 4 (control) share a position (review 18); the header, the Opus configuration and lifecycle records are never shed (AL-11). Two safety rules apply in both arms, because the existing ones do not fit audio (AL-1, AL-2): (1) the relay child health policy reads a backlog that audio-only shedding keeps between 1.5 and 3 s, so it would never withdraw a hopeless child; for audio-only streams the child is withdrawn (rerouted) when more than 20% of its records were shed over 10 s, or when its oldest queued record is older than 30 s in the arm without shedding; (2) the sender's memory limit is bytes (8 MiB, about 35 minutes of audio), so audio-only senders also withdraw a viewer whose oldest queued record is older than 30 s. These rules get JVM tests before the sender code.
- **Estimates, to be measured:** one hop about 0.9-1.3 s (20 ms capture and encode, about 100 ms mean batch wait, queue and link 50-150 ms, 600 ms playout); each relay adds 150-300 ms (it forwards records as they arrive, with no re-batching); three relays about 1.6-2.4 s.

**Admission and capacity numbers.** Source children as above; planned wire load 38 kbps per Voice listener; a relay offers itself only if receive + forward fits; one parent and one child per relay; per-listener starvation, memory withdrawal and capacity-generation advertising unchanged. Reconnect after a parent loss uses the existing recovery path, with no keyframe wait.

**Battery.** Listener: no camera, no video decoder, no GPU, screen off. The audio track uses `PERFORMANCE_MODE_POWER_SAVING` (deep buffer) because playout is already buffered by 600 ms or more. The remaining cost is the radio link and the CPU wake for each decode. Experimental arm `test_audio_live_link_priority=balanced` asks for a 30-50 ms connection interval instead of HIGH (15 ms) on audio-only links: Voice needs about 38 kbps, a 45 ms link carried 44-88 kbps in saved logs, and the relay dual-role snoops showed that the equal-interval stage (15 ms on both sides) is where M31 children died while the 45 ms and 7.5 ms stages never did. The default stays HIGH until the A/B decides, because a 45 ms child sits at the edge of the weak-pair rate. Broadcaster: microphone and Opus encode only, no camera or video encoder, so heat and drain are far below video. No battery figure is claimed: the matched unplugged endurance adapter is missing, so energy stays UNMEASURED and only relative drain (audio-only against video watching, leaf against relay) is reported.

**Wire changes (all new, all fail closed; PROTOCOL has the normative text).**

1. **Offer marker.** In a stream offer (flag `STREAM_AVAILABLE`), `capabilitySummary` bit 6 (`0x40`) means audio only, and then the codec bits 0-1 must be zero; width, height and FPS are 0; the bitrate field is the nominal audio kbps. An older phone reads summary `0x40`, finds no decoder overlap and lists the row dimmed with its existing "not supported" label (it still counts in "N live", and a tap only shows its "cannot decode that stream's codec" notice): it cannot join, so it never opens the stream as a video. The marker is exactly `0x40` (not "bit 6 plus zeros"), which also keeps the H.264 bit 2 undefined. A reader must fail closed when the marker and the header kind disagree (`0x40` with a `BTV3` header, or a `BTL1` header without the marker), and penalises only the attempted route (address, prefix, token) for 2 minutes, plus the authenticated node ID for 10 minutes when the header came over an authenticated link; never the logical stream (review 17); a relay derives its own marker from the authenticated header, never from its parent's advertisement (AL-4). Relays and forwarders advertise the same marker for the same stream. The advertised bitrate is display only (a version 5 offer rounds it to 25 kbps units and the compact field floors it, so 16 and 24 kbps are not distinguishable there); relay admission and the trickle test use the preset in the authenticated `BTL1` header, and a zero advertised rate must not skip admission for an audio-only stream. Compact (iPhone) stream offers (the compact encoder today rejects summary bits other than codecs, so its rule and vectors change with this): codec mask 0 with resolution index 3 and FPS index 3 decodes to the same marker (specification only; an iPhone cannot broadcast yet).
2. **Stream header `BTL1`** (BME1 content type 1) replaces the `BTV3` header for these streams: eleven fixed bytes (magic, audio codec 1 = Opus, sample rate 48000, channels 1, frame ms 20, preset 1 Low data / 2 Voice / 3 Music, a reserved zero byte), then the signer block (a per-stream P-256 key, the source's identity key, and the identity key's certificate over stream ID, fixed bytes and stream key; review 1, below). No fake video fields. A phone that does not know `BTL1` fails the header read ("Unsupported stream header") and closes the link: a clean error, never a black video surface. Rejected alternative: a `BTV3` header with zero video fields would make an older parser throw a misleading "Invalid video format" and bury the stream kind in a sentinel value.
3. **Media.** Only signed audio records follow: `seq || body || 64-byte signature` around the Opus configuration, one access unit, a pair or a `BTA2` batch. A video packet, a rotation packet, an unsigned or badly signed record or a second header on an audio-only stream fails the stream closed, as does anything but exactly one 20 ms mono Opus frame per unit (up to 1275 bytes; RFC 6716 allows far more, review 14). Ingress budgets are enforced before allocation (review 13).
4. **No new endpoint hello bit, content type, or change to BMS1, BME1, BFG1 or the lifecycle records.** The offer is the negotiation: only a phone that understands the marker can select the stream.

What a 0.2.0-beta phone does: a viewer lists the row as unsupported and cannot join; a relay-capable viewer never carries the stream (it cannot join it); a source ignores audio-only offers; a forced connection (for example from a stale cache) fails at the header with a clean stop. No crash, no black screen. Test T-AL6 proves it with the real 0.2.0-beta APK.

**Security.** The same layers as video, unchanged: the signed BMH2 link handshake (BMH3 where on), ordered AES-256-GCM BMS1, a separate BME1 endpoint session with the source, and for a forwarded stream the `BFG1` grant that binds source ID and stream ID to the current media key, so every listener authenticates the original source and its immediate parent, and a relay copies sealed records without re-encrypting. The `BTL1` header is inside BME1, so it is authenticated and ordered. Risks specific to radio, and what the design does:

- *Anyone nearby can listen.* As with video, the stream is encrypted between phones but admits any phone that completes the handshake, and a relay hears it. The UI says so before the first broadcast and on the listening screen. An optional "verified contacts only" audience is Q-AL1.
- *Impersonating a trusted announcer.* A stranger can run a stream titled "Fire: use north exit". Titles from unverified people are shown as unconfirmed (advert title) or with the signed handle, a verified contact gets the existing verified mark, nothing auto-plays, and a blocked person's streams are hidden. There is no "emergency" priority flag, because anyone could set it (rejected).
- *Spam and loudness.* Every join is a tap; the app never raises volume; Block works from the listening screen.
- *Resource abuse by an authenticated source (reviews 12-14).* Age-bounded queues, a single header, and receiver-side checks done before allocation or decoding: link records over 4 KiB before the header and 28 KiB after are refused unread; records are metered (60 per second, burst 600), bytes (16 kB per second, burst 512 KiB) and lifecycle records (4 per second); every unit must be exactly one 20 ms mono Opus frame by its TOC byte; timestamps are checked as a gap rule (a step must be explained by dropped sequence numbers), a resume rule (a lifecycle record restarts the baseline) and a burst allowance (the media position may lead arrival by at most 32 s). The earlier flat "1 s step, 2x per second" rule would have rejected valid shedding and resumes and is replaced.
- *Fingerprinting.* A version 5 audio-only offer has a distinguishable layout (marker plus zero geometry); the Design E count of distinct layouts includes it. Mute keeps the microphone and its indicator on; the panel says so.
- *Metadata.* Record sizes and cadence reveal a voice stream, as they reveal video today. A listener's presence is visible to its parent, as for video.
- *Hostile relay (review 1).* A viewer-forwarder holds the media key, so BME1 alone cannot stop it forging audio that appears to come from the source; the earlier text that said it could not was wrong. The source therefore signs every record with a per-stream key certified by its identity key, and every listener and relay verifies. A forwarder can still drop, delay, withhold or replay-fail audio, and can present itself as the source (the existing BFG1 limit); it cannot alter the source's audio. Cost 68 bytes per record, about 2.7 kbps on a 200 ms record. Cheaper schemes were considered: a MAC under a source-held key needs a key the listeners also hold, so every relay could forge too; TESLA-style delayed disclosure needs synchronised clocks and delays playout; signing every fifth record over a hash chain saves about 2 kbps but adds up to a second of playout delay and a join problem. A signature per record is the simplest sound scheme at 2.7 kbps (8% of a Voice link).
- *Identity exposure (review 2).* A stream source answers BMH2 until streams move to BMH3 with a per-stream pseudonym (Design F, specified, not built), so each listener, relay included, learns the broadcaster's real key and can link broadcasts across rotating discovery IDs. This feature does not add to that exposure but does not remove it; the disclosure above says so, and the signer block uses the key that authenticates the stream, so a per-stream pseudonym drops in later without a wire change.

**Alternatives considered.**

| Alternative | Verdict |
| --- | --- |
| Keep "video with the camera off" | Rejected as the headline: the source still negotiates video, advertises a video offer, needs a video decoder on every viewer and 5-10 times the link budget, and an older viewer would show a black surface |
| Use the voice-call stack (BMC2, intercom) for broadcast | Rejected: calls are one peer with conversational budgets (400 ms) and the communication audio mode; the stream stack already has fan-out, relays, forwarded-source authentication and discovery |
| `BTV3` header with zero video | Rejected (wire change 2) |
| Own flag on the discovery announcement | Not possible: all eight flag bits are used; the summary byte is free for stream offers |
| A new announcement version, invisible to older phones | Rejected: versions 2 and 5 (rotating) would each need a variant, plus compact, only to hide a disabled row |
| Periodic advertising or LE Audio broadcast for unlimited listeners | Out of scope for v1: the app cannot create arbitrary BIS payloads (see "New Bluetooth and API facilities"); periodic advertising has no acknowledgement, no per-listener admission or revocation and uneven stack support. Worth a research spike after v1 as a lossy, group-keyed, in-range-only mode |
| Mixing several sources or talkers | Out of scope; group calls are Design B |
| Upgrading an audio stream to video mid-stream | Not supported: the header is fixed for the life of a stream; start a new stream |
| Recording on the listener | Not in v1 (privacy and storage); an authenticated listener can always record with another device |

**Switches (register in [SWITCHES.md](SWITCHES.md); all Experimental or off by default).** `test_audio_live` (the feature; Settings > Features "Audio-only live"; default off, constant `AudioLiveFeatureDefaults.ENABLED`); `test_audio_live_kbps` (16/24/48 override); `test_audio_live_batch_frames` (1-20, default 10); `test_audio_live_fec`; `test_audio_live_dtx`; `test_audio_live_age_bound` (true inside the feature; false is the control arm); `test_audio_live_max_children` (default 4); `test_audio_live_link_priority` (high default, balanced arm); `test_audio_live_adaptive` (off); `test_audio_live_relay_children` (1; two is phase 2); `test_audio_live_native_opus` (true; false forces MediaCodec encode and decode). Debug microphone injection reuses `ReferencePcmInput` (reference PCM through the real encoder), so no live person is needed for tests.

**Phone tests and gates.** The same gates for every phone; eligibility follows capabilities (Opus decoder, microphone, transport). Scope: the six required phones (S24, S22, M31, ASUS, Xiaomi, A55), with the A15s as legacy-stack comparator. Generated or licensed reference speech (and one music clip) through `ReferencePcmInput`; scoring offline from the receiver's PCM trace (`DebugAudioTrace`).

| Test | Setup | Pass when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| T-AL1 direct pairs | Each reference phone as source and as listener; GATT, CoC (where supported) and Classic; Voice and Music; 10 min; one pair at a time | Wire rate within 15% of plan; zero underruns after warm-up; no gap above 250 ms outside planned events; queue age p95 at most 1.5 s; the received Opus packets are bit-identical (hash of each unit) to the sent ones when nothing is shed, which is the transparency proof for the link; for Voice also offline PESQ-WB of the decoded PCM within 0.1 of an offline encode and decode of the reference at that bitrate (PESQ is not used for Music; Music quality stays UNMEASURED); native and MediaCodec decode agree on the unit count; no video object created. Timing claims use recorded clock brackets, not an invented zero-time anchor |
| T-AL2 weak pair | ASUS source to A15s listener over GATT (44-88 kbps) and the reverse; Voice, then Low data; 10 min; then the A15s as relay on that pair | Low data passes the T-AL1 continuity gates; Voice either passes them or the result is recorded as needing Low data (the hint text is then confirmed); the stream never stalls silently; learned link rate and plan are logged; whether the relay on the weak pair is admitted and survives is recorded as measured (not a pass condition) |
| T-AL3 chains | S24 -> M31 -> ASUS -> A15s and a second order with the ASUS as first relay; GATT and CoC; 20 min; a second listener joins and leaves mid-stream | The authenticated original source and requested parent are proven at every hop (direct fallback fails the case); per-hop wire rate within 15% of plan; end-to-end media age p95 at most 3 s for 3 relays; a join or leave costs other listeners at most 500 ms of gap; a relay joins only if `relayFits` agrees |
| T-AL4 fan-out ladder | One source with 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6 direct listeners (7 phones) per transport | The T-AL1 per-listener gates at each rung; the highest passing rung per transport sets `max_children` (replacing the conservative 4); a starved child is released by the existing rule |
| T-AL5 background | Listener 30 min with the screen off, app in the background, Doze idle, on each Android release and OEM skin in the set; broadcaster 30 min screen off; the activity destroyed while listening; a broadcaster whose microphone service type is refused | Playback continues; notification and actions work; the lock screen and media controls show neutral text only; `BackgroundReliability` reports restrictions; a destroyed activity clears the notification and the media session; a refused microphone type stops the broadcast with a visible error; relative drain per hour (audio-only against video watching) reported, not scored |
| T-AL6 older build and mixed settings | The 0.2.0-beta APK as viewer, relay candidate and source next to an audio-only source; a new build with the feature off as viewer; rotating IDs plus BMH3 plus audio-only together; a hostile marker and header mismatch | Row dimmed and never joins; a forced join ends with "Unsupported stream header", no crash, no surface; the feature-off new build behaves like the older one; version 5 and BMH3 streams work; a mismatch fails closed and the route is cached as bad; video streams unaffected |
| T-AL7 interruptions | Incoming call, cellular call, media-app focus, headset unplug, Bluetooth earbuds connect, phone lock, source Pause, Mute, Stop and kill | Focus rules as specified; a call ends listening cleanly and its child re-routes; Pause and Stop states reach every hop; listeners see the right state and the notification follows |
| T-AL8 age-bound A/B | Injected 3 s and 10 s stalls at one hop (DEBUG impairment), bound on and off | Bound on: age never above 3 s plus one record after recovery, gap at most the stall; bound off: the backlog drains by catch-up; both reported for the user's decision |
| T-AL9 headset coexistence | A2DP earbuds during a Voice listen, leaf and relay | Stalls and media age against no headset reported; decides the notice text and whether relay duty pauses with a Bluetooth output (the call rule is unchanged) |
| T-AL10 security, parsing and policy (JVM) | `BTL1` and marker vectors, truncated and trailing bytes, a video packet in an audio stream, an oversized Opus unit, a header after media, a forged `BFG1`, replay, a flooding clock; the shed-class rules; the shed-rate and 30 s withdrawal rules; relay admission and trickle thresholds at 24-38 kbps | All rejected or decided as specified; vectors match the PROTOCOL table; the live reader never creates a video decoder |

Cases are generated through the suite inventory when implemented (new case IDs, input type "reference audio", assigned owners and exclusive phone aliases); no pass is inherited from existing stream cases. Microphone capture, platform noise suppression and acoustic quality stay UNMEASURED until the acoustic adapter exists.
| T-AL11 preservation | Video streams and calls on the changed stages (receiver, forwarder, broadcaster, discovery) | Existing gates unchanged: one chain per transport with the A15s as leaf, plus a call pair per transport |

**Promotion (2026-10-17, design decision).** The user asked for audio-only live on the Go live screen like voice calls, and approved turning it on by default after short phone checks instead of the full T-AL list. Checks on the 0.3.0 candidate (Voice preset, GATT, 45-90 s each): S24 to M31 direct, 2231 packets, 0 drops; ASUS to S24 to M31 relay, authenticated original=ASUS parent=S24, every record verified (0 failures), 0 drops, playback continued for over a minute with the listener's screen off; ASUS to A15s weak pair (the link that freezes 360p video), 2711 packets, 0 drops; a feature-off build next to an audio-only source neither crashed nor joined. A relay takes one listener in this version (a second listener asking for that relay waits; two is phase 2). Still open (UNMEASURED, not passed): the 10-minute pairs and other phones (T-AL1), the weak pair as relay (T-AL2), 3-relay chains and join/leave (T-AL3), the fan-out ladder (T-AL4), 30-minute background and Doze (T-AL5), the real 0.2.0-beta APK (T-AL6), interruptions (T-AL7), the age-bound A/B (T-AL8), earbuds (T-AL9) and acoustic quality. 



**Promotion criteria.** Default stays off until T-AL1 to T-AL3, T-AL5, T-AL6, T-AL7 and T-AL10 pass on the whole reference set, T-AL11 shows no regression, T-AL4 has set the fan-out cap, and the user has answered Q-AL1 to Q-AL3 (answered 2026-10-12, below). Then the one constant flips. The Experimental arms (balanced priority, adaptive rate, two-child relays, FEC, DTX) stay switchable and off; each is promoted only by its own A/B.

**Change impact (for the inventory at implementation).** The change touches the receiver, forwarder, broadcaster and sender, stream discovery and offers, the stream header decoder and the playout and audio decoder. Per AGENTS, other topologies using those stages (all stream cases and relay chains) are NEEDS RETEST on the first build that contains them, with the feature off. This design changes no existing requirement.

**Decided (user, 2026-10-12): Q-AL1 to Q-AL8 were all answered with the recommended option.**

| Question | Decision | Alternatives not taken |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Q-AL1 Audience | Anyone nearby, disclosed, like video; add "verified contacts only" (an admission check at the handshake) as a later switch | Contacts-only from v1 (safer for private channels, but removes the public announcement and emergency use) |
| Q-AL2 Default quality | Voice 24 kbps | 32 kbps (today's stream audio, +8 kbps); 16 kbps (maximum reach) |
| Q-AL3 Entry point | A choice sheet when tapping Go live, remembered | A video/audio toggle on the camera screen; a separate home tile "Radio" |
| Q-AL4 Source cap | 4 until the ladder test sets it | Bandwidth only (11-18); fixed 2 |
| Q-AL5 Age bound | On inside this feature, A/B kept | No-loss like video (unbounded lag after a stall) |
| Q-AL6 Relay children | One per relay in v1; two-child relays as a phase-2 switch after dual-role measurements | Ship two-child relays at once (more reach, untested on the M31 radio) |
| Q-AL7 Bluetooth earbuds | Do not override the route (streams already play on whatever Android selects); notice plus T-AL9 | Forbid like calls (blocks the most common radio listening device) |
| Q-AL8 Announcer mode | Add hold-to-talk later (Mute is already instant) | Ship it with v1 |

### Design RT: relay child thinning (per-child frame-rate selection at a relay)

Default off (`test_relay_child_thinning`, DEBUG launch extra; Experimental). No wire change: nothing in BMH2/BMH3, BMS1, BME1, BTV3, BFG1 or the mesh envelope changes (PROTOCOL needs no edit). It is phase 2 (P2) of the adaptive-scaling review (`adaptive-scaling-svc.md`, 2026-10-13), which proposed promoting the temporal-layer experiment into per-child selection at relays, as SFUs do.

**Problem.** A relay forwards the source's records to each direct child unchanged. With one slow child there were only bad answers: forward everything and let the child's queue age, shed to keyframes (the keyframe is the largest frame, so a starved link loops), or withdraw the child so it reroutes to the source. A relay S24 -> ASUS -> A15s starved the A15s (the ASUS -> A15s GATT link carried about 44-88 kbps) at 0.1-0.2 FPS while the ASUS itself was fine. The relay can drop frames that the child can do without instead of letting the whole stream fail.

**What the reference stacks do.** mediasoup VP9 (`worker/src/RTC/RTP/Codecs/VP9.cpp`, ProcessRtpPacket) keeps a target and a current temporal layer per consumer, drops packets above the current layer, rewrites picture IDs, and moves up a temporal layer only at a switching-up point; it never needs a keyframe for a temporal change but does for a spatial up-switch. LiveKit's dependency-descriptor selector forwards by decode target and re-checks chain integrity. Both are per-receiver filters keyed on in-band layer data, the same shape as a hop-local relay. MeshLive uses the mesh flags it already has: a frame is a droppable enhancement only when its own bitstream proves that nothing references it (`BitstreamDroppability`), which is stronger than trusting an encoder's layer id; the proof is the reason it needs no switching-up-point bookkeeping (a discardable frame is never a reference, so any layer step is safe).

**Levels per direct child.**

| Level | Forwarded to this child | Child sees | Needs a keyframe to enter / leave |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| full | everything | the source's frame rate | no / no |
| base | everything except verified-discardable video | half the frame rate (layer 0 of two) | no / no |
| audio | audio, codec configuration, rotation, lifecycle records; no video | frozen last frame, "Video paused: weak link", speech continues | no / **yes** (requested with the bounded-age back-off, 1.5 s doubling to 15 s) |

Quarter rate needs three temporal layers (`android.generic.3` / L1T3) and a way for a relay to tell layer 1 from layer 2. That is a new flag bit or a new layer field, so it is a wire change, and it is left as a follow-up behind the same switch. With two layers, base is half rate.

**Policy (`RelayChildLayerPolicy`, constants in `Config`, unit-tested).** Evidence is this relay's queue for the child (age of the oldest queued record), what this relay delivered to that child's link (bytes handed over, over 3 s), the link's capacity measured only while media waited (a drained queue says nothing about capacity), the child's own late-media heartbeat flag, and bounded-age shed events. Nothing from beyond the next hop is used (hop-local rule).

- Down: a queue 2 s old, or a shed, acts at once. Otherwise "behind" (600 ms; 300 ms when the child reports late media) for 1 s steps full -> base, and for 1.8 s (after a 2.5 s settle) base -> audio. A keyframe that just left the queue is excused (its airtime, not a steady backlog) until 2 s. With a fresh capacity measurement below 75% of the base layer's offered rate, full goes straight to audio. A stream with no layer goes full -> audio after the longer hold; a stream with no audio never goes to audio (the withdrawal rules decide, as before).
- Up: one level at a time, after the level has been held at least `upHold` (8 s), the queue has been calm (under 150 ms, child not late) for 4 s, and the measured capacity (if fresh) covers the next level by 15% and, from audio, the keyframe's airtime is under 4.5 s. A step down within 20 s of a probe doubles `upHold` (to 60 s); a probed level that then holds 30 s resets it.
- The existing withdrawal and re-anchor rules stay as the last resort; the policy acts earlier (2 s against their 2.5 s sustained).

**Dropping is end-to-end safe.** Whole records only. Each record carries its own content sequence in a sealed header (flags included in the AEAD associated data); the opener requires `sequence >= next`, so a gap is accepted while replay and reordering fail; the mesh layer requires a strictly increasing envelope sequence. This is the same property bounded media age and `THINNED` already rely on. The viewer's decoder queue does not count sequence gaps. Tests seal a real stream, thin it at each level, and open it with the real opener: the child's flags equal the sealed flags, the gap is accepted, a replay is refused, a record with a cleared flag bit is refused, and a reference-chain model decoder decodes every frame it receives at base, after returning to full, and after an audio pause followed by a keyframe. A hostile relay could always drop records; a hostile source could mislabel a reference frame discardable, but the source is the authenticated stream owner and verifies droppability from the bitstream before marking.

**Source side.** With the switch on the encoder runs the temporal-layer experiment: native libaom always (alternating explicit references), MediaCodec AV1 with `KEY_TEMPORAL_LAYERING=android.generic.2`, MediaCodec VP9 only with `test_hardware_temporal_layers` (error-resilient layered VP9 cost about 11 VMAF on the M31 OMX encoder, which remains the default reason not to ask for it). If an encoder rejects the schema it falls back to single layer; if it accepts it but a frame's bitstream does not prove droppability, that frame is not marked, so a relay sees no base layer and the policy goes straight from full to audio. `BENCHMARK temporal_layering` records requested and reported schema per phone. Layering stays on for the whole stream (the source cannot see a weak link two hops away), so the stream pays about 2.5 VMAF while the switch is on; A/B against off.

**Child side (no new record).** The pause is inferred, not signalled: audio keeps arriving, the source has not paused or turned its camera off (stream-state records), and no video record has arrived for 3 s. One video record clears it. That avoids a new relay-to-child record and the version skew an unknown record would cause on an older phone; the cost is that any 3-second video gap with audio running reads "weak link" while the switch is on (including a keyframe still in flight on a very slow link, which is a weak link). A hop-local relay-to-child notice would be exact and is the alternative if the phone test shows false notices; it needs a PROTOCOL change and a design review first.

| Alternative | Why not now |
| --- | --- |
| Relay transcode for the weak child | Costs encoder time, latency and generation loss on the relay phone; last resort in the review |
| Spatial layers / simulcast (review P3) | Raises load on the first hop; a different, larger experiment |
| Source slows for the weakest child | Already the fan-out behaviour; violates the hop-local rule at a relay and hurts the other children |
| Always-on layering without the switch | The measured 2.5 VMAF cost stays opt-in |

**Phone tests and gates (not run).** Same gates for every phone; eligibility follows capabilities (a layered encoder or libaom; both relay and child decoders). One chain at a time; frozen APK; switch on every phone of a chain by tunable `relay_child_thinning`.

| Test | Setup | Pass when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| T-RT1 weak last hop | S24 -> ASUS -> A15s, GATT last hop (44-88 kbps), stream at 150-200 kbps and 15 FPS so the base layer (about 75-100 kbps) is at the link's edge; 5 min; switch on, then off on the same build | Off: the A15s at the old 0.1-0.2 FPS (control). On: the log shows the A15s at base (or audio when the link is below the base layer) within 10 s; the A15s presents about 7 FPS (half of 15) with no freeze loop and no withdrawal; ASUS own playback unchanged (FPS and frozen share within noise of off) |
| T-RT2 audio-only fallback | Same chain, a stream above what even the base layer needs (300 kbps, 30 FPS) | The A15s shows "Video paused: weak link", speech is continuous (audio drops no worse than off), and no keyframe request storm (at most the back-off schedule); when the last hop is made stronger the A15s returns to base/full within about 60 s with a keyframe |
| T-RT3 preservation, strong children | Chains where every child link is strong (S24 -> ASUS -> M31, CoC) and a direct S24 -> viewer | Level stays full for the whole run (`relay_child_layer` has only `full`), FPS, frozen share, latency and VMAF within noise of switch-off at equal bitrate except the declared layering cost (about 2.5 VMAF); no extra keyframe requests |
| T-RT4 two children at the source | One source, one strong and one weak direct child (the weak child is a relay's child in a second run) | The strong child is unaffected by the weak one's level changes |
| T-RT5 viewer states | A child at audio level; the source then pauses, turns the camera off, ends | "Video paused: weak link" never shows for a source pause or camera-off; the cover clears on video; the existing Recovering/Stream ended covers win |
| T-RT6 old build next to new | The previous APK as source, relay and child beside the new build with the switch off and on | Switch off changes nothing; with it on and an old source (no layers) the policy goes full -> audio rather than base |

Each T-RT reads `BENCHMARK relay_child_layer` (level, reason, queueAgeMs, deliveredKbps, offered and measured rates, thinned and paused counts, keyframe requests), the viewer's `weak_link_pause`, and the usual rendered-frame cadence; an offered-load check confirms the layered source's bitrate matches the requested one before any comparison.

**Change impact (inventory).** App behaviour changes only with the switch on. Touched stages: `MeshCore.kt` (`RelayChildQueue` levels, new queue results), `ForwardedStreamSecurity.kt` (`ReceiverNodeForwardingCore`), `ReceiverStreamForwarder.kt` (controller tick and log), `CameraVideoEncoder.kt` (layer request, `temporal_layering` log), `AndroidVideoReceiver.kt`/`MainActivity.kt` (pause notice), `BoundedMediaAge.kt` (classification moved to one shared function, behaviour unchanged), `DebugTunables.kt`. With the switch off the streaming cases keep their results; the shared classification and the new `ChildQueueOffer` values need the usual relay preservation run on the next frozen build. New cases for T-RT1..T-RT6 are generated through the suite inventory, not added to existing IDs.


### Time-based relay queues and viewer buffers

Decision 2026-10-14: relay queues and viewer buffers are sized in milliseconds of media from the measured drain rate and jitter, as an A/B arm; a relay link slower than the stream keeps shedding to keyframes with the paced backlog, and the lead cap gets an A/B. Two default-off arms and the existing lead cap are built; none has run on a phone.

**What exists today (the control arm).**

| Buffer | Limit | Where | Why it is a constant |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Relay child queue (`RelayChildQueue`) | No count or byte cap. Sheds to the next keyframe when the oldest video is older than 2 000 ms minus 250 ms (assumed Auto start) minus the child's reported added playout delay, never below 400 ms (`BoundedMediaAge.streamQueueBudgetMs`, relay-child skip-to-keyframe, on by default). Cooldown 4 s, keyframe grace 3 s. The `queueCapacity` constructor argument of `ReceiverNodeForwardingCore` is only validated | `MeshCore.kt`, `BoundedMediaAge.kt` | 2 s is the largest Auto playout; the 400 ms floor is `LinkRateController.CONGESTED_BACKLOG_MS` so a scene-cut drain does not shed every keyframe |
| Relay withdrawal | `RelayChildHealthPolicy` hard backlog (the child is withdrawn so it reroutes) | `ReceiverStreamForwarder.kt` | Last resort when shedding cannot keep a link |
| Link pacer | Writes at the child-measured arrival rate times 1.2, 50 ms burst; the Experimental lead cap holds the in-flight surplus to 250 ms while the child reports late media (`test_relay_pace_lead_cap`) | `LinkSelfMeasurement.kt` | Keeps the backlog in the app queue, where it can be shed, instead of the Android socket buffer |
| Viewer playout (`MediaPlayoutClock`) | Starts at the profile delay (Auto 250 ms), grows by the late frame's lateness plus 50 ms up to the profile maximum (Auto 2 s), shrinks 1 ms per 100 ms after 60 s without late media | `MediaPlayoutClock.kt`, `PlaybackDelayProfile.kt` | Measured 2026-10-08/09: Bluetooth bursts of 104-245 ms and scene cuts of 0.65-1.3 s |
| Viewer decoder queue (`EncodedFrameQueue`) | fps x (profile maximum + 500 ms) + 3 frames (2-240), back-pressure when full | `PlaybackDelayProfile.videoQueueCapacity` | Already derived from time at construction; left as is |

**What is built (all default off; DEBUG launch extras, benchmark tunables in `tools/run_device_benchmark.py`).**

- `test_relay_time_queue` / `relay_time_queue`: the relay child queue's shed budget becomes `clamp(keyframe drain time + 2 frame intervals + worst drain stall of the last minute, floor, min(latency budget, fixed budget))`. Drain rate is the child's own arrival report for this link (hop-local); the keyframe drain time is the last keyframe's bytes over it; a stall is how long a record waited for the link beyond its own arrival and the predicted airtime of the record before it (10% tolerance). In a sustained overload (offered above 1.1 times the drain over 5 s) the stall term is dropped. No drain report yet: the fixed budget. Class `RelayTimeQueueSizer` (`TimeBasedSizing.kt`).
- `test_playout_time_target` / `playout_time_target`: a stream viewer's playout grows in one step to the 99th percentile of the arrival spread of the last minute plus 30 ms (up to the latency budget) before a frame is late, and calm shrinking stops there. The lateness rule above still applies up to the profile maximum. Class `PlayoutJitterTarget`, used by `MediaPlayoutClock`; calls and audio-only live are excluded.
- `test_time_queue_budget_ms` (default 1 500, 300-5 000) and `test_time_queue_floor_ms` (default 400, 100-1 000): the shared latency budget and the relay queue floor.
- Unchanged in both arms: the codec-safe shed itself, keyframe recovery with back-off, cooldown, keyframe grace, withdrawal, no wire byte, audio never shed. The lead cap A/B (`test_relay_pace_lead_cap`) already existed and is a separate factor.
- Log lines: `BENCHMARK relay_time_queue` (both arms; also on a change of `drainSource` or `overload` and at least every 10 s while the child queue is non-empty, with `trigger=change|periodic`, because a blocked write silenced the writer loop's own line: lane10 b5-q31-drain-193409 got 1-2 lines per 3 minutes), `BENCHMARK playout_time_target` (arm on), `BENCHMARK time_queue_arms` (at launch).

**JVM model result (injected clocks; a model, not a phone measurement).** One relay child queue, 15 FPS of 5 000-byte frames plus 50 audio records/s, a keyframe on every shed request. Mild overload (about 704 kbps offered into 650, 60 s): oldest queued video peaked at 1 850 ms with the fixed budget (3 sheds, 84 video records shed) and 522 ms with the time target (14 sheds, 112 records shed): lower latency, about four times the keyframe requests. Heavy overload (720 into 500, 40 s): 1 868 ms against 1 547 ms, 7 against 10 sheds, because the fixed cooldown dominates. A link that keeps up (1 000 kbps) sheds nothing in either arm. A 700 ms pause every 5 s into a link at twice the stream rate: the fixed budget never sheds; the time target sheds once (the unmeasured first pause), then holds a 924 ms target (700 ms stall + keyframe + 2 frames) and never sheds again. Viewer playout against a repeating 0-300 ms jitter for 300 s: the earlier rule was late on 35 frames (it shrinks after each calm minute and is late again), the time target on 1 (delay 327 ms). Whether fewer late frames or fewer keyframes is the better trade on a phone is the A/B's question.

**Known limits, stated before the test.** The first stall of a link is unmeasured and can cost one shed that the fixed budget would have ridden out. The cooldown (4 s) and keyframe grace (3 s) are fixed and bound how fast a time target can act in a heavy overload: the sawtooth peak there is the cooldown times the overload ratio in both arms, so the arm should show its effect in mild overload (offered 1.1-1.3 times the drain) and on links with repeating stalls, not in a link at half the stream rate. The child's train median reads 10-30% below the link, so the keyframe drain time is slightly long (conservative). A join backlog inside the viewer's window can raise its target for a minute.

**Phone A/B plan (not run; same gates for every phone; eligibility follows capabilities).** One frozen debug APK on all four phones; arms are launch extras only, so the build is identical between arms. Streams use a fixed requested bitrate, resolution and FPS (rate control and the Auto ladder off, as in the relay pacing runs) from a declared generated source, 5 minutes per run, three repeats per cell, cells in a randomised but recorded order; a quiet window per chain (no parallel radio load: runs of different chains do not overlap).

| Factor | Levels |
| --- | --- |
| Chain | C1 ASUS -> M31 -> A15s (dual-role relay, weak last hop); C2 S24 -> ASUS -> A15s (GATT last hop, 44-88 kbps); C3 S24 -> ASUS -> M31 (CoC both hops, strong; preservation); where a chain is unavailable another eligible three-phone chain stands in and the substitution is recorded |
| Offered load | Above the slow hop's measured capacity (about 1.2-1.3 times the child's `arrivalKbps`, so mild overload) and below it (about 0.7 times) |
| Arm | A0 control (everything off); A1 `relay_time_queue` on every relay; A2 `playout_time_target` on the viewer; A3 A1 + A2; A4 `relay_pace_lead_cap` only; A5 A1 + A4 |

Cells: C1 and C2 get A0, A1, A3, A4 at both loads first (16 cells x 3 repeats), then A2 and A5 where A1 or A4 moved a gate; C3 gets A0 and A3 at the below-capacity load. Offered load is confirmed from `stream_tx` before any comparison, and each log is checked for the arm (`time_queue_arms`, `relay_time_queue arm=time`).

Gates, identical for every phone and every cell (a cell without a valid measurement is UNMEASURED, not passed):

| Gate | Measurement | Arm passes when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Latency to live | Viewer held age (`media_age: role=stream_rx` `heldAgeMaxMs`, p50 and p95 per run) and the relay's `queueAgeMs`; a proxy, not optical latency | Above capacity: p95 lower than A0 by at least 10% in the median of the repeats; below capacity: not higher than A0 by more than 10% |
| Frozen share | Share of run time with the rendered frame rate below 95% of the effective rate | At most 5%, and no more than A0 plus one percentage point |
| Longest stall | Longest gap between rendered frames | Not longer than A0 by more than 250 ms |
| Frames shed and keyframe requests | `relay_time_queue shedFrames`, `media_age keyFrameRequests` | Below capacity: no more than A0 plus one shed event per run; above capacity: reported, not gated |
| Audio | Audio records offered and received, audio shed (must be 0) | No loss against A0 |
| Preservation | Withdrawals, route changes, re-anchors, picture quality at equal bitrate | No new withdrawal or fallback; the strong chain C3 unchanged within noise |

A run that violates a gate keeps its place in the table. If A1 or A3 fails the below-capacity shed gate but passes above capacity, the floor (`test_time_queue_floor_ms`) is the next A/B factor; if the viewer arm raises held age on joins, a shorter window is.

### Design CX: Classic address exchange and Classic media upgrade (verified contacts only, opt-in)



**Problem.** Classic RFCOMM carries about 1,500 kbps, several times what LE GATT or CoC sustain here, but it pages a fixed 48-bit BD_ADDR. Discovery is LE and uses resolvable private addresses, so the scanner never sees the BD_ADDR, and Android hides a phone's own address from apps (`BluetoothAdapter.getAddress()` returns `02:00:00:00:00:00`). Today ("Connect without pairing", `ClassicBondFree.kt`) a phone learns a peer's address only after a Classic contact, and the first contact needs a Classic inquiry with the other phone made visible (the system prompt).

**What Android lets an app know (checked against the AOSP Bluetooth module sources at commit 745ee92b87ed, the Android 6.0 behavior page and the API documentation).**

| Way to get a BD_ADDR | Verdict | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Own address: `BluetoothAdapter.getAddress()` | Constant `02:00:00:00:00:00` without `LOCAL_MAC_ADDRESS` (since Android 6.0); the permission is for privileged and system apps | [Android 6.0 changes, "Access to Hardware Identifier"](https://developer.android.com/about/versions/marshmallow/android-6.0-changes#behavior-hardware-id); `BluetoothAdapter.getAddress()` is `@RequiresPermission(allOf = {BLUETOOTH_CONNECT, LOCAL_MAC_ADDRESS})` and `BluetoothServiceBinder.getAddress` returns `DEFAULT_MAC_ADDRESS` without it ([AOSP](https://android.googlesource.com/platform/packages/modules/Bluetooth/+/refs/heads/main/service/src/com/android/server/bluetooth/BluetoothServiceBinder.java)); [immutable device IDs](https://source.android.com/docs/core/config/immutable-device-ids) |
| Own address: hidden setting `Settings.Secure` `bluetooth_address` | Not an API; a developer report has it null on Android 8.1, and the Briar project fell back to making a phone discoverable | Briar issues 225 and 1147 (secondary; the pages were not fetchable on 2026-10-14) |
| Own address: shown to the user in Settings > About phone > Status | Visible to the human, not readable by the app; the user can type it | Android Settings (varies by OEM; Samsung masks it in `dumpsys` but the Status screen is the user's own) |
| Peer address from an LE scan | Random or resolvable private address; the public BD_ADDR only after an LE bond resolves the identity | [BluetoothDevice](https://developer.android.com/reference/android/bluetooth/BluetoothDevice) address types; Core 5.4 Vol 6 Part B 1.3 |
| Advertising with the public address, so a scanner reads the BD_ADDR | `AdvertisingSetParameters.Builder.setOwnAddressType` is `@hide @SystemApi` and needs `BLUETOOTH_PRIVILEGED` for anything but the default | AOSP `AdvertisingSetParameters.java`; not available to an app |
| Identity address of a device | `BluetoothDevice.getIdentityAddress()` is `@SystemApi`, `BLUETOOTH_PRIVILEGED` | AOSP `BluetoothDevice.java` |
| Cross-transport key derivation (an LE bond derives the BR/EDR link key and the identity) | Works, but needs an LE pairing: a system pairing dialog on both phones and a stored bond, which "Connect without pairing" removes | Core 5.4 Vol 3 Part H 2.4.2.4 (section number from memory; check at review) |
| Peer address of an **accepted** RFCOMM connection: `BluetoothSocket.getRemoteDevice().getAddress()` | The BD_ADDR of whoever dialed, no address permission needed; a routing observation, not hardware proof (a tunnel endpoint would show its own address) | AOSP `BluetoothSocket.java` (`getRemoteDevice` is `@RequiresNoPermission`; the accepted socket is built from the native remote address) |
| Peer address from a Classic inquiry | Real BD_ADDR, only for a discoverable phone; but a discovery can page candidates (for names) before the app authenticates anyone, so **this feature does not use it** (below) | [BluetoothAdapter](https://developer.android.com/reference/android/bluetooth/BluetoothAdapter); [Find Bluetooth devices](https://developer.android.com/develop/connectivity/bluetooth/find-bluetooth-devices); Core 5.4 Vol 2 Part B (paging) |

The accepted-socket row and the user typing their own address are the only legal sources used.

**Design.**

1. **Contacts only, both ways, with a key both sides already hold.** Being verified is one-sided by nature (a QR scanned from a poster), and any message that says "I verified you" tells the peer so. The gate is a **Classic pairing key `K_cx`** per contact, established with an explicit per-contact opt-in on both phones ("Allow Classic Bluetooth with this contact", default off). In an answered session each phone whose switch is on and whose peer is verified sends `PAIR(epoch)`; a phone that already holds a key **also answers** a received `PAIR`; when both sent and received, both derive a candidate key from that session's exporter and the epoch and stage it as `PENDING`. A `PENDING` key is promoted to `CONFIRMED` only when the peer's `CONTACT` tag verifies under it, so a crash on one side, a reset or a re-enable recovers by the next `PAIR` without ever sending `CONTACT` into a void. Epochs are 64-bit, monotonic and never reused; a peer epoch more than 1,000 above the phone's own is ignored. If a `CONTACT` tag verifies under none of the keys held, the verifier sends `PAIR` **even when it holds a confirmed key** (one phone confirmed the new key while the other let its pending key expire and both held different confirmed keys), at most once per session and three times per contact per 24 h. `K_cx` proves that **the peer opted in to Classic with this phone**, not that it still verifies this phone (each phone checks its own store and switch at every use). A phone holding a key the peer deleted still sends `CONTACT`; the stale-`CONTACT` disclosure (the peer learns this phone still holds a key for it) is accepted and stated, and **no `HOST` and no dial happen unless both sides sent and received a valid `CONTACT` in this very session.** Revocation, reset (a new epoch) and expiry of `PENDING` are specified in PROTOCOL.
2. **Address echo.** A phone that accepts an incoming Classic connection from a key-holding contact (and not on a relay leg) reads `getRemoteDevice().getAddress()` and tells the dialer, inside the encrypted link and authenticated by a key tag, the address it saw (`ECHO`). The dialer learns **its own** BD_ADDR from its first Classic connection to a contact.
3. **Self-address record (a fallible routing observation) and what may be offered.** Sources: the address the user typed (twice, format-checked, last four digits shown for comparison) and echo votes, one per stable verified contact. **To any contact a `HOST` carries only the typed address** (user-verified). To a contact `C` it may also carry an address that `C` itself voted for or that is `provenFor(C)` (a ticket-bound bind on this phone's listener proves only that `C` reached this phone; the accepted socket does not show which address `C` dialed, so a contact could echo a false address X and then dial the real address Y, and the proof must not make X offerable to others). A malicious contact is thus sent back only what it supplied. Corroboration by several contacts is not accepted instead, because colluding contacts corroborate each other; without a typed address a phone serves only the contacts whose votes or proofs it holds. A newer vote never replaces the typed address; an incumbent for `C` changes only by `C`'s own newer vote or a bound connection by `C`; the previous selection stays offerable for 7 days; `disputed` only when two or more distinct contacts disagree with every candidate. No promotion by elapsed time; routing confidence, not hardware proof. No-backup storage; removed by the emergency wipe, when the feature is turned off and per contact on revocation.
4. **Address advertisement over the authenticated link.** After fresh mutual `CONTACT`, the hosting phone sends `HOST` (address, one-time ticket, validity) inside the encrypted session, never in an advertisement, never in the end-to-end session, never forwarded. Received addresses are claims in a separate store; they never enter or evict anything in the existing address book until a connection at that address authenticated as the expected identity.
5. **Media upgrade, make-before-break.** After the call is answered (or the viewer admitted) and 3 s of media:
   - **Roles and dial.** The side that accepted the session hosts when it holds an offerable address; otherwise the initiator. The host opens an insecure RFCOMM listener on a per-attempt random UUID and sends `HOST`. The guest pages (12 s, at most once per 30 s per contact), reads a fresh 16-byte host challenge and sends a pre-proof that binds the challenge and the exact first handshake message (built once, hashed, the same bytes written); the host verifies it before sending any handshake byte. The ticket is consumed atomically on a valid proof; a retry uses a new ticket and UUID. A failed or timed-out dial is reported to the host with `DECLINE` reason 6 so it can count failures (3 per address per hour stops it).
   - **Handshake options (stage 1).** Same protocol version as the old link, fixed; expected peer pinned to the old link's node ID and fingerprint; no build tag, no pseudonym, no advertisement-bound contact tag, no `BDO1`. `BIND` MACs (ticket, challenge, new transcript) under a key exported from the old link's key schedule.
   - **Simplification principle (root decision, round 6).** The earlier cutover sprouted interleaving holes because it aimed for a perfectly lossless switch. The invariant now is: **no cutover race may ever end the session while a carrier is alive; races may only lose records.** At the consumer any record that is stale, from a fenced carrier or out of order is dropped, never thrown; indispensable state survives by the existing state-only re-assertion; video recovers with a keyframe request after any gap; session failure happens only on an authentication or protocol violation or when no carrier is alive. Wherever a state or row existed only to keep the switch lossless, it was replaced by "drop, re-assert, keyframe" and deleted.
   - **The cutover in one paragraph.** Two carriers, `O` (LE) and `N` (Classic); one `SEND_ON` per sender and one `ACCEPT_FROM` per receiver direction. To move, the sender picks the next frame boundary, writes one in-band `SWITCH(gen, boundary)` on `O` after the last pre-boundary record, sends everything from the boundary on `N` and re-asserts state once. Nothing waits: no acknowledgement, no latency probe, no sender hold. The receiver opens the new-link gate when the `SWITCH` marker is dequeued in order; `N` records that arrive earlier are held for at most 1 s and 5 MiB (a fixed ceiling; on expiry they are dropped and `N` is closed). If `O` dies, the sender writes the same `SWITCH` on `N` (the old tail is lost) and the receiver, which sees `O` die itself, accepts `N` at once. If `N` fails or is closed, both ends fall back to `O` (`RESUME` on `O`, partial fragment assembly dropped, keyframe requested, 30 s cooldown, at most 2 fallbacks per session). Closing `N` is the only fate sharing needed.
   - **One inbound consumer.** Reader threads only parse and stamp records with a carrier instance id (a new socket per attempt, so ids never recur). Content-order-relevant items (media, `SWITCH`, `RESUME`) go through one ordered content queue and a single non-blocking consumer that applies the receiver rows at dequeue before the existing endpoint dispatch (so a blocking `awaitTerminalAck` in `AndroidVideoReceiver` cannot delay a marker). There is no epoch stamp, so a valid record that follows a marker is never discarded. Everything else (`ECHO`, `PAIR`, `CONTACT`, `HOST`, `DECLINE`, `BIND`, `READY`, `CLOSE`) is handled directly from the reader thread. A defensive rule peeks the cleartext `BME1` and mesh sequences and drops any record not above the last opened one, so the opener can never throw at a cutover.
   - **Per-carrier liveness.** A socket error, a write stalled 2 s or no inbound byte for 2 s closes that carrier only; each carrier has its own timeout, so the standby cannot mask a dead Classic link. After `O` is lost there is no standby: a stall on `N` is a session failure through the existing path.
   - **Fragmented call video.** At a normal switch no frame is split. After a `RESUME` or a switch seen over `N` the consumer drops the partial assembly and skips fragments until offset 0 arrives with an empty buffer (the only resync point); while suppressing, `requireIdleFor` must not fire for lifecycle records or signals; strict checks are unchanged elsewhere.
   - **Controls and termination, with no acknowledgements.** After every carrier event the sender re-asserts once, under fresh sequences, the current stream lifecycle state, the microphone and camera state and a pending `ENDED` (inside the existing 2 s `STREAM_END_ACK_TIMEOUT_MS` wait); never a superseded value, never old ciphertext; a late re-assertion cannot revert a newer one because a direction's records are strictly ordered and only one carrier delivers it. Pre-`ACTIVE` signals are never re-sent and no cutover starts before the call has been `ACTIVE` for 3 s. **`HANG_UP` has no ack and no retry**: it is sealed on the current carrier, `CLOSE` goes to every live carrier that finished `BIND` and `READY`, the microphone, camera and screen are released at once, writers drain in the background and the session waits for all readers or 3 s (as today). The content and `CLOSE` may arrive in either order; the first wins. One closing coordinator and one finalizer own shutdown; a second hang-up while ending is benign; a carrier ending is a bearer event, not the end of the call.
   - **Limits.** `READY` exchanges each side's receive ceiling, device-family cap and transport budget, independent of media. Send limit = min(requested, peer's receive ceiling, family caps, transport budgets); a requested bitrate is never raised; LE limits and transport-keyed state (close wait, pacer seed, flush interval, playback delay profile, audio headroom) return on fallback.
   - **Never costs the existing link.** The upgrade attempt (paging and handshake) is cancelled if the old link's oldest queued record passes 2 s while it runs.
6. **Scope in stages.** Phase 1: direct calls and direct stream links with real identities on both sides. Phase 2: stream forwarder hops (hop-local; only between contacts with keys). Phase 3: call relay hops, which first needs Design C's "one bearer end to end" rule amended; not in this design. iPhones cannot open RFCOMM and never set the bit. On a Classic link where a relay `SETUP` applies, `ECHO` is omitted, so the `BDO1` and `SETUP` first-record rules win.
7. **Bootstrap: authenticated out-of-band seeding; no inquiry, no visibility prompt.** The first Classic contact needs the host's address, and a phone learns its own only from an echo. Searching for the host by Classic inquiry is **disabled**: Android discovery can page found devices for their names, which sends the initiator's BD_ADDR before the app has authenticated anyone, names are spoofable, and an advertised per-attempt UUID can be copied to another address.

| Tier | How | Prompts | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | A phone with an offerable address and a confirmed key for the contact hosts over the LE session; the guest dials; the echo gives the guest its own address | none | Normal operation |
| 1 | The user types the phone's Bluetooth address **twice** (shown by Android in Settings > About phone > Status; the app validates the format and never reads it itself; the setting shows the last four digits for comparison). It is offered only inside the encrypted link to a contact with a key, and confirmed by the first ticket-bound Classic connection (an echo, or a bind at that address); three failed dials of an unconfirmed typed address tell the user to check the number | none | Opt-in power-user step; the same field serves the DEBUG seed `test_classic_self_address=` |
| 2 | Classic inquiry and a visibility prompt (the old first-contact search) | a system visibility prompt | **Disabled for this feature.** Reopen only if a stack demonstrably avoids unauthorized paging and the app obtains the current 128-bit service UUID from a cold cache before any page or SDP request (a UUID visible over the air is not the same as `device.uuids`) |
| 3 | LE bonding with cross-transport key derivation | Pairing dialogs on both phones and a stored bond | Rejected |

   **Mistyped address (Q-CX12, decided by the user 2026-10-14).** The address is typed twice, must match and pass the format check, and the setting shows its last four characters to compare with Android Settings. The residual is accepted and disclosed in PRIVACY: a typo that happens to name a responsive nearby device lets that device see the dialing contact's address during paging, before any UUID lookup or `BIND` can reject it (a random 48-bit typo hitting a nearby device is negligible, and a malicious contact already learns the guest's address in this design). The phone test includes a wrong address that belongs to a responsive device.

   Whether insecure RFCOMM raises a pairing dialog or stores a bond is **not** a given: AOSP `btif_dm.cc` (`btif_dm_ssp_cfm_req_evt`, `btif_dm_get_pairing_type`) auto-accepts a Just Works pairing only when neither side set the dedicated-bonding bit, the bonding was not API-initiated and the peer is not a HID pointing device (the temporary-pairing path); otherwise it raises the consent request and the bond is persistent. Absence of dialogs and bonds is a measured compatibility gate per stack (T-CX1); a stack that shows a dialog or stores a bond keeps the feature off (a DeviceFamilyPolicy entry with the evidence) and stays on LE. The "zero prompts" claim is limited to contacts with a key and a seeded address on a validated stack.

**Failure handling.**

| Failure | Result |
| --- | --- |
| Peer lacks the bit or a key, switch off on either side, iPhone, permission missing, a pseudonym or relay leg | No record is sent; nothing changes |
| One side lost its key (crash, reset) | The next session's `PAIR` repairs it (the other side answers even though it holds a key); a `PENDING` key expires in 7 days or 3 failed sessions |
| `HOST` address wrong (stale, lying contact, typo, third phone) | The dial authenticates as someone else or times out; the claim is dropped; the guest sends `DECLINE` reason 6; 3 failures per address per hour stop it; media unaffected |
| Host listener cannot be opened, guest radio busy | `DECLINE` with a reason; no retry for 30 s |
| Proof, `BIND` or identity mismatch | Silent close of the Classic socket; `bind_fail`; the ticket is not reusable |
| `N` fails, stalls or is closed after the switch (either end, either direction) | `N` is closed; both directions use `O` again; `RESUME` on `O`; partial fragment assembly dropped; keyframe requested; state re-asserted; 30 s cooldown, at most 2 per session. The session continues |
| `O` dies while `N` lives (before, during or after the switch, seen by one end or both) | `SWITCH` on `N` (or its repeat); `N` is the only carrier; the old tail is lost; keyframe requested; state re-asserted; a later `N` stall is a session failure |
| Early `N` records and the `SWITCH` marker never arrives | Held at most 1 s and 5 MiB, then dropped and `N` closed; fallback as above |
| A stale, duplicate or out-of-order record at a cutover | Dropped at the consumer; never an error |
| Both carriers gone | The existing failure path of the session |
| Echo disagrees with the selected address | One vote, not a replacement |
| Radio contention starts a freeze on the old link | Cancel the attempt, `abort reason=old_link_degraded` |
| `HANG_UP` | Sealed on the current carrier, `CLOSE` on every live carrier, resources released at once, 3 s reader wait |

**Threat model.**

| Threat | Answer |
| --- | --- |
| A one-sided verifier learns or reveals verification | Only `PAIR` (opt-in, per contact) discloses; a stale `CONTACT` tells the peer only that a key is still held, accepted and stated; everything else is gated on a key both sides established and a fresh mutual `CONTACT` |
| A stranger offers its own address to learn a rotating phone's | No `HOST` is acted on before fresh mutual `CONTACT`, which needs the key |
| A malicious contact poisons the address other contacts are sent to (including echoing a false address and then dialing the real one to "prove" it) | Other contacts are sent only the typed address; a contact is sent back only what it voted for or reached; proofs are scoped to the contact |
| Hijack of the media link | Pre-proof (fresh challenge, exact first message), pinned identity, `BIND` over the new transcript with opposite roles; media stays sealed by the unchanged `BME1` keys; a link-layer attacker can only drop or delay |
| Address spoofing | The address is only a hint; the handshake decides who answers; claims never evict address-book entries |
| A contact lies about **its own** address (`HOST`) | Points the guest at a third device for about one bounded page per 30 s; separate claim store; the guest verifies the node ID; an RFCOMM connect, not a pairing |
| A mistyped address names a responsive device | Accepted and disclosed (Q-CX12); format check and double entry make it improbable |
| Tracking through the stable address | Never in an advertisement, never to a non-key peer; a contact keeps what it learned even after removal, and no revocation can revoke a BD_ADDR (stated in Settings and the user guide); a Classic session exposes the LAP to any radio in range and the phone stays pageable while Bluetooth is on |
| Pairing before authentication by a search | No search is made by this feature (Tier 2 disabled) |
| Capability bit 5 | One more stable capability bit visible to strangers who connect and to a sniffer of a cleartext BMH2 hello; hidden in BMH3 from sniffers; disclosed in PRIVACY |
| Replay of `HOST`, pre-proof or cutover records | `BMS1` ordering; challenge and message commitment; single-use ticket; `SWITCH` generations (a repeat is ignored, a different boundary for an old generation is dropped and logged) |
| Denial of service on the listener | Phone-wide budgets (2 concurrent upgrade sockets, 6 accepts per 30 s), absolute 2 s and 5 s watchdogs, at most 2,081 pre-authentication bytes, one HMAC and one hash, no signing; the ticket survives unrelated invalid connections; suppression by a radio attacker is an accepted residual because LE continues |
| Oversized records | `BCX1` limit (64 bytes, 128 before BIND) enforced from the header's length before allocation; receiver hold limits in frames, records and bytes |
| Downgrade by stripping the bit | Link hellos are signed; blocking Classic keeps LE |

**Switches (all default off; benchmark launches read only the extras).**

- DEBUG `test_classic_address_exchange=true`: sign bit 5, `PAIR`, `CONTACT`, `ECHO`, `HOST`, the self-address record (needs bond-free Classic).
- DEBUG `test_classic_upgrade=true`: also host/dial/cutover (implies the exchange). A user session gets it from the opt-in row "Switch to Classic Bluetooth with verified contacts (advanced)" (preference `experimental_classic_upgrade`, constant `ConnectivityFeatureDefaults.CLASSIC_UPGRADE = false`), shown and active only while "Connect without pairing" is on, plus the per-contact row "Allow Classic Bluetooth with this contact" (default off) and "Reset Classic pairing". The text says plainly that a contact learns this phone's fixed Bluetooth address and keeps it after removal, and that anyone who learns it can look for the phone later. A stored choice wins outside benchmark launches.
- The typed address field (twice; Advanced, same row) and DEBUG `test_classic_self_address=AA:BB:CC:DD:EE:FF` (source `debug`, never persisted); `test_classic_upgrade_inject=listen_fail|dial_fail|bind_fail|drop_after_switch|echo_conflict|slow_dial|no_ack|stall_out|stall_in|old_carrier_loss|old_carrier_loss_receiver|hold_expire|drop_key|pending_expire|false_echo_true_dial`; `test_classic_upgrade_standby_close_ms=<n>` (Experimental, off = infinite).
- With everything off the link hellos, the SDP records, the hop types and the visibility request are exactly today's.

**Alternatives not taken.**

| Alternative | Why not |
| --- | --- |
| Address in the LE advertisement; public-address advertising; LE bonding with cross-transport key derivation | Stable identifier; privileged API; pairing dialogs and a stored bond |
| Inquiry plus UUID or name identification of the host (Tier 2) | Discovery can page candidates before authentication; names and copied UUIDs are spoofable; disabled |
| A "both verified" hello flag or a `CONTACT` that states verification | Leaks one-sided verification; replaced by the key |
| Commit-reveal or private set intersection per session | Heavier than a once-established shared key for the same property |
| Reuse presence leases as the mutual proof | Leases exist only with rotating IDs on and expire in four weeks |
| A fixed upgrade UUID | A scanner could look for it; the UUID is per attempt |
| A sender-side lookahead boundary and hold (the first cutover draft) | An idle sender never reaches the boundary and an ack round trip on a congested LE link cancels the cutover; replaced by the two-phase cutover |
| A lossless cutover with acknowledgements, epochs, commit requests and a measured latency | Each guarantee added states and rows and each review found a new interleaving hole; replaced by the lossy, never-fatal design and a model test (round 6) |
| Per-direction fallback | Doubles the state matrix; closing `N` returns both directions to LE |
| Acknowledged and retried controls (`CTRL_ACK`) | No message IDs in the call signals: a duplicate ends the call or a late retry reverts a newer state; replaced by `CLOSE` on every carrier and state-only re-assertion after each carrier event |
| Move media at the endpoint layer | Would re-key and re-bind to relays; the carrier switch keeps `BME1` untouched |

**Review 2026-10-14 (first independent read-only review, NO-GO as drafted at 24f1218; resolved in 0108e88).** CR1 contacts only in both roles; CR2 boundary and generation for every content type, single carrier owner; CR3 `RESUME` on the surviving link, bearer failure distinct from session failure; CR4 pre-proof with fresh challenge and exact first-message hash, atomic single consume, fresh ticket per retry (judged sound by the later reviews; the complete framed BMH2 hello is built once); CR5 votes of stable verified identities, no time promotion; CR6 separate claim store, provenance across all entries sharing an address; CR7 phone-wide budgets, watchdogs, length check before allocation; CR8 upgrade-specific handshake options; CR9 explicit record order; CR10 `READY` ceilings; CR11 zero-prompt claim limited, pairing dialog and bond are measured gates; CR12 bit 5 and type 14 free, mask fix at `BluetoothPrivateCallRelay.kt` lines 693 and 723; CR13 exporter derived before key erasure in canonical order, wiped at teardown, `UP` held for the whole old session (no re-derivation after the keys are gone).

**Review 2 2026-10-14 (second review of 0108e88, NO-GO; resolved in 534bc19).** R2-1 leak of one-sided verification: the pairing key `K_cx` (now refined by R3-4); R2-2 inquiry pages before authentication: Tier 1 inquiry disabled, typed address, no visibility prompt; R2-3 reliable controls: semantic retry under fresh sequences (now narrowed by R3-3); R2-4 explicit state machines with fenced generations and independent watchdogs (now simplified by R3-2, R3-8); R2-5 bounded hold (now replaced by the two-phase cutover, R3-2); R2-6 a lone conflicting vote (now strengthened by R3-1); R2-7 EIR visibility is not `device.uuids`: moot while Tier 2 is disabled, recorded as a reopen condition.

*The Review 3 and Review 4 tables name mechanisms (acknowledgements, commits, epochs, `oldLinkMs`, reports) that Review 5 deleted; they stay as the record of what was found and tried.*


| # | Finding | Resolution |
| --- | --- | --- |
| R3-1 (F1, C8) | Address poisoning: one contact votes its own device and the host offers it to other contacts; a newer vote takes over the selection | A `HOST` carries only a typed or proven address; an echo-only address is offered only to the contact that voted for it; a newer vote never replaces a typed or proven address; an echo-only incumbent changes only by a second distinct voter or a bound connection; candidate order typed, proven, echo-only for voters (refined by R4-1: proofs are scoped to the contact and other contacts get only the typed address) |
| R3-2 (F2, C6) | Cutover: an idle sender never reaches "+400 ms"; an ack round trip on a congested LE link (a 542 ms CoC queue) cancels it; 200 ms hold vs 500 ms receiver hold; undefined timing | Two-phase cutover: `SWITCH(gen)`, keep sending on the old link until `SWITCH_ACK`, then boundary = next sealed sequence at a frame boundary (a keyframe if one starts within 300 ms), `SWITCH_END` in-band; no lookahead, no sender hold; start gated on the old link's queue under 100 ms (`peerDelivery.queueMs()`; replaced by the measured `oldLinkMs`, R4-6); the receiver hold is bounded by wall-clock time (old-link latency plus 300 ms) and by frames, records and bytes (2 video frames, 25 audio records, 5 MiB; a frame is one record of up to 4 MiB); 200 ms is a measured receiver-side target with margin, not a hard gate |
| R3-3 (F3, C3) | Control retries: call signals have no message ID, a duplicate accept or ringing throws, a late camera or microphone retry reverts a newer state | Reliable retry only for `HANG_UP`, lifecycle type 4 and type 8 (`HANG_UP` was removed from the list by R4-8), as the current state only under fresh sequences (a strict per-direction order and the fence make a late retry unable to revert, so no wire revision is needed; a sender-local supersession rule replaces it); microphone and camera are latest-state-wins, re-asserted once; pre-`ACTIVE` signals never retried and no cutover while one is pending; API note: `EndToEndContentOpener.open` returns no sequence today |
